Lingvamyxa Language Specification

Status

Unified draft. This document contains the structural core, the L2/L3 source
profiles, registry and message operators, runtime services and authentication.

The normative Structure and translator architecture is Revision 2. Its detailed
implementation assignment is `struct_refactoring_version_2.txt`. Revision 2
replaces the descriptor-prefix/per-name-C-layout and runtime-namespace model of
`struct_refactoring.txt`; the latter is retained only as historical evidence
about the hosted translator and must not be used as an architectural base.

The parser and its P0 source tree remain valid. The semantic/lowering core of
the translator is to be rebuilt around one generic runtime Structure. Existing
hosted code may be reused only where its behaviour is independently compatible
with this specification (for example source parsing, diagnostics, escaping and
some output utilities). Passing the old translator's tests is not evidence that
its descriptor, namespace, closure or generated-typedef architecture remains
normative.

The universal runtime header is:

    typedef struct Lmx Lmx;

    struct Lmx {
        Lmx *node;
        size_t len;
        void *data;
    };

There is no additional mandatory descriptor reference before this header, no
per-source-name C structure layout, and no runtime lexical namespace. `node`
is the reverse containment link to the parent in the source/runtime Structure
tree. Non-`node` references may form an arbitrary graph. `data` is interpreted
by the registered address range in which it falls; a primitive leaf may, for
example, point at an element of the global `all_chars` pool. Names are not
stored in `Lmx`. The sole mandatory name aid is the external reverse index

    Lmx * -> ShortNameId

which supports path reconstruction and runtime textual lookup from an explicit
root. It is an index, not a namespace and not part of value semantics.

Every source method is emitted as a program-wide reachable C entry point with a
unique, normally source-path-derived, name. Its callable occurrence is an
ordinary node in the Structure graph whose `data` identifies that entry point.
The baseline invocation supplies exactly `callable->node`, the `Lmx *` of the
Structure containing the selected callable occurrence. Source
arguments, results, body fields and so-called local variables remain Structure
nodes and are reached through Structure links; they are not lowered directly to
semantic C locals. C locals may be used only as backend temporaries and later
proven optimizations.

`merge` is a runtime tree-copy operation. It allocates new Lmx occurrences,
copies the selected containment tree, rewrites `node` links in the copy and
retains links to already compiled global method entry points. It is neither a
descriptor composition pass nor a request to invoke the translator or C
compiler at runtime. A merge result is an ordinary Structure and may itself be
merged.

Message memory and concurrency are part of the common L3 semantics. Every
Message owns exactly one logical arena; all dynamic mutable ordinary Lmx nodes
and payloads of that Message are allocated in it. When admitted for execution,
that Message is an L3 Thread. One L3 Thread executes at most one turn at a time,
and its admitted inbox and staged outbox preserve FIFO order. Different
Messages, with different arenas, may execute concurrently. Host worker count,
OS-thread mapping and the physical block/collector implementation remain
lowering choices; one-arena ownership, FIFO order and same-Message turn
serialization do not. A successful turn publishes its staged FIFO outbox, or a
failed turn rolls it back, before the local collection pass at the common
end-turn boundary; termination ultimately releases the Message arena after
required transfer and cleanup.

Revision-2 rules in sections 0, 2, 3, 7, 13, 19.17–19.25, 19.28–19.29,
20.2.1 and 21 take precedence over any remaining implementation-status prose.
Where an old hosted passage uses `namespace`, `descriptor`, `closure`,
`typedef` or `static`, it describes historical implementation machinery unless
the passage explicitly states that the construct is ordinary user data or an
explicit raw-L1/C operation.

0. Core Formula

Lingvamyxa is based on the following model:

    L0 native artifacts run.
    L1 is an external bootstrap, implementation and lowering stratum.
    L2 and L3 are the only Lingvamyxa source levels.
    L2 is the complete language surface, including low-level machine operations.
    L3 is the high-level subset of L2 without low-level machine operations.
    Source syntax builds Structures.
    Source traversal also builds Mix overlay intervals in parallel with
    Structures and arrays.
    Receivers and subroutines consume Structures.
    The core parser has one structural syntax.
    Operator tokens that cannot be source identifiers are parsed as operator fields without `:`.
    Whitespace-bounded operator forms are accepted directly by the core structural parser.
    Compact non-quoted spellings with invalid bare-name characters are split into ordinary fields and operator-token fields by the same structural tokenization pipeline.
    Levels restrict the active receiver/operator set.

The language does not assign final meaning directly from surface syntax. Surface syntax builds generic Structures. A receiver or subroutine consumes a Structure and assigns typed semantics to it.

User describes the world with Structures. Branches contain ordered Structure
occurrences; leaves denote primitive storage, code, references, array storage
or emptiness according to the address range of `data`. Tables, registries,
schemas, messages, descriptor-like records and executable forms are composed
from the same Structures; none introduces a second object/type universe.

L0 and L1 remain necessary implementation strata, but neither is a Lingvamyxa
source level. Tables, registries, messages, services and Message Threads are
ordinary families of Structures, arrays, receivers and runtime facilities
inside the L2/L3 language; they do not introduce further language levels, a
hidden semantic store or a runtime namespace.

Canonical semantic pipeline:

    source text
        -> core structural parse
        -> source/P0 tree
        -> Lmx construction recipes and globally named C method bodies
        -> runtime Structure graph construction and explicit link traversal
        -> receiver/operator/subroutine consumption through Lmx occurrences
        -> interpretation or lowering

The translator may use ordinary compile-time symbol tables while compiling a
source file. Such tables are compiler work data only. They must lower a source
name to Structure traversal, a direct resolved link, a method-pool reference or
an explicit runtime path lookup. They must not be reified as a semantic runtime
`Name -> Binding` environment.

A Structure does not execute merely because it exists. Execution, translation,
explicit declaration, validation or query handling starts only when a visible
receiver/subroutine consumes it under a defined mode.

Argument position does not change that rule. A Structure supplied as an argument
remains a Structure for the parser. During translation or interpretation, a
compact Frame/Structure is treated as a call only when its head resolves to a
callable Structure occurrence under the selected source profile. A bounded,
anonymous or vertical Structure is not executed merely because it appears among
arguments.

0.0. Language Scope

Lingvamyxa is one structural language with several consumption modes.

The same source mechanism builds Structures. Different receivers and profiles
consume those Structures as data, executable code, explicit description data,
table rows, messages, service routes, build instructions or transport payloads.

The Revision-2 source/runtime value ontology is deliberately uniform:

    Value = Lmx reference

Primitive values are leaves of this graph, not a separate object hierarchy.
`data` points into a primitive address domain such as `all_chars`, and the
address-domain classifier supplies the storage interpretation. Callable values
are Lmx occurrences whose `data` identifies a globally linked C method. Empty
values are Lmx leaves with the runtime-defined empty representation. Array
syntax and rectangular array operations remain part of the language; the exact
Revision-2 integration of ArrayReference storage with this universal Lmx header
is an explicitly unresolved ABI item, not permission to restore per-name C
layouts or descriptor prefixes.

Every Structure is materialized in its owning Message arena and has stable
identity while reachable. Module/top-level and method-body Structures differ by
their roots and lifetime, not by a source-semantic `static` class. A field,
argument, return value, method occurrence and source local are all represented
as Structures or links between Structures.

For ordinary L3/Message execution, that selected storage is the owning
Message's single logical arena. The arena may contain multiple backing blocks
and registered physical address domains. Mutable Structures, non-interned
primitive cells, Arrays and merge results belong to it; immutable global pools
such as `all_chars` and compiled method records/code may be shared. The arena
belongs to the Message and follows it across turns or host-worker migration.

There is no semantic runtime lexical namespace. In particular there is no

    Name -> ordered active Binding occurrences

map copied, merged, pushed or popped at method entry/call/return. A method gets
the containing Structure node and follows real Structure links. A call through
an already resolved reference likewise uses that reference; it does not install
the callee's names into a caller environment. Lexical parent access, when
required by source, is explicit traversal through `node`.

Names exist in source text, in parser/compiler work state and in exactly one
mandatory runtime auxiliary index:

    Lmx * -> ShortNameId

The index contains no forward binding stack and no cached full path. Given a
known `Lmx *`, a diagnostic/full current path is reconstructed by repeatedly
reading the short-name id and following `node`. Given an arbitrary path string
at runtime, lookup requires an explicit root, splits the path into short names,
enumerates that root's ordered children, compares their ids through the reverse
index and repeats from the selected child. Since the tree may mutate, lookup is
performed against the current tree and may produce a different result later.

The reverse index must be updated when occurrences are created, copied, moved
or removed. A missing or inconsistent required entry is a runtime-integrity
error for textual lookup and path diagnostics. It must not change
direct-reference calls, merge, primitive interpretation or any operation that
already possesses the required Lmx pointer.

A registry is ordinary program data: a collection of Structures and arrays
organized for lookup. A table is likewise an ordinary data organization made
from Structures and arrays. Tables may explicitly contain descriptors,
relations, schemas or implementation choices, and a receiver may explicitly
query them. They are not a runtime namespace, the mandatory representation of
types, or an implicit side effect of value construction. A table named `class`
or a relation named `layout.backend` has meaning only to the explicit profile or
consumer that reads it.

Language-core compatibility remains `implements(varA varB Consumer)` where it
is explicitly requested, but it compares Structure paths used by `Consumer`;
it does not require per-value descriptors or a runtime namespace. Registries,
rule engines, unification and backtracking remain expressible as ordinary
Structure/Array subsystems and are consulted only by explicit operations.

In historical implementation-status passages, the phrases "registered in the
namespace", "namespace descriptor" and "namespace binding" refer to the old
translator's compilation tables. They do not define Revision-2 runtime state.
New implementation work must express the corresponding behaviour as source
tree construction, direct graph links, explicit root-relative lookup or global
method-pool linkage.

0.0.1. First, Lingvamyxa is a general structural data notation.

It can be used for human-readable records, trees, nested configurations,
schemas, registries, message payloads and table-like data in the same problem
domain as XML, JSON, YAML and CSV.

Unlike those formats, Lingvamyxa does not have a separate data syntax and
program syntax. Both are source forms that build Structures.

Example of mixed tree and table-like data:

dataset: harvest
    meta:
        title: "Regional harvest report"
        season: 2026
        source:
            organization: `Field Bureau`
            system: `manual survey`
        ---
        location:
            country: Uruguay
            region: `north field group`
        ---
        note:
            "This part is XML-like tree data: nested named Structures."
    ---

    table:
        columns: (serial "id") region           crop    (real "tons") (decimal "moisture")
        rows:
                 1             "north field"    wheat   12.50         0.14
                 2             "south field"    corn    8.75          0.19
                 3             "east ridge"     barley  10.00         0.16
                 4             "river lowland"  rice    21.30         0.22
        ---
end: harvest

The `meta` section is tree-shaped data, similar in role to an XML/YAML document.

The `columns:` line is a compact schema row. Each schema field is an explicit
compact Frame such as `int(id)`, `Text(region)`, `real(tons)` or
`decimal(moisture)`. Horizontal whitespace separates those schema fields.

The `rows:` section is true table-like data. A table-consuming profile reads the
row body as an ordinary flat stream of fields and groups it by the number of
schema fields declared by `columns:`. Horizontal whitespace separates row
fields. Extra horizontal spacing is only visual alignment and does not create
additional fields.

The row values are visually aligned under the corresponding schema fields from
`columns:`. This alignment is presentation only; the structural meaning still
comes from ordinary whitespace-separated row fields and the column count. A
profile must not infer missing cells from the physical source line. If a table
needs an empty or absent cell in a dense row, write an explicit value such as
`None`.

Exact quoted identifiers such as `north field` and `river lowland` keep
multi-word field values atomic.

The `dataset`, `meta`, `table`, `columns` and `rows` receivers are not special
parser syntax. They consume ordinary Structures. A profile may lower this data
to CSV, SQL rows, binary records, in-memory arrays, Lingvamyxa Structure
serialization, JSON for legacy interoperability, or another backend
representation.

0.0.2. Secondly, Lingvamyxa has Mix overlay markup and document semantics.

Mix is the Lingvamyxa overlay markup model. It is not an XML-like tree
markup system, not an HTML preprocessor and not a separate mini-language.
Mix is a parallel active mark and interval overlay built during the same
source traversal that builds ordinary Structures and arrays. Its target
runtime is a Message-based graph within the common Message ontology. The
current Java overlay/page/cursor implementation is transitional and requires
the Message-style refactoring described in section 19.29.2.1; its present
storage mechanics do not redefine the ontology.

The compact formula is:

    source traversal
        -> Structures and arrays
        -> Mix active mark / interval overlay
        -> receiver/profile consumption

The source text may still look tree-shaped, because that is convenient for
human reading and for structural parsing. The Mix view is different: it
projects source positions, Structure nodes, array rows, fields and explicit
marks into overlapping active sets. Therefore a Lingvamyxa source file can have
Mix overlay information even when it contains no explicit curly-brace marks.
Ordinary structural forms are also markable source-interval entities. Curly
braces are only the explicit document-facing spelling for adding extra active
marks to the same overlay model.

Curly braces introduce Mix bounded forms `{...}`. They are bounded structural
forms like parentheses and index brackets, but their result is a Mix payload
Structure. When the P0 parser sees `{` outside ordinary string/comment/raw
payload, it enters Mix payload parsing. The payload between `{` and its
matching `}` is parsed by the same structural parser in its own local level-0
context, so quoted strings, python-like raw strings, exact identifiers, block
strings and comments keep their ordinary payload rules. A `}` inside such a
payload is payload text, not the closing delimiter of the surrounding Mix
bounded form.

The resulting Mix bounded form payload Structure is stored immediately as a
parser node with the source position/span of the mark and a Mix flag. Physical
line breaks inside the mark do not create source-level/layout events for the
surrounding Structure. The opener column of `{` does not become the local
indentation base for the Mix payload.

The Mix bounded form is anchored to a surrounding source item or source level.
This anchoring is layout syntax, not mark semantics:

    item: {color: red} value
    item: value {color: red}

Inline and tail Mix forms belong to the current field sequence of the current
source item.

    {color: red}    item: value

A leading Mix prefix before the first ordinary item on a physical line is
stored before that item and uses the item's source level. In relaxed notation
the physical width of the Mix prefix may occupy part of the indentation space;
in canonical dotted notation the following dots still compute the source level
normally.

    {color: red}
        item: value

A column-0 physical line whose content is only one or more Mix bounded forms
is a margin Mix run. It is queued and anchored to the next ordinary LMX source
item after the run. A sequence of such margin Mix lines keeps its order and
uses that next item's source level. If EOF is reached first, the run is attached
to the current/document-end context.

A standalone Mix line that is not a column-0 margin run follows the ordinary
source-level/indentation rules of the line on which it is written.

Ordinary structural consumers and translators may ignore Mix-flagged nodes,
while document/Mix consumers project them into the active mark/overlay view. A
mark frame such as:

    {color: red}

opens one active Mix mark entry. In this example the mark head is `color` and
the active close name/value is `red`. The entry remains active until it is
closed or until the containing source/document unit ends according to the
selected profile. Unclosed entries are not silently forgotten; they remain
tracked as still-open entries and may become diagnostics, document-wide
intervals or profile-specific state.

Explicit close forms operate over the active stack of marks:

    {color: red}
    {color: green}
    text
    {end: red}
    {end: green}

`{end}` closes the last still-active explicit mark. `{end: name}` closes the
most recent still-active explicit mark whose close name/value is `name`.
Closing `red` in the example above closes the `{color: red}` entry; the
`{color: green}` entry remains open until it is closed separately.

When several active entries share the same close name/value, the most recent
matching entry wins, regardless of the mark head:

    {style: red}
    {color: red}
    text
    {end: red}

Here `{end: red}` closes `{color: red}`, not `{style: red}`. The close operand is
the active close value, not an XML-style repeated element name.

This is intentionally different from XML and HTML nesting. Closing names the
active value that is no longer wanted; it is not a repeated tag name and it
does not force all open marks into a single strict element tree. A later or
inner mark does not destroy the earlier one. For example, while the parser is
inside both `{color: red}` and `{color: green}`, the current `color` value for a
simple renderer may be `green`, but the underlying active chain still contains
`red` below it. A consumer may inspect the whole chain.

The active attribute view is therefore not just a flat map from name to one
value. It is at least a key-to-stack, key-to-list or key-to-active-set view over
the ordered active Mix entries. A rendering profile may project this view to a
simple last-open-wins table. A semantic, editor, diagnostic or registry profile
may use the complete active chain and all intersecting active sets. P0 does not
perform conflict resolution for Mix marks.

Example of the same tree and table-like data with explicit Mix overlay marks:

{style: system}
dataset: harvest
    meta:
{style: title} {color: blue}
        title: "Regional harvest report"
{style: date}
        season: 2026
{end: title}
        month: April
{end: date}
        source:
            organization: `Field Bureau`
            system: `manual survey`
        ---
        location: {color: green}
            country: Uruguay
            region: `north field group`
        ---
        note:
            "This part is {red} marked {end} document data over ordinary Structures."

    --- {end: blue}

    table:
        columns: (serial "id") region           crop    (real "tons") (decimal "moisture")
{end: green}
        rows:
{gray}           1             "north field"    wheat   12.50         0.14
{cyan}           2             "south field"    corn    8.75          0.19
{gray}           3             "east ridge"     barley  10.00         0.16
{cyan}           4             "river lowland"  rice    21.30         0.22
        ---
end: harvest
{end: system}

From one compact unified syntax, the parser can simultaneously create
hierarchical Structures, arrays and the Mix overlay graph. The Mix graph is a
layer of overlapping, partially hierarchical active sets laid over source
positions and structural data. The language algorithm can therefore know the
active values of all relevant elements at every position and can update them
directly.

Mix scanning is universal. Mix interpretation is profile-dependent.

A source/document consumer may know the ordinary Structure context, the array
or row context, the source position context, the active explicit marks and the
implicit structural intervals around the current position. Marks found inside a
string or raw text payload are recorded under that payload context, with their
own isolated marker index/scope, instead of being mixed into the
surrounding source-level active mark table. Additional interpretation is needed
only when a consumer chooses to activate, render or consume such internal
payload marks.

For example, a surrounding mark such as `{style: system}` may select a profile
in which printed strings are treated as document text and their internal Mix
marks are interpreted. A SQL string profile may highlight or bind internal
placeholders. A plain string profile may ignore them completely. The raw string
value is not changed merely because Mix marks are visible to the parser or
document consumer.

The Mix layer maintains active marks while traversing the source. It can
therefore expose all data at a position, including Structure context,
structural-array context, current top attributes and the full active mark
chains, with fast access and without requiring large contiguous memory blocks.

Mix overlay data is produced below or alongside the level receivers; it is not
message execution by itself. Explicit Registry/query and message receivers may
consume accepted Mix-derived intervals, annotations and structural evidence.

0.0.3. Thirdly, Lingvamyxa is a high-level programming language.

The executable layer supplies ordinary algorithmic computation, function and
subroutine definitions, control receivers, expressions and calls.

The model layer describes structural models, contracts, constraints, protocols
and explicit description Structures connected by graph links.

The message and explicit data/query layer describes messages,
validation and explicit policy combination, expression execution, logical message generation,
configuration, table/rule/query receivers and high-level dispatch.

Low-level programming is not a foreign escape hatch. The low-level and
implementation-facing layers provide controlled access to storage, addresses,
external functions, unsafe operations and implementation-specific lowering when
the selected profile admits them.

The exact level names and their boundaries are defined in the next section.

At service level, Lingvamyxa is a structural service and message system.

Compiler state, runtime state and service data use the same primitive,
Structure and Array ontology without becoming one mandatory registry. Explicit
descriptors, models, operation tables, routes, messages, handlers, build
targets, validation rules and transport profiles are ordinary Structures and
arrays accepted by receivers. A service may organize them in a registry, but
only an explicit operation gives that registry an effect.

For this reason, a Lingvamyxa implementation is not only a compiler in the
narrow sense. A complete implementation may also act as a runtime, build system,
registry host and service framework for small and large services.

The built-in HTTP Lingvamyxa REST profile provides a standard HTTP transport
for Lingvamyxa messages between applications and external HTTP clients. Every
hosted application may expose a REST/LMX endpoint. A message address combines
that application endpoint with a REST route; an omitted endpoint means the
current application. HTTP is the transport envelope. Routes are explicit
descriptors that map to Message Thread inboxes; a profile may store them in a
table. Lingvamyxa messages and serialized Lingvamyxa Structures remain the
semantic payload.

JSON may be supported as a legacy or interoperability adapter, but JSON is not
the default payload model of the HTTP Lingvamyxa REST profile and does not
define Lingvamyxa message semantics.

0.1. L2/L3 Table and Message Smoke Example

The following example defines no new syntax. It illustrates the core formula in
two source profiles: the L2 module uses explicit tables, target and low-level
build receivers; the high-level L3 module uses ordinary Structures and tables
together with message descriptors, executable definitions and control forms.

Short version of the "hello.lm3" file in the default executable script profile:

print: "Hello World"

In this profile, the file root is consumed as an L3 top-level executable body.
The longer multi-module version below spells out the registry, build target and
explicit executable entry point.

0.1.1. Source File Level Profiles

The source file extension may select the outer level profile. This is not
inferred from the first receiver in the file.

In the default profile:

    `.lm2` means that the file root is consumed as an implicit L2 body.
    `.lm3` means that the file root is consumed as an implicit L3 body.
    `.lm4` and `.lm5` are legacy spellings, not source profiles, and are rejected.

Explicit wrappers remain available only for levels admitted by the active
source profile. Only `L2:` and `L3:` are level receivers. The legacy `L4:` and
`L5:` names are not registered as level receivers and are rejected wherever a
level receiver is expected, whether root or nested. A translator source also
has no separate root `registry:` level alias. Table/query source is written
directly on an L2 or L3 module surface with `table:` and `join:`; dependencies
requiring hoisted declarations are selected with `predef:`. A `.lm2` file does
not need an outer `L2:` wrapper, and a `.lm3` file does not need an outer `L3:`
wrapper.

The implicit root profile is deliberately attached to the source extension,
not inferred from an arbitrary receiver name. A root-level `table:` in an
`.lm2` module is direct L2 Table construction; the same form in `.lm3` is
direct L3 Table construction. Renaming either module to
`.lm4` does not select another implicit profile; the source is rejected. A
root-level `table:` in a plain `.lmx` file is only ordinary Structure data
unless it appears inside an explicit admitted level.

L2 and L3 admit an explicit top-level dependency receiver for modules whose
declarations must be available to compiler resolution before later declarations:

    predef:
        "model.lm2"
        "receivers.lm2"
    end: predef

Before the module prelude is planned, direct `table:` and `join:` Frames whose
body begins with `source` may construct compiler-owned Table values in source
order. For an implicit `.lm2` or `.lm3` module this scan covers the file root;
for a mixed or explicitly wrapped source it covers the body of each explicit
`L2:` or `L3:` Frame. This state belongs only to translation and build-profile
resolution. It is not a runtime Namespace, is not attached to an Lmx node and
does not become part of source value semantics. An unmarked Table or join is an
ordinary runtime construction site and is left to runtime lowering/evaluation;
its column names and description fields never opt it into the early scan. The
early scan does not descend into conditional, guard, function or other receiver
bodies. The ordinary pass still validates each Frame and creates any explicitly
requested compiler resolution entry, but it does not reconstruct a source site
that the early pass already completed. No row, column or cell becomes a runtime
name binding during this prepass.

The current local-source early prepass scans source-marked Frames only on
implicit or explicit L2 surfaces. Ordinary declaration can still materialize a
source-marked `table:`/`join:` on an explicit L3 surface. Hoisted implicit-`.lm3`
handling and `.lm3` `predef` targets remain implementation work; this is not a
semantic restriction of L3.

A `table:` may put the bare atom `source` first in its direct body:

    table:
        source
        name: `model.generic-key`
        columns: (@: char key) (@: char value)
        rows:
                 sample  ok
    end: `model.generic-key`

This requests an ordinary Table available to translation and explicit query
operations without requiring a public ANSI C table symbol. It does not select a
RegistryView or introduce names from cells. The marker must be the first direct
table-body field and appear exactly once; a misplaced or duplicate marker is an
error. It is a projection/lifetime option for this Table, not another semantic
document kind.

An early local source-marked `join:` is constructed only when every source
Table is already available from compiler bootstrap state, a selected `predef`,
or preceding source declarations. If an ordinary `import:` will provide a
missing source later, the join remains deferred to the ordinary pass. An
unmarked join never enters this readiness check.

`predef` has the path-list shape of `import`, but is a hoisted import. Before
ordinary top-level lowering and before the module prelude is planned, its
selected modules expose their declarations to compiler resolution in source
order. Table contents are not imported as runtime names. The translator must
recursively discover executable `fn`/`sub` bodies at every Structure depth and
emit their real, globally unique one-input C methods. It must not turn nested
executables into descriptor-only fields or omit their bodies. A named Structure
declaration supplies a runtime builder/evaluation site; `predef` does not
materialize a static Structure instance or generate a per-source-name C layout.
The selected modules pass through their ordinary level-specific declaration and
emission phases exactly once. Thus `predef` does not mean "registry-only" or
"do not translate".

The exact meaning and linking syntax of a bodyless `fn:` remain unresolved as
specified in 7.2.1. `predef` must not invent a zero-returning implementation, an
attached callable descriptor, a generated function-pointer type or a runtime
Namespace entry for such a form. An explicitly linked bodyless callable uses an
ordinary callable Lmx occurrence and the same one-containing-Structure method
ABI as a callable with a source body.

At module-level L2, the top-level atom `prototype` may consume an explicitly
linked backend callable declaration and emit the required ANSI C prototype
under the selected L2/L1 profile. It does not scan arbitrary Tables and cannot
derive a prototype from an attached descriptor because Revision 2 has no such
runtime object.

An ordinary `import:` does not acquire this early behavior by containing tables,
and a module is never heuristically classified by its contents. Semantically,
the `predef` receiver is top-level-only, preserves normal relative-path
resolution and import deduplication, and is available on L2 and L3 module
surfaces. The selected module retains its own source profile; using `predef`
does not make an L2-only machine operation available in L3.

The current bootstrap accepts `.lm2` targets only. `predef` is admitted on an
L3 surface; `.lm3` targets remain implementation work under the high-level-subset
model.

There are no implicit `.lm4` or `.lm5` profiles. Both extensions and both
legacy level wrappers are rejected. Table/Registry values may be constructed in
`.lm2` or `.lm3` modules and their explicit declarations may be selected with
`predef:` when hoisted dependency loading is required.

The bootstrap parser may use explicit configuration Tables. Its automatic
candidates and an explicit `LM_P0_REGISTRY` override currently accept `.lm2`
modules only. This is an implementation loader restriction, not ambient
language Registry semantics.

The translator's earlier source-only bootstrap path is stricter: every root
`table:` or `join:` Frame selected by that path must carry the first-body
`source` marker. An unmarked Frame is diagnosed rather than silently ignored
or inferred from a `class` column.

0.1.2. Long Multi-Module Version

Configuration data in `hello_model.lm2` uses direct L2 Tables. The `source`
marker requests translation-time/module Table materialization without a public
generated C table symbol:

    table:
        source
        name: HelloConfig
        columns: class     (field_type class)  (owner class)  (default char)
        rows:
                 greeting  Text                owned          "Hello World"
                 maxI      int                 value          3
                 maxJ      int                 value          3
    end: HelloConfig

    table:
        source
        name: HelloConfig_rules
        columns: class           (condition char)
        rows:
                 positiveBounds  "maxI > 0 && maxJ > 0"
    end: HelloConfig_rules

    table:
        source
        name: entryPoint
        columns: class  (implementation Symbol)
        rows:
                 app    helloMain
    end: entryPoint

    table:
        source
        name: BUILD
        columns: class          (l2 binding)                 (win char)    (unix char)
        rows:
                 exe_suffix     lm_build_exe_suffix          ".exe"        ""
                 path_sep       lm_build_path_sep            '\\'          '/'
                 getcwd         lm_build_platform_getcwd     _getcwd       getcwd
                 chdir          lm_build_platform_chdir      _chdir        chdir
                 include        None                         "<direct.h>"  "<unistd.h>"
    end: BUILD

Message and behavior description Structures in `hello.lm3` are ordinary L3
receiver forms, not a separate level:

    predef:
        "hello_model.lm2"
    end: predef

    behavior: HelloConfig
        message: run helloMain
    ---

    fact: model HelloConfig
    fact: message HelloConfig run helloMain
    fact: entryPoint app helloMain

The toolchain-facing target and build instructions remain low-level receivers
in `hello_build.lm2`:

    target:
        app
        kind: executable
        sources: ("src/hello.lm3")
        entry: helloMain
        output: "build/bin/hello_world.lm0.exe"
    ---

    build: app

Executable definitions in `hello.lm3` continue on the high-level L3 surface:

    fn: words () iterator(Text)
        yield: "Hello"
        yield: "World"
    end: words

    fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
        if: n <= 1
            return: 1
    return: n * factorial(n - 1)

    sub: helloMain
        (): cfg
            greeting: "Hello World"
            maxI: 3
            maxJ: 3
        ---

        System\out\println: cfg\greeting

        each: word in words
            System\out\println: word
        end: each

        outerLoop:
            for: int(i, 1) (i <= cfg\maxI) i++
                for: int(j, 1) (j <= cfg\maxJ) j++
                    if: i = 2 && j = 2
                        System\out\println: "--- continue outerLoop ---"
                        continue: outerLoop
                    ---

                    System\out\println: ("i: " + factorial(i) + ", j: " + factorial(j))
        end: outerLoop

        System\out\println: "Done."
    end: helloMain

Notes:

    `end: words` explicitly closes the `fn: words () iterator(Text)` definition.
    The `return:` at the source level of `fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)` is
    terminal return for that function definition. It appends the runtime return
    and closes the executable definition. The `sub: helloMain` definition is
    closed explicitly by `end: helloMain`. The `.lm3` file root supplies the
    implicit L3 frame.

    `fn: words () iterator(Text)` gives the `fn` receiver the fields
    `words`, `()`, `iterator(Text)` and the following `yield:` fields. The
    `fn` receiver consumes them as name, empty formal argument Structure,
    return-description Structure and executable body.

    `(): cfg` constructs an anonymous Lmx Structure and publishes it as the
    child occurrence `cfg` at that runtime construction site. The ordinary
    Table named `HelloConfig` above is available to compiler/profile resolution
    through the explicit `predef` import and may be consumed as
    configuration/schema data, but its name does not make `HelloConfig:` a
    constructor or attach metadata to `cfg`. The Table's rows do not become
    runtime names.

    `yield: "Hello"` gives the `yield` receiver one produced value. The
    receiver consumes the current `fn` return-description Structure, verifies
    that `iterator(Text)` declares a yield protocol, and checks the produced
    value against that protocol. Neither `iterator` nor `Text` is a parser
    keyword; both are source names resolved by the compiler/profile to ordinary
    Structures or operations. No runtime Namespace lookup is implied.

    `each: word in words` gives the identifier `words` to the `each` receiver as
    an iterator source. The explicit compact call form `words()` is also a valid
    Frame when a profile/receiver wants the iterator state to be created before
    `each` consumes it.

    The `match`, default-pattern, failure-handler and full build-target semantics
    are not implied by this example. They remain receiver/profile semantics
    described in their own sections.

This example should be read with five simple structural rules in mind:

Rule 1. Source text builds Structures.

   A source form does not execute merely because it looks like a statement.
   It first builds a generic Structure. The active receiver later consumes that
   Structure and assigns typed meaning to it.

Rule 2. `head: tail` means that `head` consumes one argument Structure.

   The colon is a receiver-valence marker, not assignment syntax and not a
   keyword-specific grammar. Compiler/profile-resolved forms such as `fn:`, `if:`, `int:` and
   `return:`, and profile-defined forms such as a hypothetical `model:` or
   `fact:`, share the same surface mechanism. Their meanings differ because
   their receivers consume their argument Structures differently. Mentioning a
   head here does not register it.

   Therefore omitting the colon changes the Structure:

       return 1   # two neighboring fields; not "return value 1"
       return: 1  # a return Frame whose receiver consumes value 1

   `return:` has a deliberate double role: inside an executable body it is a
   runtime return receiver, and at an admitted executable trailer position it
   may also close the executable definition. `end:` is different. It is only a
   structural close receiver; it has no ordinary body-level runtime action.

Rule 3. Source levels build the tree.

   The parser tracks source-level changes, not physical source lines as
   containers. Dots in canonical notation, indentation in relaxed notation,
   colon short-form, semicolon, bounded parentheses/brackets, source-level markers and
   physical newline separators may all affect the current level model. Comma is
   an explicit field separator without source-level effect. Child
   items are written at the next deeper source level. An atomic increase of the
   current source level opens a Structure. Returning by one source level
   completes the currently open Structure before the next source item is parsed.
   Cutting more than one level at once is reserved for admitted tail-cutter
   semantics in the next layer.

Rule 4. A source-level cut is the ordinary trailer event.

   A block does not need an explicit close merely because it is named. The
   ordinary trailer event is the source-level cut when a following source item
   or admitted marker makes that cut visible. It is not the physical newline
   character by itself, and it is not the text of the following lower-level
   item. Explicit forms such as `end: name` and
   terminal return forms become tail-cutter trailers only in a valid
   source-level tail-cutter position. They may close source-level/block tails,
   but they do not close unmatched parenthesized/bracketed bounded Structures;
   those must still be closed by the matching `)` or `]`. Short colon forms are
   not bounded by those delimiters, so an
   admitted `end: name` may terminate a short form in a valid source-level
   tail-cutter position.

Rule 5. `end: name`, `---` and terminal return forms are not competing block systems.

   `end: name` is an explicit named tail-cutter, useful in long or deeply
   nested blocks and when the close target should be visible. P0 records it as a
   tail-cutter trailer only when it actually closes an open source-level frame.

   `---` is an explicit source-level marker. It materializes a level point
   without adding an ordinary receiver item. It is mainly useful for anonymous
   same-level sections where an ordinary source item cannot show the necessary
   down/up level transition.

   A terminal return form is an executable-specific trailer when the semantic
   executable layer admits it at the source level of the executable definition.
   For a value-producing `fn`, that form is `return: value`; for a no-value
   executable profile, a bare `return` may be admitted. A `return` written
   inside the body level is an ordinary runtime return.

Style convention used by this example:

    Use the ordinary source-level cut for short local Structures.
    Use `end: name` when the closed target is long, important or not visually
    obvious.
    Use `---` for anonymous same-level sections or visible empty level markers.
    Use terminal `return: value` for compact final function returns when the
    source level makes the target clear.

These notes are only a reading guide for the example. The exact syntactic and
semantic rules for Structures, Frames, source levels, short forms, bounded
Structures, receivers, `end:`, `---` and terminal return forms are specified in the
following sections.

1. Source Levels and Implementation Strata

1.0. General Language Role and External Lexical Standards

Lingvamyxa is a cross-platform structural language with an explicit external L1
bootstrap, implementation and lowering substrate. In the default hosted profile,
L1 may lower through ANSI C/C99, but C is not the language's semantic parent or
self-hosting level. Source text builds Structures. Receivers, callable Lmx
occurrences, Tables and query operations consume those Structures and assign
meaning to them. Source names may be resolved by compiler state or by explicit
runtime graph traversal; there is no semantic runtime Namespace.

A compact description is:

    Lingvamyxa is a structural language: source builds Structures; receivers
    consume them. Functional and logical uses are those Structures under
    different receivers. Self-hosting infrastructure begins at L2/lm2. The
    hosted bootstrap may lower through an external L1 substrate. It is not a
    class-object language.

More precisely:

    * L0 is the native-artifact stratum and L1 is the external procedural,
      portable lowering stratum; neither is a Lingvamyxa source level.
    * L2/lm2 is the complete Lingvamyxa surface. It includes high-level
      computation and explicit table/query mechanisms as well as typed storage, addresses,
      physical layouts, unchecked access and other low-level machine operations,
      and is the first self-hosting infrastructure level.
    * L3/lm3 is the high-level subset of L2. It uses the same structural,
      table, graph, module-composition and runtime mechanisms, but
      excludes operations whose source semantics expose low-level machine state.
    * Models, contracts, tables, messages, validators,
      services, rules and Message Threads are receiver/operator/runtime
      families within L2/L3, not additional language levels.

Lingvamyxa implementations may also act as service runtimes. The built-in HTTP
Lingvamyxa REST profile provides a standard event/message transport between
groups of Message Threads and service instances. HTTP is the transport
envelope; Lingvamyxa messages and Structures remain the semantic payload.

There is no class-object model and no attached model descriptor. A model or
description, when a profile needs one, is an ordinary explicitly referenced Lmx
Structure. It may contain fields, defaults, constraints and callable
occurrences, but it is not prepended to or hidden inside another node.
Construction executes the selected runtime builder/evaluation operation and
produces ordinary Lmx occurrences. It does not instantiate a generated C type,
attach an own-descriptor pointer or create a Name-to-value environment.

The functional side is also structural. A function or subroutine definition is a
Structure consumed by the `fn` or `sub` receiver. A call spelling is a Frame, but
it becomes a generated call only after compiler resolution or explicit graph
traversal selects an actual callable Lmx occurrence. Invocation calls the
occurrence's method entry with `callable->node`, its containing Structure, as the
method's sole explicit input. Function return is represented by `return`
receivers and terminal return, not by a separate assignment syntax.

The logical side is represented by ordinary relation tables, rules and query
receivers. Model, union, operator, method, message and validator descriptions
may be stored in such data explicitly. Generic dispatch, message validation,
completeness checks and lowering may query those values when requested, but a
table is not an implicit runtime Namespace, attached type record or object
system.

The source syntax itself does not hard-code object semantics, functional
semantics or logic semantics. It builds generic Structures. The active L2/L3
profile plus compiler-resolved or explicitly traversed receiver/operator
occurrences determine which semantic domain consumes a Structure. The same
surface form may describe a low-level operation, function definition, explicit
model Structure, table row, validator, rule, query, message or build
configuration, depending on receiver consumption.

Lingvamyxa deliberately reuses selected mature external lexical standards instead of redefining every atom. Numeric literals, character constants and text/string literals follow ANSI C / C99 spelling because L1 is ANSI C / C99 and many such literals can be copied directly into generated L1 code. Exact quoted identifiers use SQLite-compatible exact-name semantics because Lingvamyxa Structures may describe external relational schemas and because SQLite provides a simple exact quoted-name model.

This reuse applies only to lexical atoms. It does not adopt C assignment syntax,
C declaration grammar, C control syntax, SQL execution semantics or YAML
mapping semantics. Lingvamyxa owns the structural syntax and semantics:
colon-governed Frames, source-level tree composition, compact calls, short-tail
field separation, terminal return, explicit `fn`/`sub`/label/value occurrences,
Tables, queries and declared head consumption. Compiler symbol tables are
translation machinery only and do not add a runtime lexical-namespace state.

The practical rule is:

    Lingvamyxa owns structure.
    External standards may own selected lexical atoms.

Consequently, an ANSI C numeric literal, character constant or string literal may often be preserved as text during L1 lowering, while its surrounding Lingvamyxa Structure is still parsed, typed and consumed by Lingvamyxa receivers. Likewise, an exact quoted identifier may preserve an external SQLite name without forcing that name into the narrower bare Lingvamyxa identifier grammar.

1.1. L0 Native Artifacts

L0 is the native binary or machine-code artifact stratum actually executed by
the CPU and operating system. It is not a Lingvamyxa source level.

Examples of L0 artifacts:

    app.lm0.exe
    app.lm0
    core.lm0.dll
    core.lm0.so
    core.lm0.dylib
    core.lm0.a
    core.lm0.lib

1.2. L1 External Substrate

L1 is an external bootstrap, implementation and lowering layer. It may host initial parsers, translators, runtime components and receivers. L1 has its own syntax and semantics and is not part of the Lingvamyxa source-semantic core described by this document.

When a profile admits an embedded `L1:` raw text block, `L1` is a foreign
substrate escape receiver. It does not become a third Lingvamyxa source level;
only `L2` and `L3` are level receivers.

The implementation source of Lingvamyxa is Lingvamyxa.

A hosted bootstrap distribution may include generated C snapshots. These
snapshots are portable lowering artifacts, not the source-semantic
implementation of the language.

On a new platform, CMake and a C compiler may build the snapshot compiler.
That compiler then rebuilds the Lingvamyxa sources through the normal LMX
pipeline.

Lingvamyxa is source-self-hosting at the level of its own source code: the
compiler core is written in Lingvamyxa, while the hosted bootstrap profile
lowers it through an external L1 substrate selected by the L2-to-L1 projection
profile. In this implementation, that low-level cross-platform implementation
substrate is ANSI C / C99. This does not make ANSI C the semantic parent of
Lingvamyxa, and it does not turn the generated C projection into the compiler's
source implementation. It means that the L2 bootstrap is not bound to one
particular operating system, CPU family, binary format, ABI or native code
generator, but to a portable implementation substrate - in this case, a
C99-compatible one.

The LMX semantic machine begins at the Lingvamyxa L2 level. LMX lowers its own
L2 machine through a selected external L1 substrate. In the hosted bootstrap
profile described here, that substrate is ANSI C / C99. The external L1/C99
substrate is not the VM of the language and is not its semantic parent.

L2 and L3 Structures may be translated, interpreted, validated or resolved by machinery
written in Lingvamyxa itself. The external L1/C99 substrate only materializes
that machinery on a host platform.

In this sense, Lingvamyxa self-hosting is more hermetic at the level of
substrate abstraction and more portable than a platform-bound native-only
self-hosting chain. The Lingvamyxa sources, explicit description/configuration
Structures and L1 projection rules remain the same across platforms, while platform-specific differences are
delegated below the Lingvamyxa semantic boundary to the selected multiplatform
L1 toolchain: the C99 compiler, C runtime/library, linker and target ABI.

The L2/L3 language is even more hermetic: it is bound to the L2-to-L1
projection contract, not to a particular operating system, ABI, binary format
or CPU family.

This document does not prescribe one L1 target language or final C result
convention. It does prescribe the universal `Lmx { node, len, data }` node and
the generated source-method input convention: every generated method receives
exactly one `Lmx *` identifying its containing Structure. It receives no hidden
out pointer, Namespace, descriptor, closure environment or Message Thread.
The physical arena allocator, collector algorithm and exact return channel
remain runtime ABI work. Their semantic boundary is already fixed: ordinary
dynamic mutable values allocate in the owning Message's one arena, and one
local collection pass runs at end-turn after FIFO publication or rollback.
Source Lmx identity must not depend on incidental C automatic/static storage.

1.3. L2

L2 is the complete Lingvamyxa level. It contains every high-level operation
available in L3 and additionally exposes the low-level machine surface: raw
storage and typed addresses, raw loads, physical layout/storage construction,
unchecked access, direct foreign-substrate calls and low-level synchronization
or atomic operations. Selected low-level thread primitives may be introduced
for shared-memory arrays or other implementation-facing data, but ordinary
interactive, concurrent, UI and service programming may use the Message Thread
model defined by the receiver families in section 19.

Compilation, interpretation and mixed execution are backend/profile choices,
not the distinction between L2 and L3. An L2 implementation that uses the
machine surface must lower those operations against an explicit memory/layout
model.

1.4. L3

L3 is the high-level algorithmic and computational subset of L2. It is not a
sandbox, authority boundary or effect restriction. L3 code may modify ordinary
state, construct Table/Registry values, compose modules, send messages and
use services when the corresponding high-level receivers exist. It may be
interpreted by the core runtime, compiled through the lowering pipeline or use
a mixed execution profile.

L3 excludes only operations whose source semantics expose low-level machine
facilities. This includes raw storage/address operators, address-depth
declarations, raw loads and address-producing forms, physical layout/storage
allocation, direct `c.*` or raw L1 calls, unchecked array access, low-level
synchronization/atomic operations, and parser-internal forms only when they
expose raw storage, addresses, unchecked indexing or target-layout state. A
profile may impose additional validation, authority, purity or effect rules,
but those rules are orthogonal to the L2/L3 level distinction.

Ordinary L3 has no raw pointer/address semantics. Every source value is reached
through an Lmx reference; Structures and array payload domains are not distinct
object/reference classes. Indexing Arrays and array-like containers must check
bounds automatically. Code that needs unchecked access or another machine
operation belongs to L2; a high-level wrapper Structure or handle operation may
hide the raw representation for both L2 and L3. The exact Array ABI remains
unresolved and is not inferred here.

`receiver.l3.exclude` admits `predef` and excludes physical `c.struct` /
`c.union` / `c.enum` / `c.array` plus L2 machine receivers `include`,
`define`, `ifndef-default`, `os`, `ifdef`, `guard`, `extern_c`. Prefix `@`
(raw address-of) is rejected on an L3 surface. Implicit-L3/local-early-prepass
parity for `table` and `join`, and L3 gates for unchecked index on raw
addresses, remain alignment work.

1.5. Receiver Families Are Not Levels

Explicit description Structures, direct `table:`/`join:` value construction,
validation, message dispatch, service handling and explicit query operations
are ordinary shared L2/L3 receiver families.
Only a concrete receiver whose semantics expose a low-level machine operation
is L2-only. Their detailed receiver families are specified in section 19.

The names L4 and L5 do not denote source profiles, implicit file
profiles or semantic strata. The legacy extensions `.lm4`/`.lm5` and root
receivers `L4:`/`L5:` are rejected rather than mapped silently to L2/L3.

Description, Table/Registry, evidence, composition, query, message,
Message Thread, service and authorization families are ordinary shared
L2/L3 receivers specified in section 19. They are available in both L2
and L3 unless a concrete operation exposes the low-level machine surface.

1.6. Level Receivers

`L2` and `L3` are the only level receivers. A level receiver consumes its
nested Structure and sets the active receiver/operator set for that body.

Example:

L3:
. operator:
. . if
. . callableFrom: L2 L3
. . implementation: lmx_if
end: L3

The same high-level operator contract/Structure may be consumed from L2 or L3. An
operator whose contract exposes raw addresses, raw loads, physical storage,
unchecked access or other section 20 machine facilities is L2-only. Ordinary
Table/Registry value construction and explicit query are not such facilities.

1.7. Startup and Entry Targets

An entry point is not the first event in the physical life of a program. A
binary loader, target runtime, C runtime, Lingvamyxa runtime, restored state,
registry data, build profile or service host may already exist before source
level execution begins. The entry point is only the selected target to which
control is handed after that environment is ready.

The two Lingvamyxa levels use different default startup conventions:

    L2
        L2 is compiled and close to L1 / ANSI C. For an executable artifact,
        the default L2 entry target is `main`, because that convention maps
        directly to C99 and to ordinary native executable startup.

        Example:

        L2:
        . fn: main () int
        . . c.puts: "Hello"
        . . return: 0
        . end: main
        end: L2

        lowers conceptually to a C `main` function. This is a bootstrap and
        lowering convention, not a philosophical center of the language. A
        raw `L1:` block may also contain an ordinary C `main`; in that case
        the raw C block supplies the executable entry target.

    L3
        L3 is the ordinary algorithmic/script level. In the default script
        profile, the root body of an L3 source item may be consumed from the
        beginning of the file, Python-style. A build or runtime profile may
        also select a visible L3 function or subroutine as the executable
        target, but `main` is not a source-semantic requirement for L3.

        Message and service profiles are L3 entry conventions, not another
        level. A runtime may choose a visible `start` receiver, an `onStart`
        handler, a service Structure/target or an explicit message send instead of
        consuming the file root linearly.

        Example conceptual target in an `.lm3` module:

        HelloWorld:
        . start:
        . . System\out\println: "Hello"

        The runtime profile may select `HelloWorld\start`.

The common rule is:

    startup = prepared runtime graph/profile + selected visible target

The selected target may be the conventional C-compatible `main` at L2, the file
root in an L3 script profile, or an explicit L3 message/callable/service target.


2. Value Ontology

The complete Revision-2 source-semantic value ontology is:

    Value = Lmx reference

There is one universal node header and no second semantic object model:

    struct Lmx {
        Lmx *node;
        size_t len;
        void *data;
    };

`node` is the parent occurrence in the containment tree. The root of an
independent tree has the runtime-selected root convention (normally `NULL`);
a tree inserted into another Structure has that Structure as its parent.
Following only `node` always walks toward the containment root and must not
form a cycle. References encoded through `data` or through payload elements may
point elsewhere, including sideways or upward, and therefore make the complete
set of Structures a graph.

`len` and `data` form the payload. `data` is intentionally untyped at the C
surface. The runtime classifies it by address-domain membership, not by a
descriptor field or a generated C type. Examples of separately registered
domains include:

    child/branch storage
    primitive pools (`all_chars`, integers, other scalar cells)
    callable entry-point storage or the implementation-selected code range
    Structure-reference storage
    rectangular array storage
    empty/sentinel storage
    explicitly registered foreign or low-level storage

The exact unit of `len` for every address domain, the final representation of
empty typed payloads and the complete Array ABI are Revision-2 decisions still
to be specified. The hosted `ArrayReference` representation is migration input,
not a selected part of that ABI. Until the open items are resolved, an
implementation must expose a domain operation that can enumerate ordered child
occurrences of a branch; it must not infer a source-specific C layout.

A primitive is an Lmx leaf. For example, the source field

    char: '!'

may have `data == &all_chars['!']`. The address lies in the character domain,
so no type tag, name or descriptor pointer is needed in the node. An assignment
changes the leaf's `data` reference; it does not mutate the shared immutable
character cell. Mutable scalar pools, foreign addresses and allocation domains
must be explicitly registered so that address classification is deterministic.
An arbitrary escaped stack address or unregistered foreign pointer cannot be
classified and is not a conforming high-level value.

A branch is an Lmx occurrence whose classified payload exposes ordered child
occurrences. Every directly contained child has `child->node == branch`.
Duplicate short names are represented by distinct ordered children rather than
by overwriting one slot. The unqualified path segment `name` is exactly the
same occurrence as `[0]name`; later occurrences remain addressable by their
explicit occurrence index. This rule is independent of how merge happened to
append children.

Names are not stored in the Lmx header or branch payload. The mandatory reverse
index is:

    Lmx * -> ShortNameId

It is maintained outside the graph. It may use a hash array internally. It
contains only the short name of the occurrence, never the full path and never a
forward binding stack. A current full name is derived from this table plus the
`node` chain. Runtime resolution of an arbitrary textual path is a traversal
from an explicitly supplied root: for each segment, enumerate current children,
read each child's ShortNameId through the reverse index, select the required
occurrence, and continue. Tree mutation may therefore change the result of the
same textual lookup without changing the text.

A direct Lmx pointer is already a resolved reference. Invocation through a
callable occurrence, merge of a known subtree and traversal through an existing
reference must not perform a global name lookup and must not depend on a
`Name -> value` namespace. Failure of the reverse index is an indexing/runtime
integrity error for textual lookup and diagnostics; it does not create a new
value semantics.

The address of every live Lmx node is stable because direct graph references and
the sole reverse-name index use that address as identity. A storage manager may
move a node only through a stop-the-world or equivalent operation that rewrites
every affected graph reference and side-table key atomically; ordinary source
execution and merge observe stable addresses.

A callable is an ordinary Lmx occurrence. Its classified `data` identifies a C
method entry point from the program-wide method pool/range. Its `node` identifies
the containing Structure whose address is passed to that entry point. The C
method body is not the callable value and is not a per-Structure vtable member.
Copying a callable occurrence during merge creates a new Lmx occurrence and
rewrites its `node`, while its immutable link to the already compiled method may
be retained.

At both L2 and L3, Structures are passed by reference. Ordinary assignment,
argument passing and return do not copy a tree. An operation whose semantics are
copy, serialization or `merge` may allocate new occurrences. Source-semantic
lifetime never follows an incidental C automatic/static storage choice.

Arrays retain their specified rectangular indexing and shape semantics. This
revision does not adopt the hosted `ArrayReference` as the language ABI and
abolishes the claim that Structure nodes require per-array or per-Structure
descriptor prefixes. The final array payload domain, `len`, shape storage,
element representation, element/`node` relationship and view-sharing policy
are unresolved and listed again in section 19.29.12.5. Ownership is not
unresolved: every mutable ordinary Array header, shape, element/backing
allocation and view metadata belongs to the containing Message's one logical
arena.

2.1. Structural Compatibility without Runtime Descriptors

A type is not a runtime entity. Compatibility is the explicit fact that `varA`
may be used in place of `varB` in `Consumer`. The structural predicate
`implements(varA varB Consumer)` in section 19.21.3 operates on paths in the
source/current Structure model; it does not require a namespace descriptor.

`Consumer` is the consuming structure: code or data, walked as a P0 tree. «Used in Consumer» means it
mentions `varB` and concrete branches of `varB` (name paths) at syntactic field-follow
and argument sites. The comparison does not require every first-level name of
`varB` on `varA`. It requires only those paths of `varB` that `Consumer` actually touches.

    implements(varA varB Consumer)
        iff every path in uses(Consumer varB) is present on varA
            and every primitive leaf of those paths is admitted as in 2.2.3

`uses(Consumer varB)` is the set of syntactic name paths (`varB`, `varB.foo`, `varB.foo.bar`, …)
collected from Consumer. Collection stays at P0 field-follow; it is not alias analysis,
callee expansion, computed-name evaluation or heap dataflow. A path that is not
syntactically visible in Consumer is unchecked at this site, or is carried explicitly
across an LMX Message boundary.

This is generic by default. The requirement set is computed from the Consumer
body, not from a separately declared interface taken as a whole. An unused
method or field of `varB` is not required. An empty `uses(Consumer varB)` is true: there is
nothing to use. There is no two-argument form. Without a consumer there is no
type.

The predicate does not compare values behind unused names, field order,
duplicate count, unused nested contents, callable signatures, mutability,
ownership, effects, ABI spelling or target-language layout. A name such as
`validate` has no privileged hook behavior: when a used path names it, `varA` must expose that ordinary name along the path.

Repeated occurrences do not add multiplicity to this predicate. One occurrence
of an exact name and any greater number of occurrences expose the same one name
along a path. Unqualified lookup of that name selects occurrence `[0]`
(section 2.3). Occurrence-qualified field access names a specific occurrence
and does not change the used-path rule.

Constructing a Structure does not create type metadata and does not add
compatibility evidence.

Compatibility is directional and consumer-relative. `implements(varA varB Consumer)` does
not imply `implements(varB varA Consumer)` and does not imply `implements(varA varB Consumer2)` for a
different consumer. Backend projection implements an already made structural
decision and is not compatibility evidence. Explicit converters (section 2.2.4)
are ordinary receivers; `implements` does not invoke them.

A translator or interpreter may apply `implements(varA varB Consumer)` at an
explicit structural replacement, argument consumption and return admission,
supplying Consumer from the remaining known source tree, or from an explicit
Message Consumer or used-path list. This check is compiler/runtime work over
Structures, not creation of a hidden runtime namespace or descriptor object.
Those applications are not a two-argument source receiver.

2.1.1. Two Thicknesses of the Same Predicate

The same `implements(varA varB Consumer)` is strict or relaxed according to how thick `Consumer`
is, not according to a second type system.

When `Consumer` walks fields of `varB` — for example `FileManager` consumes `f\close` and
`f\read` on a `File` — the used tree is fat down to those leaves. Admission is
strict: `varA` must provide every such path. A `File` method that `FileManager`
does not touch is not required. This used-tree check is the language's generic:
the contract is the Consumer body.

When `Consumer` is thin — for example `File` itself is the consumer of a primitive
`FILE` word that it later passes into its own methods — `uses(Consumer FILE)` does
not enter a method tree on that word. The leaf is a memory cell. Admission is
then the primitive-leaf rule of section 2.2.3: conversion is not forbidden, and
a converter may be selected from an explicitly supplied description Structure.
This is wider than duck typing: the bits remain uncommitted until this Consumer
asks, and a keyed converter may still produce the word Consumer needs.

Wanting `File` to be a file, and not an abstract machine word, is therefore not
a prohibition `u32 ↛ FILE`. It is the decision to put the operations on the
`File` structure and to make Consumer call `file\close` rather than a parallel
function over a raw word. If Consumer only calls an external `fclose(word)`,
the consumed value remains a word.

2.1.2. What This Is Not

It is not Java's nominative interface taken as a whole. Java requires every
method of the declared interface; unused methods are commonly stubbed and fail
later. Lingvamyxa requires the paths this Consumer actually uses.

It is not Python's class-tagged object with optional manual `int(s)`. A Python
value already has a class; conversion is a call the program writes, or a late
`AttributeError`. Lingvamyxa conversion at a primitive leaf may use an explicit
description Structure composed by runtime `merge` and is aimed at what this
Consumer asked.

Near neighbours exist and are not this predicate: C++ template instantiation
checks operations that were instantiated; Haskell collects constraints from a
body but still names type classes; Go and TypeScript compare a declared shape;
pydantic, serde and .NET `TypeConverter` coerce to a declared schema. None of
them is `implements(varA varB Consumer)` plus merge-keyed converters on the used tree.

2.2. Substrate, Primitive Leaves and Conversion Keys

2.2.1. Memory Substrate Is Not a Fourth Value Kind

Physically, leaf payloads occupy registered memory/address domains. External
input before a receiver recognizes it — a decimal numeral, UTF-8 text, an
image, a machine word — is substrate storage. Recognition builds or updates
Lmx nodes whose `data` points at the selected domain.

This does not create a separate primitive object kind. The source/runtime
ontology remains one kind of reference:

    Value = Lmx reference

The domain of `data` tells the runtime how the payload is physically handled.
For example `&all_chars[33]` is recognized as a character because it lies in
the character pool range. A branch payload is recognized because it lies in a
branch/child-storage range. A callable is recognized because its data lies in
the common method range or implementation-selected callable record range.

Address classification is an explicit Revision-2 ABI contract. It may use
linker sections, registered arenas and platform-specific pointer comparison;
it need not pretend to be strictly portable ISO C. Each accepted pointer must
belong unambiguously to one registered domain and satisfy that domain's
alignment/object-boundary rules. Unregistered pointers are raw L2 substrate,
not silently accepted high-level leaves.

After a codec, parser or `fromLmx` receiver runs, the result is a Structure
graph whose leaves point to classified storage. Recursion stops where the
consumer stops following children. A profile may attach explicit descriptive
Structures to values through ordinary graph links, but such descriptions are
data and are not mandatory per-node headers used to discover a payload kind.

`implements` compares only the source/current Structure paths its Consumer
actually uses. If a profile wants semantic contracts beyond physical address
classification, it must make those contracts explicit Structure data and pass
or reference them explicitly. Target-language assignment rules (including
ANSI C) remain a backend concern.

2.2.2. Optional Primitive Descriptions Are Ordinary Structures

A primitive is not a built-in object class in the grammar. It is an Lmx leaf
whose `data` belongs to a registered primitive domain. A profile may additionally
author a description Structure in the same way as `User` or `File`: fields,
runtime `merge`, and table rows that are themselves Structures. Such a
description must be reached by an explicit graph reference or operation; it is
not a hidden descriptor attached to every primitive and not a namespace binding.
Hosted tables `class`, `class.semantic`, `class.category`, `class.range`,
`class.spelling` and `class.c.projection` are historical/profile data, not the
language's list of primitives and not required by address classification.

Two axes sit on the same description and must not be collapsed:

    semantic contract
        what Consumer may ask of the cell: count, integer, flag, ratio,
        float(profile), complex(profile), atom, address, opaque, none.

    architecture binding
        how this profile lays the cell down: bit width, signedness, range,
        backend spelling (`uint32_t`, `FILE *`, `int8_t`). Spelling is
        projection data. It is not a Lingvamyxa value kind.

An explicit profile may use runtime `merge` of description Structures as a
lattice. Each merge part appends real occurrences in order; a later part does
not replace an earlier same-name field. Unqualified lookup still selects `[0]`,
the first occurrence, and explicit occurrence notation selects later fields
(section 2.3). `u32` is not «the
set ℤ». It is a cell whose description merged count/integer contracts with a
32-bit unsigned range and a C spelling.

2.2.2.1. Shape of an Explicit Optional Description

When a profile elects to supply a primitive description, that description is an
ordinary Structure. Profiles may name its fields as they wish. A Consumer or
converter sees it only through an explicit reference/path; the runtime does not
consult it to classify the primitive leaf.

    cell
        marker that this description is a primitive leaf, not a Structure
        constructor. Presence only.

    semantic
        default `[0]` contract name on the lattice (section 2.2.2.2).

    numeric / integer / floating / reference / opaque / callable / description
        boolean flags recording which contracts were merged in. Hosted
        `class.category` is this row. Flags are not a second predicate.

    width
        `fixed(N)` or `profile`. Distinct from range: `int` is profile-width
        C99 `int`; `u32` is fixed 32.

    signed
        whether the integer contract uses a sign bit.

    range
        closed interval on the cell interpreted as ℕ₀ or ℤ, or `opaque`, or
        a profile token such as `C99-int(profile)`. Pascal-style `0..255`.
        Range is a validator field, not a new primitive kind.

    spelling
        backend type token for lowering. Many descriptions may share one
        spelling (`Boolean` and `int8` may both spell `int8_t`).

    convert
        optional named converters keyed for section 2.2.4. Later merge parts
        append later occurrences of the same converter name; they do not
        displace `[0]`.

Nothing in this shape is a Java class. It is data that `merge` composes and
that `uses(Consumer varB)` may or may not touch.

2.2.2.2. Semantic Contract Lattice

Contracts are fields, merged from general to specific. A useful numeric spine
is:

    flag ⊂ count/ℕ₀ ⊂ integer/ℤ ⊂ ratio/ℚ ⊂ float(profile) ⊂ complex(profile)

ℕ₀ includes 0 (indices, `size_t`). School ℕ starting at 1 is not the count
contract. Irrationals 𝕀 are not a contract: no architecture implements them.
IEEE `double` is `float(profile)`, not ℝ. `bigint` / `real` / `decimal` in a
profile are still descriptions on this lattice (exact integer, high-precision
float, decimal float) with different architecture bindings (GMP, MPFR,
decNumber), not extra Value kinds.

Orthogonal contracts, same substrate, not rungs of that spine:

    atom
        finite named ℕ₀ (enumerations, symbols). Range is the admitted names
        or ordinals, not «ℂ».

    address
        L2 typed address. Integer-shaped; not numeric-compatible with `int`
        unless this Consumer uses it as integer.

    opaque
        handle identity. `FILE` lives here. The cell may be pointer-sized;
        the contract is not integer.

    none
        `void`: no cell content. Distinct from unit.

    unit
        empty Structure (the other leaf, section 2.2.2.6), not a primitive
        with range `0`.

Merging `integer` into a description does not make `+` a field of the cell.
Operators remain callable receivers reachable through the selected structural
profile. If `C` writes `x + 1`, that is the `+` receiver consuming `x`;
admission of `x` at that site uses the numeric/
integer flags only when that consumer's used paths, or the operator's
admitted operand contract, ask for them.

2.2.2.3. Architecture Binding

Width, signedness, range and spelling bind the contract to a machine. They
are how a Java-style interface would be implemented under a concrete CPU/ABI,
except the «implementation» is data on the same Structure.

    u8      count + integer, unsigned, fixed 8, range 0..255
    i32     integer, signed, fixed 32, range INT32_MIN..INT32_MAX
    int     integer, signed, profile width, range C99-int(profile)
    size_t  count, unsigned, profile width, range 0..SIZE_MAX(profile)
    Boolean flag, range -1..1, spelling may be int8_t
    double  float(profile), range C99-double(profile)
    FILE    opaque, range opaque, spelling FILE
    void    none

Two descriptions with the same spelling are not thereby the same contract:
`Boolean` and `int8` may both lower to `int8_t` and still differ in `semantic`
and `range`. Two descriptions with the same contract and different spellings
are profile aliases (`uint8` / `u8`).

The historical hosted `class.semantic` stores the old translator's selected
contract name. Hosted `class.range`
stores the range field. Hosted `class.category` stores the merged flags.
Hosted `class.spelling` / `class.c.projection` store lowering. Completeness of
those tables is a profile duty (every named primitive has a description), not
a language enumeration. The current bootstrap rows are incomplete; missing
rows are a hosted-profile gap, not missing kinds. Revision 2 does not require
these tables for ordinary leaf representation.

2.2.2.4. Authoring by `merge`

A profile authors a primitive by merging contract parts and then architecture
fields. Conceptual source, not a required spelling:

    Cell:
    end: Cell

    Count:
        semantic: count
        numeric: 1
