A function return value is one logical source-level Lmx reference, regardless
of its classified payload domain. If the return is an `fm` Structure, updating
the fields of its existing
return Structure and returning one Structure reference admitted by
`implements(returnedValue declaredReturn returnConsumers)` are source-semantically equivalent.

A function may return a Structure with ordered and/or semantic named fields.

Example return signature:

    (f32 harvest, f32 redApples, f32 greenApples)

Canonical positional structural return into an existing `fm` result Structure:

    return: bed\area 0 0

Named return variables may also be updated before an explicit terminal value return:

    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
    return: harvest redApples greenApples

The canonical return mechanism for an `fn` is `return: value`. In a valid
terminal trailer position, `return: value` also closes the `fn` source frame.
Named return variables are child occurrences of an already existing result
Structure. The field updates occur before the return control exit; return itself
only forwards the result reference.

14.3. Destructuring

Destructuring syntax using `=` is not part of the current native structural core.

The semantic operation still exists: a produced Structure may be retained as one
Lmx value, stored in a compiler service temporary, or used to update target
occurrences from its fields by name or position.

Possible explicit receiver form:

unpack: toEat(myFilter, Monday)
    harvest
    redApples
    greenApples
end: unpack

Conceptual meaning:

    tmp receives toEat(myFilter, Monday)
    harvest receives tmp\harvest
    redApples receives tmp\redApples
    greenApples receives tmp\greenApples

If the produced Structure has no field names, a receiver-defined positional unpack form may map fields by order.

?
The canonical destructuring/update syntax is not specified. A final execution-model specification must define whether destructuring is written through an `unpack` receiver, through target-list syntax without `=`, or through another explicit structural form.
?

---ANSI C---
A translator may implement destructuring by retaining the produced Lmx reference
in a compiler-generated service temporary and then generating field updates one
by one. The temporary is not a source local, hidden namespace or closure field.

variant 1:
For a compile-time-proven invariant graph shape, generated code may replace
generic traversal with validated direct Lmx references/offsets. It must not use
a generated per-source-name C layout or attached descriptor.

variant 2:
Otherwise generated code reads fields through generic runtime Lmx traversal.
---ANSI C---

15. Structural Grammar Sketch

This grammar sketch describes the structural parser only. It does not define final receiver semantics.

15.0. P0 Source-Event Automaton

This subsection is normative for P0 structure recognition. The grammar below is
only a compact readable projection of the same machine.

P0 is one recursive source-event automaton over source positions:

    P0 = (S, E, delta, O)

where:

    S  is the parser state;
    E  is the stream of source events produced by the source scanner;
    delta consumes one event and updates the state;
    O  is the produced parser tree with source spans and node flags.

There is no separate line parser, brace parser, bracket parser or expression
parser at P0. Physical lines are input fragments, not containers in the
abstract tree. Parentheses, index brackets and Mix braces all enter nested P0
contexts; their difference is the channel where the resulting Structure is
used.

State:

    S.position
        current source position and absolute span base.

    S.lexical_mode
        SOURCE
        C_STRING
        EXACT_IDENTIFIER
        PYTHON_RAW_QUOTE
        BLOCK_STRING
        RAW_COMMENT
        LINE_COMMENT

    S.level_state
        the normalized source level state. Canonical dotted spelling maps
        leading dots to integer source levels. Relaxed indentation maps
        indentation columns to the same integer source levels through the
        indentation-stack rule.

    S.form_stack
        ROOT(level 0)
        VERTICAL(level K)
        SHORT(head at level N, implicit argument level N + 1)
        BOUNDED_PAREN(base level B)
        BOUNDED_INDEX(base level B)
        BOUNDED_MIX(local base level 0)

    S.output_cursor
        the current ordinary Structure insertion point, plus the current
        source span being attached to the next node.

Events:

    LEVEL(K)
        next source item is at normalized source level K.

    ATOM(value)
        ordinary token/value after operator splitting and payload recognition.

    HEAD(value)
        head token before `:`.

    COLON
        opens a short form for the preceding HEAD.

    NEWLINE
        physical newline separator.

    COMMA_SEPARATOR
        comma separator at the current scan depth. It separates fields like
        whitespace, but it has no indentation/source-level effect.

    SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR
        semicolon separator/trailer at the current scan depth. It may terminate
        the current open short form.

    OPEN_PAREN / CLOSE_PAREN
        `(` and matching `)` bounded Structure delimiters.

    OPEN_INDEX / CLOSE_INDEX
        `[` and matching `]` bounded index/access delimiters.

    OPEN_MIX / CLOSE_MIX
        `{` and matching `}` Mix bounded form delimiters.

    TAIL_CUTTER(candidate)
        a candidate visible close/cut form, such as `end`, `return`, `until`
        or a dash-fence delimiter. Candidate status is later validated by the
        current receiver/profile context.

    EOF
        end of source.

Lexical shielding:

    If S.lexical_mode is not SOURCE, braces, parentheses, brackets, commas,
    semicolons, colons, dots, indentation and tail-cutter words are payload
    characters for that lexical mode. They do not produce structural events
    until that lexical mode closes.

    C_STRING closes by its C string delimiter rules.
    EXACT_IDENTIFIER closes by its exact identifier delimiter rules.
    PYTHON_RAW_QUOTE closes by its matching raw quote run.
    BLOCK_STRING closes by its matching column-0 `===...` fence.
    RAW_COMMENT closes by its matching column-0 `***...` fence.
    LINE_COMMENT closes at physical newline.

    Therefore a `}` inside a string, exact identifier, raw string, block string
    or comment inside a Mix bounded form does not close that Mix bounded form.

Level transitions:

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K + 1))
        opens one Structure field under the current source parent. Portable P0
        source-level syntax opens block structure by one atomic level increase.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K))
        produces a sibling item at the current source level, unless the current
        scan depth is controlled by an open short/bounded form.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K - 1))
        closes the current visible vertical Structure by the ordinary one-step
        source-level cut.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K - M), M > 1)
        is not ordinary block syntax. It is admitted only when the current
        source event is consumed by a valid tail-cutter layer for that context.

    An empty physical line is a level-0 boundary event. It may close an open
    level-1 vertical tail by the ordinary level decrease, but it is not a
    same-level delimiter below level 1.

Short-form transitions:

    HEAD COLON at source level N creates:

        Frame(head, BodyStructure at implicit argument level N + 1)

    Inline tail fields after `:` are appended to that BodyStructure at the
    implicit argument level N + 1.

    NEWLINE inside an open short form performs the synthetic short-form level
    step from N + 1 back to N. This step is only a separator step. It is not by
    itself the trailer of the short form.

    After that newline:

        if the next source item appears at level N + 1, the atomic level
        increase N -> N + 1 opens a new Structure field in the same short-form
        argument sequence;

        if the next source item appears at level N or smaller, the short form
        is complete and the next item is handled by ordinary sibling, cut or
        receiver-trailer rules.

    COMMA at the current scan depth separates fields and does not terminate an
    open short form. A single comma does not append an empty field. Repeated
    explicit separators may materialize positional skips where the consuming
    profile admits them.

    SEMICOLON at the current scan depth terminates the current open short form.
    It does not append a field to that short form. After terminating the short
    form, it separates the next source item/argument segment at the same scan
    depth. If no short form is open at that scan depth, it is an explicit
    source-item/argument-segment separator or a cosmetic trailing marker
    according to the surrounding context. Repeated explicit separators may
    materialize positional skips where the consuming profile admits them.

Bounded-form transitions:

    OPEN_PAREN starts a nested BOUNDED_PAREN context. Its contents are parsed by
    the same P0 source/token rules, with source-level composition measured
    relative to the bounded opener. CLOSE_PAREN closes that context. If a short
    form is still open inside the bounded context, it is completed as part of
    closing the bounded context.

    OPEN_INDEX after a target starts a nested BOUNDED_INDEX context. Its
    contents are parsed by the same P0 source/token rules. CLOSE_INDEX closes
    that context and attaches the resulting Structure as an index/access suffix
    to the preceding target or to a receiver form that admits bracket syntax.

    OPEN_MIX starts a nested BOUNDED_MIX context for a Mix bounded form `{...}`.
    Its contents are parsed by
    the same P0 source/token rules in a local level-0 context. CLOSE_MIX closes
    that context. The resulting Structure is stored as a Mix-flagged parser
    node with its source position/span. Ordinary structural consumers may skip
    Mix-flagged nodes; document/Mix consumers project them into active overlay
    intervals and marks.

    Mix bounded forms are then anchored to the surrounding source stream.
    Inline/tail Mix is recorded in the current field sequence. A leading Mix
    prefix before an ordinary item is recorded at that following item's source
    level before the item itself. A column-0 Mix-only physical line is queued
    as a margin Mix event and attached to the next ordinary LMX source item;
    several such lines keep their order. A non-margin standalone Mix line uses
    the ordinary source level of that line.

    A matching closing delimiter line may return to the opener level. The
    closer token itself is the bounded-form close event, not an ordinary
    sibling source item.

Tail-cutter transitions:

    Tail-cutters are not magic words in P0 by spelling alone. A token such as
    `return` or `until` becomes a tail-cutter only when it appears at a source
    event where a valid receiver/profile context admits that close/cut role.
    Otherwise it remains an ordinary item/operator/receiver token for later
    semantic processing.

    `end: name` is a visible structural close form. Its argument is not
    evaluated as a runtime expression. It is compared with the admitted close
    targets supplied by the receiver being closed: normally the privileged
    `name` argument as the canonical close target, and the receiver/head key
    as an admitted structural alternative.

    `return`, `return: value`, `until` and dash-fence delimiter forms are
    members of the same tail-cutter family, but each is admitted only in the
    receiver/profile positions defined for it.

EOF transition:

    EOF closes the root context after all open structures that may be closed by
    EOF have been completed. Any remaining open bounded form or lexical mode is
    a syntax error.

Output invariants:

    P0 constructs Structure, Frame, Atom/value and Mix-flagged nodes with source
    spans. It does not execute Structures, evaluate expressions, resolve
    functions, declare variables or decide type semantics.

    A named Structure is translated or interpreted as a call/function/subroutine
    only when a later semantic layer resolves its head to an explicit callable
    Lmx occurrence, provider link or structural path. An anonymous Structure
    remains Structure data until a receiver consumes it under a receiver-defined
    rule. No runtime namespace is part of P0 output or later invocation.

    Any search for a matching `)`, `]` or `}` must use the same lexical modes as
    P0 itself. There is no separate opaque boundary walker that merely counts
    characters.

    program             ::= { empty_line | physical_item }

    physical_item       ::= raw_file_comment_block
                          | block_string_literal
                          | logical_line

    raw_file_comment_block
                        ::= raw_comment_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                              raw_ignored_line*
                            raw_comment_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            where 3 <= N <= 80

    raw_comment_fence(N)
                        ::= exactly N "*" characters at column 0

    block_string_literal
                        ::= block_string_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                              block_string_content_line*
                            block_string_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            where 3 <= N <= 80

    block_string_fence(N)
                        ::= exactly N "=" characters at column 0

    trailing_horizontal_space
                        ::= { " " | tab }

    horizontal_space    ::= " " | tab

    logical_line        ::= source_level line_content newline

    indentation         ::= { "." | " " | tab }

    source_level        ::= integer source level assigned by the
                            source-level normalization prepass

    source-level normalization prepass:
                            maps the physical source spelling to source-level
                            item/level events
                            before the structural grammar consumes it;
                            in canonical dotted notation, source_level is the
                            count of leading dot characters;
                            in relaxed indentation notation, source_level is
                            computed by the indentation-stack rule.

    empty_line          ::= trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            level-0 structural boundary event;
                            closes an open level-1 vertical tail by ordinary
                            source-level decrease, but is not a same-level
                            delimiter below level 1

    line_content        ::= dot_only_marker [ comment_body ]
                          | comment_body
                          | disabled_body [ comment_body ]
                          | line_item_sequence [ comment_body ]

    dot_only_marker     ::= empty content after indentation
                            valid only if source_level > 0;
                            may delimit/open an anonymous Structure at that level

    comment_body        ::= "#" ignored_until_newline

    disabled_body       ::= "%" enabled_line_body

    enabled_line_body   ::= line_item_sequence

    compact_frame       ::= name "(" [ argument_list ] ")"

    line_frame          ::= source_level line_item_sequence newline

    line_item_sequence  ::= source_item
                            { semicolon_segment_separator source_item }
                            [ semicolon_segment_separator ]

    source_item         ::= colon_frame_body
                          | nullary_line_body
                          | structural_value

    colon_frame_body    ::= head ":" [ line_arguments ]

    semicolon_segment_separator
                         ::= ";"

    // Semicolon terminates the current open short form/source item at the
    // current scan depth and separates the next source item or argument
    // segment. Comma is an ordinary field separator and never terminates the
    // current open short form.

    head                ::= bare_identifier
                          | exact_identifier
                          | symbolic_head

    nullary_line_body   ::= name
                            valid in executable statement position only when
                            `name` resolves to a zero-argument consumable binding:
                            registered nullary operator/receiver or visible zero-argument fn/sub

    line_arguments      ::= short_tail

    short_tail          ::= short_field
                            { short_tail_field_separator short_field }
                            [ "," ]

    short_tail_field_separator
                         ::= horizontal_space+
                          | ","

    short_field         ::= colon_frame_body
                          | structural_value

    // In ordinary physical short-line form, comma separates fields in the
    // current short form. Semicolon is not a short-tail field separator: it
    // terminates the current short form and exposes the following segment at
    // the surrounding scan depth.
    // Physical newline is not a short-form trailer; after newline, the next
    // source-level event decides whether the open frame receives more fields
    // or is completed by level rules.
    // Operator-token consumption is deferred to a later consumer.

    argument_list       ::= bounded_item_sequence

    bounded_item_sequence
                         ::= bounded_item { bounded_item_separator bounded_item }
                             [ bounded_item_separator ]

    bounded_item_separator
                         ::= horizontal_space+
                          | comma_field_separator
                          | semicolon_segment_separator
                          | newline/source-level composition

    comma_field_separator
                         ::= ","

    bounded_item        ::= colon_frame_body
                          | nullary_line_body
                          | structural_value

    // Inside bounded Structures, short colon-forms may appear inline.
    // Horizontal whitespace separates simple bounded fields only when no
    // short colon-form is open. Inside an open short colon-form it remains
    // the short-tail field separator. Comma is also a short-tail field
    // separator and never closes the short form. Semicolon terminates an open
    // short form at any scan depth and then separates the following bounded
    // item/argument segment.
    // Newline/source-level composition is not a short-form
    // trailer by itself; the following source level determines continuation
    // or closure. Inside a bounded form that continues across a physical
    // newline, the next physical line must remain inside that bounded form:
    // it must be at the corresponding deeper dotted source level or, in
    // relaxed notation, at an indentation column deeper than the line that
    // opened the bounded form. A line whose next source token is the matching
    // RPAREN/RBRACKET is the closing line and may return to the opener level.
    // The matching RPAREN/RBRACKET closes the bounded Structure; if a short
    // form is still open inside it, that short form is completed as part of
    // closing the bounded context.

    bounded_structure   ::= "(" [ argument_list | vertical_argument_list ] ")"

    source_fragment     ::= P0 source parsed in a local level-0 context

    mix_structure       ::= "{" source_fragment "}"
                            parsed by the same P0 source/token rules in a
                            local level-0 context and stored as a Mix-flagged
                            Structure node

    index_access_suffix ::= "[" [ argument_list | vertical_argument_list ] "]"
                            attached to a preceding target by an expression,
                            update or receiver profile that admits index/access
                            syntax

    vertical_argument_list
                         ::= newline source_line { source_line }
                            where each contained source_line follows the same
                            source-level rules as ordinary vertical composition

    structural_value
                         ::= compact_frame
                          | bounded_structure
                          | mix_structure
                          | atom_or_value

Square brackets in the portable core are not a free-standing replacement for
parentheses. `target[...]` is bounded index/access syntax attached to a preceding
target or to a receiver form that explicitly admits bracket syntax. `[]:` in
head position is a symbolic receiver spelling, such as an Array
constructor, not the same thing as an index suffix. A standalone `[a b]` has no
portable core meaning unless a profile explicitly admits it.

The vertical Structure tree is produced by source levels and explicit visible delimiters: following source items at one-step smaller source level as level delimiters, canonical dotted delimiter lines, relaxed dash-fence lines, and admitted source-level tail-cutter forms such as `end:` or terminal return where valid. The physical newline separator is not itself the trailer; it only helps expose the next source item and its level.

For a source item at level N, vertical child arguments are written at level N + 1. A following source item at level N + 1 is one positional child item of that frame/Structure. In a short colon-form, the inline tail fields are also treated as fields at the implicit argument level N + 1. A following source item deeper than N + 1 belongs to the immediately preceding child item only if that child item opens a Structure. Scalar literals and identifier values do not become headers merely because a deeper item follows.

A canonical dotted delimiter line or relaxed dash-fence line at level K may delimit an anonymous positional Structure at level K. If it is followed by source items at level K + 1, it opens such an anonymous Structure. The next delimiter at level K closes the previous anonymous Structure and may open another one when followed by deeper items. A following source item at level K - 1 closes that anonymous Structure by the ordinary one-step cut. A larger cut is not ordinary P0 syntax and requires an admitted tail-cutter layer.

An empty colon Frame is not rejected by P0 merely because its body has zero
fields. It is still a structural item. The active receiver, validator,
translator or profile decides whether a nullary colon Frame is meaningful in
that position. For example, a profile may reject `level0:` followed by sibling
`level1:` when `level0` requires a body, or reject `level0(level1:)` when the
inner empty Frame is not an admitted argument. Those are receiver/profile
diagnostics, not a separate P0 grammar category.

This grammar is intentionally incomplete around full update semantics, full indexing, full field-follow access, type constructors, literals and relation forms because those areas require final design decisions. Native structural grammar does not include `=` assignment, `=` named-field syntax, bare `end`, `end <target>`, bare non-nullary receiver/callable calls or a built-in `case` form. The standalone dash-fence line is part of vertical structure syntax as a nameless delimiter: it may open an anonymous positional Structure when followed by a deeper item, separate sibling anonymous Structures, or close/cut an anonymous/implicit section.

Ordinary line arguments are whitespace-separated short-tail fields. A parser must not use receiver signatures to insert hidden nesting while parsing `line_arguments`.

Comma and semicolon have different P0 roles at the current scan depth. Comma is an ordinary explicit field separator, like whitespace without indentation/source-level effects; it may add another field to an open short tail. Semicolon is the explicit separator/trailer: for a short form, it terminates that short form and never adds another field to the open short tail. When no short form is open at the current scan depth, comma is optional conventional separator notation and semicolon may act as an explicit source-item separator or trailing marker. A trailing comma or semicolon with no following item is accepted as a cosmetic trailing marker at any block level, including file/root level 0. A comma or semicolon inside a deeper bounded Structure or compact Frame belongs to that deeper form.

When a semicolon terminates a short form, it terminates/materializes the current
headless argument segment at that scan depth. If the neighboring segment has no
explicit head of its own, P0 may expose it as a separate headless Structure.
This preserves the source-item boundary for higher profiles. A
declaration/profile layer may then consume those headless Structures as repeats
of a preceding repeatable head, while an expression layer may reject them or
treat them as ordinary neighboring Structures. P0 does not silently append the
headless item to the terminated short tail and does not use namespace lookup to
decide whether the item is a call or a repeated declaration.

Repeated explicit separators at the same scan depth may materialize an empty
positional field. This is not specific to semicolon: `a,,c`, `a;;c`, `a,;c`
and `a;,c` all contain an explicit positional skip between `a` and `c` when the
current bounded/profile context admits empty positional fields. A leading
separator may similarly skip the first positional field. A single trailing
separator remains a cosmetic trailing marker. The parser records such a skip as
a positional-skip field; a receiver, validator or translator may consume it
only if the corresponding positional argument has an admitted default form.
Otherwise it must report an error rather than silently compacting the argument
list.

Structural grammar has no separate anonymous-function syntax. A bounded
Structure, compact Frame or vertical anonymous Structure remains Structure data.
The parser never marks a Structure as executable merely because it is nested
inside arguments. Translation or interpretation of that Structure happens only
when the active receiver consumes it under a receiver-defined rule, or when the
translator resolves a compact Frame head to an explicit callable Lmx occurrence,
provider link or structural path. No runtime namespace participates in this
decision.

Labels are not a separate structural grammar category. A source line such as
`outerLoop:` is a colon frame with an empty inline tail and a required following
vertical block. The active receiver/profile decides whether that frame creates
a nullary callable/control Lmx occurrence in the current call/body Structure,
a control-target link, both, or an error. A later bare line such as `outerLoop`
is also not special label grammar; in executable statement position the
translator may resolve it to that explicit occurrence/link. Scope is represented
by the actual Structure tree, not by a runtime binding overlay.

15.1. Structural Semantics in EBNF Form

The following EBNF describes the structural-semantic normal form produced by the
parser. It is not a separate surface syntax and it does not assign binding,
variable, type, field, atom, description, operation or execution semantics. Those meanings
are assigned later by receiver consumption, explicit description Structures and the active
level/profile.

The notation uses `Structure(...)` and `Frame(...)` as semantic constructors:

    SourceFile
        ::= RootBlock

    RootBlock
        ::= Block(0)

    Block(K)
        ::= Structure({ BlockElement(K) })

    BlockElement(K)
        ::= FrameElement(K)
         |  ValueElement(K)
         |  ExpressionElement(K)
         |  AnonymousStructure(K)

    FrameElement(K)
        ::= Frame(Head, BodyStructure, FrameTrailer)

    Head
        ::= BareIdentifier
         |  ExactIdentifier
         |  SymbolicHead
         |  ProfileHeadPath

    BodyStructure
        ::= Structure({ Field })

    Field
        ::= FrameElement(current)
         |  ValueElement(current)
         |  ExpressionElement(current)
         |  BoundedStructure
         |  CompactFrame
         |  AnonymousStructure(current)

    ValueElement(K)
        ::= NumericLiteral
         |  StringLiteral
         |  BareIdentifier
         |  ExactIdentifier

    ExpressionElement(K)
        ::= ExpressionStructure

    CompactFrame
        ::= Frame(Head, BoundedBody, RPAREN_BOUNDED)

    BoundedStructure
        ::= Structure({ BoundedElement }, RPAREN_BOUNDED | RBRACKET_BOUNDED)

    BoundedElement
        ::= FrameElement(bounded)
         |  ValueElement(bounded)
         |  ExpressionElement(bounded)
         |  CompactFrame
         |  BoundedStructure

    AnonymousStructure(K)
        ::= Structure({ BlockElement(K + 1) })

This semantic EBNF is read with these structural rules:

    ShortTail
        ::= Field { (HorizontalSpace+ | COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR) Field }
            [ COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR ]

    BoundedBody
        ::= [ BoundedElement { BoundedSeparator BoundedElement }
             [ BoundedSeparator ] ]

    BoundedSeparator
        ::= HorizontalSpace+
         |  COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR
         |  SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR
         |  NewlineSourceComposition

    COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR
        ::= COMMA_SEPARATOR

    SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR
        ::= SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR

    SEMICOLON_SHORT
        ::= SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR

    FrameTrailer
        ::= SEMICOLON_SHORT
         |  RPAREN_BOUNDED
         |  RBRACKET_BOUNDED
         |  LEVEL_DROP
         |  DELIM_NAMELESS
         |  RECEIVER_TRAILER
         |  EOF_TRAILER

    RECEIVER_TRAILER
        ::= END_NAMED
         |  UNTIL_CLOSE
         |  TERMINAL_RETURN
         |  ProfileTrailer

Comma and semicolon semantics:

    COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR appends/separates fields in the open ShortTail. It
    never terminates the current open short form.

    SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR first acts as SEMICOLON_SHORT if a short form
    is open at the current scan depth. It never appends a field to that
    ShortTail. After the short form is materialized, the same semicolon
    separates the following source item or argument segment.

    When no short form is open at the current scan depth, comma may act as
    ordinary separator notation and semicolon may act as an explicit source-item
    separator or cosmetic trailing marker.

    Therefore:

        call(cast: Type value, other)

    has one semantic argument segment for `call`, because `other` is still a
    field of the open `cast` short form. To end `cast` and supply `other` as the
    next argument segment, use semicolon:

        call(cast: Type value; other)

Physical newline semantics:

    Physical newline is not a short-form trailer. It ends the physical line.
    If the next source item is at the open short form's implicit argument
    level, that item continues the open short form. If the next source item is
    at the short form's own source level or smaller, the short form is complete
    and ordinary sibling/level-close rules apply.

Bounded delimiter semantics:

    RPAREN_BOUNDED closes the current parenthesized BoundedStructure or
    CompactFrame. RBRACKET_BOUNDED closes the current bracketed
    BoundedStructure. If a short form is open inside that bounded form, that
    short form is completed as part of closing the bounded context, and then
    the bounded form itself is closed.

Source-level semantics:

    A line at source level K + 1 is a child field of the current item at level K.
    A line at source level K - 1 closes the current open Structure by the
    ordinary one-step cut. Larger decreases require an admitted tail-cutter
    layer.
    A delimiter at level K may open an AnonymousStructure(K) when followed by
    items at K + 1, or close/separate the current anonymous or implicit section
    at level K.

Receiver semantics:

    Frame(Head, BodyStructure, FrameTrailer) is still only data after parsing.
    The receiver named by Head consumes BodyStructure and assigns semantic roles
    to positional fields, anonymous Structures, named child Frames, labels,
    operators, declarations, explicit model descriptions, message/table data and control
    trailers. The parser does not infer those meanings from spelling alone.

16. Canonical Examples

16.1. Hello World

Source:

print: "Hello World"

Full form:

L3:
    print: "Hello World"
end: L3

Normal form:

L3(
    print("Hello World")
)

Shortest form:

print: Hello World

`Hello` and `World` are accepted only if the active L3 `print` receiver and lexical rules allow bare atoms/symbols in that position.

16.2. Function with Structural Return

Source:

fm: estimateHarvest (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    if: bed\crop = Apples
        return: bed\area * 1.7 bed\area * 1.7 bed\area * 1.1
return: bed\area 0 0

Semantic outline:

    `fn` consumes a definition Structure.
    `PlantBed: bed` constructs an Lmx subtree if `PlantBed` resolves through
        compiler work state to an admitted Structure recipe, callable/provider
        link or explicit structural operation. No runtime namespace or attached
        descriptor is involved.
    The return Structure has three named fields.
    `if` consumes condition and body Structures.
    The inner `return:` exits with the Apple-specific structural result.
    The final `return:` is terminal return. It supplies the default structural result and closes the function definition.

16.3. Explicit Return with Named Return Variables

Source:

fm: estimateHarvest (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
return: harvest redApples greenApples

Semantic outline:

    Named return fields are Lmx child occurrences in the selected call/result
        Structure.
    Head/update position updates those concrete occurrences or their explicit
        graph links.
    Terminal `return:` exits, returns their current values as one return Structure and closes the function definition.

16.3.1. Recursive Function with Terminal Return

Source:

fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
    if: n <= 1
        return: 1
return: n * factorial(n - 1)

Semantic outline:

    `return: 1` is a runtime return inside the `if` branch.
    In expression position, `factorial(n - 1)` is a recursive call.
    The final `return:` supplies the recursive result and closes the function definition.

17. Summary Table

    f(a, b)                    compact Frame named f
    f: a b                     line command Frame named f with whitespace-separated short-tail fields
    f: a, b                    f receives a and b; comma is an ordinary explicit field separator
    f: a; b                    f receives only a; semicolon terminates the short form, so b is the next source item at the current level
    f: 1 2                     ordinary short-tail fields, equivalent to f(1, 2)
    f: / child lines at . / end: f
                                canonical dotted vertical Frame named f; vertical fields are at source level N + 1
    f: / indented child lines / end: f
                                relaxed indentation vertical Frame named f
    (a b), (a, b)              bounded Structure; comma is optional conventional notation
    ()                         present empty bounded Structure; not a positional skip
    name: value                ordinary nested Structure, often consumed as a setting/semantic field by receiver
    name:                      empty colon Frame; P0 keeps it, receiver/profile decides whether it is valid
    leading dots               canonical visible source level units in the leading indentation zone
    indentation                relaxed source level units before ordinary source items
    dotted delimiter line      canonical anonymous Structure delimiter; may open, separate or close at its source level
    ---...                     dash-fence source-level marker, 3..80 `-` characters; materializes level K without an ordinary receiver item;
                               close/open/separator behavior follows from neighboring source levels
    smaller-level source line  ordinary one-step source-level cut/trailer; larger cuts require an admitted tail-cutter layer
    same-parent-level line     ordinary source-level cut for the previous child frame and start of the next sibling item
    empty line                 level-0 structural boundary; closes an open level-1 vertical tail
    #                          line comment marker; ignores text to the end of the physical line
    %                          disabled item/block marker; syntax is still parsed and checked
    {...}                      Mix bounded form; inline/tail belongs to the current item, leading prefix belongs to the following item,
                               column-0 Mix-only lines anchor to the next ordinary LMX source item
    ***...                     column-0 raw file comment fence, 3..80 `*` characters; closing fence must have the same length; syntax inside is ignored
    ===...                     column-0 block string fence, 3..80 `=` characters; closing fence must have the same length
    end: name                  explicit named tail-cutter for a visible frame/entity/label block; not the only close mechanism
    L2/L3                      the only level receiver frames
    sub                        receiver that constructs a callable Lmx occurrence and emits its real C body
    fn                         receiver that constructs a callable Lmx occurrence and emits its real C body
    return                     executable-body return receiver/operator; also terminal executable close at definition level
    descriptor                 ordinary explicit description-data spelling if a profile registers it; never an attached type/namespace entry
    operator                   proposed explicit operation-description form; compiler bootstrap may read explicit legacy Tables as work data
    if                         registered receiver, semantics depend on level
    match                      proposed receiver; not registered by the current bootstrap; no built-in `case` syntax
    merge                      runtime Structure operation; when reached, creates a fresh root and copies operand children/subtrees in order with node rebasing
    join                       registered Table receiver; source-marked joins are implemented for translation-time Tables; runtime Table join remains pending
    while                      registered receiver, semantics depend on level
    operator/infix tokens       structural operator fields consumed into expression Structures
    (): result                construct one ordinary Lmx Structure under the selected runtime lifetime policy
    []                         head receiver spelling for ordinary Array forms; exact Revision-2 Array payload ABI is unresolved; postfix `target[...]` is index/access
    @                          L2 address-of storage location
    \                          L2 prefix raw load, and typed field-follow separator in `a\field`
    table                      registered receiver constructing an ordinary explicit Lmx Table value; hosted storage layout is non-normative
    relation/rule/query        ordinary explicit Structure/Table vocabulary; no current generic receiver heads
    c.union                    registered L2 physical-layout receiver; generic union is not registered
    c.array                    registered L2 raw C-array storage receiver; ordinary [] remains an Lmx Array whose exact domain is unresolved
    method/requires.method     legacy bootstrap Tables; not normative implements input
    implements                 implements(varA varB Consumer) consumer-relative structural compatibility receiver; translated or interpreted with the same predicate
    message/operation          ordinary Lmx Structures/explicit description data in L2/L3; no current generic receiver heads


18. Remaining Open Points

This section keeps only the remaining open points that affect common syntax,
L2/L3 execution, expressions, arrays, text, field-follow, closure and ANSI C
projection variants.

18.1. Source-Semantic or Syntax Uncertainties

!!!
Field-follow traversal policy for unknown models, dynamic Structures and
nullable handles is not fixed. Missing exact names and out-of-range occurrence
ordinals already use the checked field-access rule above: they are diagnosed at
translation time when statically known and otherwise by checked runtime access.
!!!

18.2. External or Profile-Level Specification Areas

The exact operator token set and backend lowering rules may be extended by
profiles, but the ANSI C projection deliberately keeps C precedence and
associativity for primitive arithmetic, comparison and boolean expressions.

?
The L2 memory/operator specification must define exact semantics for `@`,
prefix `\`, field-follow `\field`, low-level addressable mutation, raw loads,
raw indexing and address arithmetic.
?

?
The array/text specification must still define slicing, negative indexes if
any, multidimensional arrays, text encoding and the boundary between parser
form and receiver/operator form. Bounds failure itself uses `throw`/`catch`
as specified in section 12.3.
?

?
Initialization and addressable mutation of ordinary source fields remain to be
finished without replacing the structural update semantics in sections 7.3 and
14.1. Nested `fn`/`sub` bodies are real globally reachable C methods and their
callable occurrences are Lmx children of Structures. They do not capture a
runtime namespace, environment or C closure record. A value traditionally
called captured must instead be an explicit field or graph link reachable from
the containing Structure passed as `callable->node`. `c.closure-struct` may
remain only as an explicitly requested raw-C adapter and is never the baseline
source-method representation.
?

Without `throws`, erroneous results may be represented as ordinary Lmx values
allowed by the declared result contract. With `throws`, they are `throw`
delivered to `catch` (sections 19.11 and 19.29.14.4). Iterator exhaustion,
message validation/execution failure and checked array bounds failure use that
same `throw`/`catch` rule. They are not encoded as `None` and are not
`assert`. The exact Revision-2 result/failure subtree and C return status are
still open; this does not permit a hidden source-method argument.
18.3. ANSI C Implementation Variants

---ANSI C---
Revision 2 fixes the generated source-method call boundary and does not treat it
as a backend variant:

    entry(callable->node)

There is exactly one explicit `Lmx *` argument. Direct primitive arguments,
hidden output pointers, `LmContext *`, `LmMessageThread *`, descriptor pointers,
namespace/environment pointers and generated closure records are not conforming
alternatives for source methods.

The exact C return/status type and the arrangement of argument, local, result
and failure nodes behind the containing Structure remain open. Backend helper
functions may have private C signatures. Inlining, scalar replacement, cached
child lookup and direct machine operations are allowed only as proven
optimizations of the generic Lmx semantics; they must preserve observable node
identity, current-tree lookup, runtime merge, repeated-name order, recursion and
concurrency. Ordinary Structure field access cannot depend on a generated
per-name C layout. Explicit `c.*` storage keeps its separately requested raw-C
semantics.
---ANSI C---

19. L2 & L3 Operators and Receiver Families

19.0. Scope of This Section

This section defines the L2/L3 source-level receiver/operator families for
executable, explicit-description-data, data/query, message and service bodies. The first
entries are control-flow receivers; later subsections retain Registry/Table
facilities only as explicit ordinary values and operations.
`merge` is the intentionally broader structural receiver in this list. L2 and
L3 use the `merge` spelling for ordered Structure construction. Table profiles
use `join` to combine explicitly named Table values and produce an ordinary
result Table. A minimal bootstrap L2 profile may expose only the subset
listed in section 20.5, while richer L2 profiles and ordinary L3 profiles may
admit the full set or profile-specific extensions.

This shared list does not mean that L3 contains all L2 operators. L2 has an
additional low-level machine operator/parser-form surface in section 20. L3 is
the high-level subset and cannot use that machine surface. This is an
abstraction boundary, not a sandbox or authority boundary.

The control operators in this section are not raw low-level jumps. A translator
may lower them to branches, labels, Lmx control/result Structures,
cleanup-stack operations or other implementation artifacts behind the
one-pointer method boundary. Structural `merge` always remains an execution-
time generic Lmx tree-copy operation; the compiler emits its recipe/call but
does not execute it, normalize a descriptor or construct its result early.

Unless stated otherwise, these operators have the same source semantics in L2
and L3 when the active profile admits them. Differences belong to profile
constraints, available value types, memory model and lowering policy, not to a
separate parser syntax.

All Revision-2 source implementations in 19.1–19.17 resolve actual Lmx
occurrences from the containing Structure, direct links or an explicit root.
They do not use a runtime Namespace, attached descriptor, closure environment,
hidden MessageThread method argument, per-name C aggregate or semantic C local.
Where an older subsection below still illustrates one of those hosted forms,
that illustration is implementation history only; its surface receiver/control
contract survives only after being lowered through the Revision-2 architecture.

19.0.H. Current Revision-1 Bootstrap Operator Inventory (Historical)

The following operator summary reports the current hosted implementation. Its
status words remain useful for migration planning, but every described
namespace/descriptor/static-layout/eager-merge mechanism is non-normative.

The left column below is a historical item label, not a subsection number.
Status words refer to the current bootstrap:

    IMPLEMENTED       the exact spelling and stated semantic contract exist;
    REGISTERED ONLY   the spelling resolves, but the stated contract is absent
                      or has different semantics;
    TABLE/DATA        ordinary Structure/Array-based data, not an operator or
                      a fourth Value category;
    NOT IMPLEMENTED   the following text proposes a concrete source contract.

Later subsection headings describe the full design families. They do not imply
that a source receiver exists; registration and contract status are stated
explicitly below.

    0.  if / else
        IMPLEMENTED statement receivers; L2/L3.

    1.  match / default-pattern receivers
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register `match:` as a statement/expression
        receiver consuming ordered pattern/body pairs; `default` remains an
        ordinary pattern atom.

    2.  while
        IMPLEMENTED statement receiver; L2/L3.

    3.  until
        REGISTERED ONLY as a generic trailer. The post-condition loop contract
        in section 19.4 is NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: give the trailer a
        loop-closing lowering that re-enters the closed frame while false.

    4.  for
        IMPLEMENTED for the compact counter/triplet form in section 19.5:
        `for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++` plus a vertical or compact body.
        Range-style forms such as `for: i 0 n` remain NOT IMPLEMENTED.

    5.  each
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register a statement receiver over an array
        or iterator descriptor; L3 array traversal uses checked access.

    6.  return
        IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 executable-body receiver; terminal executable close
        is also implemented at definition level.

    7.  break
        IMPLEMENTED for the nearest loop frame; L2/L3.

    8.  continue
        IMPLEMENTED for the nearest loop frame; L2/L3.

    9.  finally
        IMPLEMENTED executable/scope cleanup receiver; L2/L3.

    10. catch / throw / throws
        IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 recoverable failure flow. `throws:` on `fn`/`sub`
        lists names. `throw: Name(arguments)` leaves the current
        executable with a dummy return. `catch: Name (parameters)` is a
        skipped landing pad; after it, execution continues in the same block.
        A throwing call must be a statement or the entire assignment; nested
        throwing expressions are rejected. Duplicate `catch` names in one
        block are an error; a series uses nested `---` blocks. C lowering is
        a same-TU throw channel plus `goto` to the selected catch.

    11. retry / retryable
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register retry-frame and retry-transfer
        statement receivers with explicit cleanup semantics.

    12. assert
        IMPLEMENTED diagnostic statement receiver; L2/L3.

    12a. log / error
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers. Proposed: explicitly bind
        observation statement receivers whose descriptors select their
        handlers/policies. A descriptor may explicitly reference an observation
        Table, but no ambient table participates.

    13. redo
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register a loop-frame transfer that repeats
        the current body without advancing its source.

    14. yield
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register a function-body receiver that emits
        an iterator value and persists the resume state in the iterator
        Structure. Exhaustion is `throw: Stop`, caught by `each`; it is not a
        `None` result from `next`.

    15. labelled break / labelled continue
        NOT IMPLEMENTED; current `break` and `continue` are unlabelled. Proposed:
        introduce the label by an explicit lexical declaration and resolve the
        optional target in the current namespace. Any backend label index is
        only an implementation of that namespace state.

    16. merge / join
        `merge:` is REGISTERED ONLY for the contract below: its current handler
        composes and binds a named structural descriptor and emits its hosted C
        type/layout projection. It emits no result-object storage and constructs
        no Structure instance. The ordered high-level Structure merge specified
        in section 19.17 is NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: keep descriptor/Structure
        merge (L2/L3) separate from optional physical layout projection (L2-only).
        `join:` currently has legacy source-row behavior; normative `join:`
        consumes explicitly resolved Tables and produces an ordinary arena-owned
        Table reference.

    17. table / source / import / predef
        `table:` constructs an ordinary arena-owned Table and may explicitly
        bind its result. `source` is a table-body projection/lifetime marker, not
        a receiver and not a RegistryView selector. Current static C and
        Registry-row projections are legacy implementation paths.
        `import:` is an IMPLEMENTED ordinary module import. `predef:` is an
        IMPLEMENTED hoisted import, but the current bootstrap excludes it from
        L3. Proposed: admit `predef:` on L3 surfaces too; a selected dependency
        retains its own level and exports only explicit namespace declarations;
        Table rows/cells are never imported as names.

    18. cast / implements
        The language form is `implements(varA varB Consumer)` as specified in section
        19.21.3. The hosted translator currently still evaluates a two-name
        first-level subset internally as a migration gap; that is not a source
        receiver and must not be written as `implements(varA varB)`. Translation and
        interpretation use the same source-semantic algorithm once Consumer is
        supplied: resolve `varA` and `varB`, walk Consumer for syntactic paths of
        `varB`, and admit `varA` when every such path is present. Unresolved names
        or bindings
        with missing/malformed descriptors are diagnostics; field
        values, unused nested contents, `requires.method`, `method`, ordinary
        rows and `default` are not consulted.
        See section 19.21.3.
        `cast:` is REGISTERED ONLY for high-level conversion semantics: its
        current emitter allow-lists the *target* name in `class.range` or
        `cast.target`, ignores the source and `Consumer`, and lowers to a
        target-language C cast. That is not the primitive-leaf rule of
        sections 2.2.3–2.2.4. Proposed: converters are ordinary receivers
        selected by merge key on the substitution edge; `implements` only
        observes that the key exists and that conversion is not forbidden;
        restrict raw representation/address casts to L2; the current L3 gate
        is missing.

    19. class / relation / rule / query
        `class` has no privileged core table meaning. A Table named `class` is
        ordinary data. `relation` and `rule` may be ordinary descriptor/data
        conventions. An expression `query(Table, coordinates...)` is NOT
        IMPLEMENTED generically; if added, it explicitly receives its Table and
        returns matching data, empty, or ambiguity.

    20. message / validate / execute / emit
        A message is TABLE/DATA: one LMX document whose root is the standard
        message envelope and whose `expression` is ordinary LMX. Its address is
        `endpoint + route`; the envelope fields are defined in section 19.19.1.
        The `send:` statement in item 27 is IMPLEMENTED for transporting these
        bytes, but it does not itself parse or validate the document.
        `validate`, `execute` and `emit` are NOT IMPLEMENTED as source
        operators. Proposed: register expression receivers
        `validate(Message, NamespaceSnapshot, ExplicitPolicies) -> ExecutionPlan`
        with `throws: Failure`, and
        `execute(ExecutionPlan) -> Result` with `throws: Failure`, plus an
        `emit:` statement receiver that appends a proposed message to the
        current Message Thread outbox. Failure is `throw`/`catch`, not a second
        return type. These operations belong to L2/L3. The lower-level hosted runtime
        now has FIFO inbox/outbox byte queues and local route delivery, but that
        ABI does not itself parse, validate or execute the LMX document and does
        not make these source operators implemented.

    21. model / descriptor / union / c.union
        `model` is ordinary Structure data. A standalone `descriptor:`
        declaration receiver is NOT REGISTERED by the current bootstrap. Named
        Structure descriptor declarations and the current physical `merge:`
        path do attach normalized first-level descriptor views to their explicit
        namespace bindings and emit hosted type/layout projections without
        emitting Registry field rows or implicit instance storage. Broader
        descriptor content remains ordinary Structure/Array data interpreted by
        selected consumers.
        High-level `union:` is NOT IMPLEMENTED. A future variant
        declaration must create an explicit descriptor without choosing physical storage. The
        physical `c.union:` machine-layout receiver is IMPLEMENTED, but its
        required L3 exclusion is not yet enforced. `field.union` is a TABLE/DATA
        layout relation.

    22. operator / receiver / method
        There are no `operator:`, `receiver:` or `method:` declaration receivers.
        The current bootstrap derives initial bindings from `operator.*`,
        `namespace`, `receiver.*`, `method` and `requires.method` Tables. This is
        implementation machinery. A future declarative receiver must explicitly
        create descriptor bindings; it must not insert ambient rows as its
        language meaning.

    23. evidence / validator
        ordinary Structure/Array data, not source operators. Legacy
        `profile.validator` Table lookup exists, but
        the generic validation pipeline is NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: the
        `validate` operation from item 20 returns an ExecutionPlan containing
        validation/authority evidence, or `throw: Failure` with that diagnostic
        as payload; no separate `evidence:` receiver is required.

    24. entry / provides / satisfies / conflict
        ordinary descriptor/data fields, not registered receivers. A program may
        cache `provides`/`satisfies` results in a Table, but `implements(varA varB Consumer)`
        computes compatibility from namespace descriptors and `Consumer`.
        Namespace conflicts and Table
        merge conflicts remain distinct.

    25. procedural / object / functional / logical
        Consumption modes, not operator spellings. Proposed representation:
        an explicit receiver-descriptor field such as `consumptionMode`.
        Concrete actions remain ordinary resolved calls or explicit `validate`,
        `execute`, `query` and `derive` operations.

    26. provider / codec / toLmx / fromLmx
        provider/codec descriptors are ordinary Structures and generic provider
        selection is not yet implemented. They enter resolution only through
        explicit namespace bindings; an operation may use an explicit policy
        Table internally. Legacy `serialization.codec` lookup is IMPLEMENTED.
        `toLmx` and `fromLmx` are
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source operations; only internal table serialization
        helpers currently exist. Proposed generic methods:
        `toLmx(Value) -> Text` with `throws: Failure` and
        `fromLmx(TargetDescriptor, Text) -> Value` with `throws: Failure`,
        selected through visible callable/codec descriptors rather than parser
        primitives. Encoding failure is `throw`/`catch`, not a union return.

    27. stop / exit / send / reply / await / schedule / thread
        `stop:` is an IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 statement receiver. Its admitted forms
        are `stop:` and `stop: Status`; the optional expression is evaluated at
        the call site and converted to `int`, with omitted status meaning zero.
        It requests cooperative termination of the explicitly executing Message
        Thread only: RUNNING becomes STOPPING, but `stop:` does not itself leave
        the current fn/sub/block or skip its cleanup. The first accepted ordinary
        stop request retains its status; a later fatal diagnostic/runtime failure
        may override that ordinary stop status. The final turn runs local
        collection and then becomes STOPPED.

        `thread:` is an IMPLEMENTED top-level L2/L3 source receiver. Its current
        form is:

            thread: HandlerName (IncomingMessage: message)
                route: "/absolute/local/route"
                handler statements
            end: HandlerName

        The handler has exactly one named parameter with exact descriptor
        `IncomingMessage`; direct source calls of the handler are rejected.
        `route:` is required compile-time metadata, not an executable statement.
        The route is a double-quoted printable-ASCII absolute path beginning
        with `/` and containing no backslash, query or fragment. Duplicate
        routes in one application translation are rejected. A handler may be
        declared directly or supplied by ordinary `import:`. Executable thread
        declarations in `predef:` modules and conditional `os`/`ifdef`/`guard`
        branches are rejected by the current startup profile.

        Each declared handler is materialized as one mailbox Message Thread.
        One invocation handles the one LMX document dequeued for that turn.
        `IncomingMessage.lmx` is a borrowed `const char *` view and
        `IncomingMessage.length` is its byte length; both remain valid only for
        the handler turn. The generated application entry always constructs a
        runtime and attaches its root; when at least one handler is declared it
        also constructs a pool, starts every handler and binds its route.
        Teardown drains cascaded committed work before stopping and joining the
        handlers.

        `send:` is an IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 statement receiver in these bootstrap
        forms:

            send: Route LmxBytes Length
            send: Endpoint Route LmxBytes Length

        The arguments are evaluated exactly once, from left to right. `Route`,
        `Endpoint` and `LmxBytes` lower to byte-addressable C strings in the
        current hosted profile; `Length` is converted to `size_t`. The first
        form uses the current application endpoint. The second admits only an
        absolute HTTP(S) endpoint. A send copies the complete LMX document into
        the current Message Thread's FIFO outbox. It is committed at the common
        end-turn boundary and does not perform a hidden return. An immediate
        address error or a commit-time delivery error requests failure of the
        sending Message Thread.

        The explicit address operands are temporary bootstrap routing
        projections of the envelope's `to.endpoint` and `to.route`; they are not
        a second address model. `LmxBytes` must still contain the complete
        standard message envelope. Until envelope parsing is inserted, the
        caller is responsible for making the explicit projection agree with
        `to`. The validation stage must eventually reject a mismatch.

        Local sends resolve `Route` in the runtime route table. A missing route
        fails with `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_ROUTE_NOT_FOUND` (64). An HTTP(S) send is
        retained as a remote address but, until the REST/LMX provider is
        configured, fails with
        `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_TRANSPORT_PROVIDER_NOT_CONFIGURED` (65). Invalid
        address syntax fails with `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_INVALID_ADDRESS` (66).
        A configured provider reports a native transport failure as
        `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_TRANSPORT_FAILED` (67), a syntactically valid HTTP
        response which does not admit the message as
        `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_HTTP_REJECTED` (68), and an invalid provider result
        as `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_ERROR` (69). HTTP 2xx admits
        the transport delivery; HTTP 404 maps to the same route-not-found
        status 64 as local lookup. Raw HTTP status numbers never become actor or
        process exit statuses.

        `exit:` is an IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 application receiver:

            exit:
            exit: StatusExpression

        The omitted status is zero; the optional expression is evaluated
        exactly once and converted to `int`. The first accepted application
        exit request selects the ordinary exit-request status. `exit:` is
        cooperative and does not perform a hidden return, jump or lexical unwind: the
        current fn/sub/handler continues through its visible source control flow
        to the common turn boundary. It is available wherever the current
        MessageThread context is explicitly propagated, including managed
        nested functions, but is rejected in an unmanaged external function.

        Request and shutdown are separate phases so an actor never joins
        itself. The requesting Message Thread becomes STOPPING, finishes its
        current outbox and collection boundary, and only then publishes the
        application exit as ready. A delivery or collector failure in that
        requester replaces the requested ordinary status with the requester's
        effective failure status. Once ready, the application controller stops
        the pool and root, waits for declared actors/workers, releases the pool,
        detaches/releases the root and deletes the runtime before returning to
        the host. An actor callback only requests this sequence; it never runs
        join/delete itself.

        Host-process status keeps the existing failure-preserving priority:
        a nonzero effective root status wins; otherwise a nonzero selected
        application exit status wins; otherwise the first nonzero declared
        actor status wins; cleanup failure supplies `1` only if all earlier
        choices were zero. Thus `stop: 3` followed in the same root turn by the
        first `exit: 5` records exit status `5` without rewriting the root's
        already selected status `3`, and the host returns `3`.

        The requester outbox is committed before the shutdown cut. Deliveries
        from other turns are linearized by the runtime mutex: entries admitted
        before the cut follow normal FIFO delivery, while entries that reach
        transport after the cut are cancelled as shutdown work without creating
        a spurious route/transport failure. The current generated application
        has one runtime and at most one application pool. Coordination of
        several independently constructed low-level runtimes/pools remains the
        responsibility of their explicit host controller.

        `reply`, `await` and `schedule` are NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers.
        Proposed contracts: `reply` sends the declared result or, on `throw`,
        a failure message with the same name and payload; `await(Future) ->
        Value` with `throws: Failure`; and `schedule(Message, Time)`.

        The explicit host runtime, worker-pool and actor start/join ABI is
        IMPLEMENTED. Its mailbox subset includes mailbox actor start, local
        route binding, owned-LMX FIFO inbox/outbox delivery and current-message
        access during a handler turn. `thread:` now exposes actor construction,
        binding and one-message-per-turn dispatch. Source `send:` now lowers
        over the same ABI.

    28. copy / move / borrow / own
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source operators; only lower-level ownership/runtime
        helpers exist. Proposed: resolve them through explicitly bound operation
        descriptors whose signatures accept the relevant references/arenas.
        High-level operations may be L2/L3; raw allocator/address operations
        remain L2-only.

    29. watch / derive
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: `watch(Query, Handler) -> Subscription` and
        `derive(Query, Rule) -> candidate rows/messages`. `reactive` is a
        profile/mode value, not a separate operator.

    30. service / route / HTTP adapter
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers. Proposed: `service:` materializes a
        service descriptor and binds it explicitly; `route` explicitly updates
        the route-map value it receives. HTTP remains a provider/transport
        adapter selected through a visible descriptor, not a core operator.

    31. authenticate / verify / authorize / ticket
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers. Proposed expressions:
        `authenticate(Credential, Verifier) -> AuthEvidence` with
        `throws: Failure`, `verify(Claim, Verifier) -> Evidence` with
        `throws: Failure`, and `authorize(Evidence, Operation) -> Allowed`
        with `throws: Failure`. Ticket creation
        and revocation operate on explicit Ticket Structures; `ticket` itself
        is not an operator.

Current bootstrap receiver/operator summary:

The following list contains exact spellings registered by the current L2
translator. It is an implementation baseline, not a claim that every later
profile family is already a core receiver.

    substrate receivers:   L1
    level receivers:       L2, L3
    definition receivers:  fn, callable, fm, sub
    control statements:    if, else, while, return, stop, break, continue,
                           synchronized, finally, assert
    declarations/values:   descriptor, const, (), [], None, OK, false, true
    module/source:         table, import, predef, join, merge
    machine/build receivers (intended L2-only; current L3 gates incomplete):
                           include, define, ifndef-default, os, ifdef, guard,
                           extern_c, c.struct, c.union, c.enum, c.array
    expression receivers:  cast: TargetDescriptor expression
                           implements(varA varB Consumer)
    modifier/trailers:     external; end, until
    prelude atom consumers:
                           include, define, os, ifdef, fn, layout, constant,
                           alias, forward, prototype, guard, extern_c, unit

The current `merge` handler consumes layout-describing sources, publishes a
composed descriptor under its explicit target name and emits that descriptor's
hosted type/layout projection. It never emits an implicit target object or
materializes a Structure instance; a value must be created separately through
an explicit constructor. It does not yet implement the generic high-level merge
contract. Current `join` still composes legacy source rows; it must become
ordinary Table-to-Table composition. `predef` is admitted in L3. Local-source early prepass still scans only implicit or
explicit L2 surfaces, although ordinary declaration can still materialize
table/join on an explicit L3 surface. Hoisted implicit-`.lm3` handling and `.lm3`
predef targets remain implementation work. These are implementation gaps, not
intended level restrictions. The built-in named-layout fallback remains
implementation debt; physical C layout is only a backend projection.
Named-Structure descriptor declarations and physical `merge:` declarations no
longer publish implicit owner or field Registry rows, instances or object
storage.

The exact `c.array` receiver is registered as an L2-only machine-storage
receiver and is rejected on an active L3 surface. Its nested bracket Frame is a
raw C declarator consumed by `c.array`; it is not dispatched as the ordinary
`[]` ArrayReference constructor.

The current expression operator tables register these exact families:

    prefix:   ++, --, +, -, @, \, !, ~
    postfix:  ++, --
    infix:    +, -, *, /, %, =, !=, <, <=, >, >=, &&, ||,
              &, |, ^, <<, >>, \
    index:    [, ]
    atoms:    ., None, OK, false, true

Tables and profile vocabulary are not source operators merely because they have
names. The current bootstrap currently uses the following Tables to construct
part of its initial namespace and backend configuration:

    class / class.kind / class.present
        legacy classification data; not core class semantics;
    method / requires.method
        legacy data relations; the `implements` receiver does not read
        them;
    namespace / receiver.*
        legacy input used to build explicit receiver bindings;
    operator.prefix / operator.postfix / operator.infix / operator.index
        expression spelling and emitter tables;
    serialization.codec / profile.validator
        codec and validation metadata tables.

Items marked TABLE/DATA above describe ordinary keys, table patterns, result
Structures, runtime roles or proposed profile vocabulary. They do not become
operators merely because they have names. The registered low-level spelling is
`c.union:`, not a generic `union:` receiver. `toLmx` currently appears only in
internal table-serialization helper names and `fromLmx` is absent; neither is a
registered operation-table method yet. A profile makes a proposed term
executable only by explicitly installing an exact receiver binding and its
validation/lowering descriptor.

The current bootstrap implements only unlabelled `break` and `continue`.
Labelled control may be added as a profile extension by registering ordinary
frames such as `outerLoop:` as control targets, but that mechanism is not part
of the implemented summary above.

19.1. `if` and `else`

`if` is a statement receiver. It consumes a condition expression and a statement
Structure for the true branch. `else` is a separate sibling statement receiver.
It may immediately follow an `if` at the same statement level and consumes the
false-branch statement Structure.

Semantic form:

    if(condition, body)
    else(body)?

Compact sibling form:

if: condition
    trueBody

else:
    falseBody

Explicit named-close form, useful for long blocks or diagnostics:

if: condition
    trueBody
end: if
else:
    falseBody
end: else

Nested form:

    if: condition
        trueBody

    else:
        falseBody


Single-line form:

    if: condition doSomething

The condition is evaluated once before branch selection.

Only the selected body is executed.

If no following sibling `else` receiver is provided and the condition is false, `if` completes normally without executing a body.

`else` is not parser syntax and not a reserved P0 word. It is an ordinary
receiver occurrence whose statement semantics are meaningful when it
appears as the following sibling of an `if` in the selected profile. If another
statement separates `if` and `else`, the pair is not adjacent; the target
projection or profile validator may reject the resulting control flow.

19.2. `match` and default-pattern receivers

`match` selects one body from a set of positional pattern/body pairs or from anonymous branch Structures. `case` is not a structural keyword.

Simple positional example:

match: value
    1
    print: one
    2
    print: two
    default
    print: other
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(value, 1, print(one), 2, print(two), default, print(other))

The `match` receiver may interpret the tail after `value` as repeating `(pattern, branch)` pairs. `default` is an ordinary atom/receiver-defined pattern value, not a parser keyword.

Block branch example:

match: value
    1
    ---
        print: one
        log: matched one
    ---
    2
    ---
        print: two
        log: matched two
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(
      value,
      1,
      (print(one), log(matched one)),
      2,
      (print(two), log(matched two))
    )

Anonymous branch Structure example:

match: value
    ---
        1
        print: one
    ---
        2
        print: two
    ---
        default
        print: other
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(
      value,
      (1, print(one)),
      (2, print(two)),
      (default, print(other))
    )

There is no automatic fallthrough from one branch to the next unless the active `match` receiver explicitly defines such behavior.

For union values, a `match` may destructure the active variant by ordinary pattern Structures.

Example:

match: shape
    ---
        Circle c
        drawCircle: c
    ---
        Square s
        drawSquare: s
    ---

If the matched value is a closed union and no default pattern is present, an implementation may require exhaustive patterns.

19.3. `while`

`while` is a pre-condition loop.

Semantic lowering shape:

    while(condition, statementStructure)

Example:

while: i < n
    print: i
    i: i + 1


The condition is evaluated before each iteration.

If the condition is false before the first iteration, the body is not executed.

A `while` body is a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

19.4. `until`

`until` is a conditional close for the current executable block. It closes the current block, executes that block as a post-condition loop body, then evaluates its condition.

Semantic form:

    until(currentStatementStructure, condition)

Labelled example:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

In this example, `until:` closes the labelled executable block `readLoop`. The
condition is the boolean-producing Structure `endOfInput(record)`. `readLoop`
also identifies the explicit control/callable Lmx occurrence for the block, as
described in section 7.6; it is not a runtime namespace binding.

The body is executed first. The condition is evaluated after the body.

The loop repeats until the condition is true. If the condition is false, control transfers to the beginning of the block closed by `until`.

`until` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

19.5. `for`

`for` is a controlled loop receiver for numeric ranges, counters, index ranges or other bounded iteration policies.

Compact counter/triplet form:

for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++
    print: i


The first three arguments are consumed as loop-control fields:

    init      -> initialization Structure
    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    step      -> iteration-step statement Structure

The vertical child Structure is consumed as the loop body.

Single-line compact body form:

    for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++ print(i)

In this form, `print(i)` is the fourth argument. The `for` receiver signature
consumes that fourth argument as its statement tail, so it lowers as the loop
body. The token form `i++` is valid only in profiles that register it as an
operator-token form.

Range-style example:

for: i 0 n
    print: i


Semantic requirement:

    `for` creates one or more loop-local Lmx iteration nodes.
    `for` advances those nodes according to the selected control policy.
    `for` executes the body once for each produced iteration state.

`for` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

?
The compact counter/triplet form is specified above. Other source forms for numeric ranges, inclusive/exclusive bounds, step values, reverse iteration, index/value pairs and destructuring of iteration bindings are not specified in this section.
?

variant 1:
Use compact positional forms such as `for(i, 0, n)`.

variant 2:
Use explicit short-tail fields such as `for: i 0 n 1`, or use an explicit
profile Structure only when the `for` receiver defines that graph shape.

variant 3:
Treat all range policies as ordinary receiver Structures consumed by `for`.

19.6. `each`

`each` iterates over a container or a Structure admitted by
`implements(source iteratorRequirement eachBody)` for the
requirement exposed by the resolved `each` receiver.

Array-like example:

each: item in items
    draw: item


Receiver-like example:

each: items item
    draw: item


Semantic requirement:

    `each` obtains a sequence of values from its source.
    For each value, `each` materializes or updates the iteration-variable Lmx
        nodes in its call/body Structure.
    `each` executes the body once for that value.

`each` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

For arrays or array-like containers, `each` may lower to an index loop.

After that used-path check, the `each` receiver separately consumes the
iterator's callable `next` occurrence. It invokes that entry as
`entry(next_callable->node)`; iterator state is an Lmx subtree reachable from
the containing Structure, not a separate `env` argument. `next` produces a
value by `yield` or ends
the sequence by `throw: Stop`. `each` catches `Stop` and finishes the loop.
That is the same `catch` rule as section 19.11. Exhaustion is not a `None`
element: `None` may be a valid yielded value. Any other `throw` from `next`
propagates unless the `each` body or caller has a matching `catch`. Failure
to consume `next` as a call is an ordinary receiver error, not another
compatibility relation.

In the source form:

    each: item in source

the `source` field is consumed by the `each` receiver as an iteration source. If
`source` resolves to a container, `each` iterates over that container. If its
