belongs to evaluation in step 2 because that operation itself requests a new
owned representation. A boundary copy or serialization may instead allocate in
step 4. The subsequent `return`, if any, still returns exactly the representation
which the explicit operation already produced.

For whole-branch transfer, step 4 changes ownership of an existing region and
invalidates sender-side edges. It need not change object identity.

Borrowed references into a destroyed owner are invalid after the cut/release.
A surviving owner may retain valid data only if:

    it already owned the data;
    the existing data owner remains alive;
    the existing region was detached/reparented/transferred consistently;
    an explicit copy/serialization operation created another representation;
    immutable/external sharing is allowed by the selected profile.

The runtime must avoid a window in which a collector sees a selected result as
unrooted. Publishing or reserving the result edge must occur before the source
owner becomes collectible.

In a precise future implementation, compiler/runtime metadata for a return may
be reserved in the caller/continuation frame before entering the callee. The
callee can then publish a reference without allocating on the return path.


19.29.14.9. Mandatory Message-Boundary Collection Policy

The default Message Thread collector scheduling policy is regular rather than
arbitrary.

In the full default Message Thread model, a local collection pass runs at
message boundaries.

One turn may include any of these events before reaching its single common
end-turn boundary:

    an incoming message payload has been copied, reconstructed by
        serialization, or ownership-transferred into a valid receiver owner;

    an outgoing message payload has been copied, serialized or transferred to
        the target queue, transport or target owner;

    a message handler completes normally;

    a message handler exits by `return`, `throw`, `retry`, `redo`, assertion
        diagnostic exit, cancellation or Message Thread termination;

    a reply/failure has been committed to its future, continuation or target
        message owner.

None of the listed events triggers an additional collection by itself. After
the turn has completed FIFO outbox publication or failed-turn rollback and
released its transient roots, the runtime runs exactly one local collection
pass before continuing ordinary Message processing.

The pass may be a cheap no-op when the local owner graph did not change.

It is local to the current Message Thread.

It does not stop other Message Threads.

It does not scan foreign arena graphs.

It does not require a global heap lock.

It does not perform global tracing collection.

It may inspect only the current Message's graph and one arena, registered
address-domain scanners, continuation roots, message-frame roots, explicit L2
pins/handles and implementation priority metadata.

The collector must preserve:

    owner validity;
    no-escaping-borrow;
    exact ordinary StructureReference/ArrayReference return identity;
    no hidden return allocation/copy/promotion;
    no raw mutable cross-Message-Thread references;
    copy/serialization/whole-branch-transfer boundary rules;
    valid external-resource ownership and cleanup ordering.

The regular processing pattern is one collection per turn, at end-turn:

    receive message
        -> join or activate (section 19.28.6)
        -> execute handler or root-body
        -> commit outbox
        -> turn cleanup
        -> run local collection pass
        -> next turn or stop

Concretely, both the generated direct-root `end_turn` path and every
pool-scheduled end-turn path run actor-local collection. A normal callback turn
increments both turn and collection counters. An actor stopped after it was
queued may skip entry execution but still crosses the final
collection/end-turn boundary before becoming STOPPED.

The collection path first invokes `lm_own_tree_cut(root_owner)` and then
`lm_own_arena_reclaim(root_owner)`. At present `lm_own_tree_cut` promotes
lazy-owned edges; reclaim marks registered roots/pins, conservatively scans
pointer-sized words and releases unmarked tracked allocations. This is a
non-moving bootstrap collector, not yet the precise address-domain-aware Lmx
tracer required by Revision 2, but it is no longer merely an end-turn no-op.

The next precise reclamation step is an address-domain-aware traversal of the
one Message arena. Turn and handler state remain ordinary Lmx nodes/roots in
that arena, not nested owner arenas. Once the turn's transient roots have been
released, the collector may reclaim their unreachable nodes/blocks while
preserving every published result and continuation root. This refinement must
not allocate, copy or promote an ordinary Structure or Array merely because
`return` publishes its reference.

A failed collection records collector failure and uses the failure-dominant
runtime request. It therefore cannot be hidden by an earlier successful
`stop:` or process-entry `return: 0`; the stopping turn retains a nonzero
failure status.


19.29.14.10. Long Message Thread Computations and Supervisor Termination

A Message Thread in the default profile is expected to remain message
responsive.

The default runtime is not required to interrupt arbitrary deep computation
inside one handler merely to run the local collector. If a Message Thread enters
a long computation and does not reach a message boundary or another declared
cooperative yield point within its watchdog limit, a supervisor may cancel,
fail, abort or destroy that Message Thread according to scheduling and
diagnostic policy.

This is intentional. Long computations which must coexist with message
scheduling and regular local collection should be expressed as stepwise or
resumable work.

Possible designs include:

    lazy receiver/operator evaluation;
    iterator or `yield`-style production;
    query/search receivers split into bounded steps;
    continuation messages to the same or another Message Thread;
    futures whose producers execute bounded work units;
    retryable frames with bounded attempts;
    explicit handler-level work slicing;
    profile-defined cooperative yield points.

The corresponding source/runtime operators such as `send`, `await`, `yield`,
`schedule` and `thread` have independent implementation status. The conceptual
memory model does not falsely mark an unimplemented source receiver as
available.

If an algorithm must execute as one long uninterrupted low-level operation, it
belongs to an explicit L2 execution profile with defined memory,
synchronization, cancellation and lifetime behavior. Such a worker is not
automatically a Message Thread merely because it runs on an OS thread. It may
be wrapped by a Message Thread facade which sends it commands and receives
typed results.

Therefore:

    default Message Thread
        message-driven, collected at boundaries, supervisable;

    long Message Thread algorithm
        split into lazy, stepwise, continuation or message-visible units;

    long uninterrupted machine algorithm
        explicit L2 worker/profile, normally hidden behind a high-level
        Structure/handle/message adapter for L3 clients.

Emergency memory pressure uses the same ownership principle. A supervisor may
reclaim memory by terminating low-priority or unresponsive Message Threads. The
memory reclaimed is the selected owner regions or whole selected thread graph,
not individual objects discovered through one process-wide tracing heap.


19.29.15. Future L2 Low-Level Threads, Shared Blocks and Explicit Release

This structural memory model does not yet define the complete source API for:

    standalone low-level execution threads;
    raw shared memory blocks;
    direct allocator calls;
    explicit `freemem`-style release;
    low-level cancellation and join;
    complete atomic/memory-order operations.

Portable host-thread, mutex and condition operations now exist as an
implementation/runtime ABI. This subsection remains future work for a generic
source-facing L2 machine-thread and shared-memory API. The host ABI is not
currently a `machine.thread:` or another public source receiver.

These are future L2 operations because their source semantics expose machine
storage, addresses, synchronization or allocator lifetime. L1 remains the
external implementation/lowering substrate and is not a Lingvamyxa source
level. L3 may use checked wrappers but does not directly expose these machine
operations.

The current hosted Message implementation already realizes a canonical
actor-local ownership domain and the non-overlapping-turn boundary. Its
additional sibling `LmOwnArena` shells are migration-era physical allocation
regions and must converge on, or be observed as, blocks/ranges of one logical
Message arena. The physical end-turn collector and the
join/activate/serialize algorithms remain implementation/current-profile
details. Revision 2 requires that every source-visible value remain an Lmx
node/link in its Message arena, that cross-Message transfer be explicit, and
that no raw mutable pointer silently cross into storage owned by another
concurrently executing Message. The common collection point is fixed at
end-turn; result-transfer and delivery algorithms remain open inside that
boundary.

Mix is a Message-based graph in this same ontology. Its current Java
cursor/thread architecture is transitional and requires complete Message-style
refactoring; its locks and independently scheduled callbacks do not define a
second concurrency ontology or a portable synchronization algorithm.

A future L2 profile may define operations for:

    creating a standalone low-level execution thread;
    joining, cancelling or terminating that thread;
    allocating a raw memory block;
    allocating a shared memory block;
    mapping a file or external buffer;
    publishing shared storage;
    synchronizing with atomics, locks and barriers;
    defining memory-order rules;
    releasing raw/shared storage through a matching explicit operation;
    wrapping the resource in a checked Lmx handle node or subtree.

Conceptual future L2 sketch:

    machine.thread: workerThread workerEntry workerArgs
    mem: sharedBlock bytes shared(mutable)
    freemem: sharedBlock

This sketch is not normative current syntax. The corresponding operators are
NOT IMPLEMENTED unless an active L2 profile explicitly supplies their callable
occurrences/providers.

A low-level execution thread is not automatically a Message Thread. It may
expose raw addresses, shared mutable buffers and platform synchronization under
its explicit L2 profile. A running Message is the higher structural runtime
graph with explicit roots, exactly one arena, a FIFO inbox/outbox and one
non-overlapping execution lane. Only its mapping to host workers, fairness,
capacity/backpressure and physical arena/collector machinery remain profile
choices.

L3 code may see:

    a checked typed handle;
    an explicit description Structure;
    a capability;
    a service/message adapter;
    an automatically bounds-checked collection;

rather than the raw address, allocator or synchronization operation.

An explicit low-level `freemem` operation, when implemented, is not:

    a local Message Thread collection pass;
    an end-turn collection pass;
    an ordinary Structure/Array destructor;
    a message-boundary rule;
    a high-level `return` operation.

It is a low-level release primitive for memory allocated by the matching L2
allocation profile. Its validity requires a contract which prevents double
release, mismatched allocators, use after release and release while another raw
user remains active.

Future profile work must define at least:

    exact L2 source spellings;
    allocation ownership and matching release rules;
    shared-block publication rules;
    memory ordering and synchronization;
    thread start/join/cancel semantics;
    interaction with Message Thread wrapper handles;
    interaction with service and device adapters;
    diagnostics for double release and use after release;
    cross-level escape diagnostics;
    which operations are always L2-only;
    external-provider failure and cleanup behavior.

Until such a profile is specified and implemented, raw shared blocks and
standalone low-level threads remain L2/L1 implementation mechanisms, while
ordinary L2/L3 Message code communicates through Messages and Lmx nodes owned
by their one Message arena or shared only through explicitly immutable/foreign
domains under the selected explicit runtime policy.


19.29.16. Normative Summary and Implementation Status

The normative memory rules are:

    ordinary Structure
        exactly one `Lmx { node, len, data }` occurrence; no descriptor prefix,
        generated extension, name field or per-source C aggregate;

    containment
        `node` points to the unique parent; parent/child containment edges form
        a tree; other classified payload references may make the whole graph
        non-tree-shaped;

    payload class
        selected by the registered address domain containing `data`; no hidden
        source type tag is added to Lmx;

    names
        absent from Lmx; the sole mandatory node-name aid is the external
        `Lmx * -> ShortNameId` reverse index;

    lookup
        an existing Lmx pointer is followed directly; relative lookup starts at
        the supplied containing Structure; `node\field` follows the actual
        parent; arbitrary textual/absolute lookup requires an explicit root and
        walks the current tree;

    repeated names
        `name == [0]name`; `[0]` is the first matching child in forward
        source/result order and later occurrences are `[1]`, `[2]`, ...;

    method
        an ordinary callable Lmx occurrence plus a program-wide addressable,
        collision-free C entry; invoke exactly `entry(callable->node)`;

    source local / argument / result / body field
        Lmx nodes and graph links, never semantic C automatic variables;

    C temporaries
        implementation-only values permitted behind the Lmx boundary or by a
        later optimization with proof that graph mutation is unobservable;

    merge
        runtime fresh result root; copy each operand root's direct children and
        containment subtrees in operand order, then body children; allocate new
        occurrences, rewrite `node`, re-index names, preserve source operands
        and reuse immutable/callable payloads by domain policy;

    module/link
        compile explicit dependencies and make their method/provider entries
        available; do not scan a directory, eagerly execute merge, flatten a
        foreign Structure or instantiate every declaration before control
        reaches it;

    runtime namespace
        does not exist; compiler symbol tables are work data only and method
        call/return performs no namespace merge, push, pop or capture;

    descriptor / object / vtable
        no implicit runtime category; descriptor-like records are ordinary
        explicit Structures read only by explicit consumers;

    Array
        retains its source semantics, but exact Revision-2 payload/shape ABI is
        unresolved; this cannot restore a descriptor prefix or named C layout;

    Message
        a Message-ontology Lmx graph owning exactly one logical arena; every
        dynamic mutable ordinary Lmx node/payload of the Message belongs to it;

    L3 Thread
        a Message admitted for execution; it has FIFO inbox/outbox order and
        executes no more than one turn at once; distinct Messages may run in
        parallel; OS thread, worker-pool or cooperative-host mapping remains
        open;

    call result / failure / lifetime / collection
        result/failure ABI and physical collector algorithm remain open;
        allocation into the owning Message arena and local collection after
        FIFO publish-or-rollback at every end-turn are fixed and constrained by
        stable live Lmx identity, complete address-domain tracing and
        reverse-index consistency; no open detail may add another explicit
        source-method parameter or runtime namespace;

    Mix
        a Message-based graph in the common Message ontology;
        current Java cursor Threads, raw cell blobs and branch compaction are
        transitional mechanics requiring complete Message-style refactoring
        (section 19.29.2.1); common Mix coordination is FIFO Message interaction,
        not transplanted shared-memory locks/callbacks;

    L2
        may expose low-level machine allocation, raw storage, addresses,
        synchronization and explicit release;

    L3
        excludes those low-level machine operations but is not a sandbox,
        authority or purity level;

    Registry / Table
        ordinary explicit Lmx data and operations, never hidden name/type state;

    ANSI C storage duration and linkage
        backend details that must implement stable Lmx identity and
        program-wide callable reachability rather than define source ontology;

    L1
        external implementation/lowering substrate, not a Lingvamyxa source
        level.

Historical/current hosted-bootstrap implementation (non-normative Revision-1
status; useful only as a migration inventory):

    `libown`, `LmOwnArena` and actor-local owner graphs exist;
    `LmMessageThread` state and RUNNING / STOPPING / STOPPED transitions exist;
    generated standalone programs create an application runtime, attach an
        explicit unpooled root actor and drive its external process-entry turn
        loop directly;
    no process-global current/root Message Thread or TLS scheduler binding exists;
    portable pthread, Win32 and `single` host providers exist;
    `LmMessageThreadRuntime`, N-worker pools and FIFO actor scheduling exist;
    native pools execute different actors concurrently while serializing turns
        of each individual actor;
    an actor may migrate between host workers without moving or sharing its arena;
    `single` performs the same actor scheduling cooperatively without OS workers;
    pool-owned actor start, self-stop/failure, controller join, pool stop and
        lifecycle destruction exist;
    every scheduled end-turn performs actor-local collection;
    `stop:` is an implemented L2/L3 cooperative actor receiver;
    process-entry `return` requests root-loop stop after generated cleanup;
    ordinary stop is first-request-wins, while diagnostic/runtime collection
        failures use a failure-dominant status channel;
    explicitly constructed local and direct module-level named/anonymous
        Structures, `fm` result Structures, arena-backed local/module Array and
        runtime Table values, and closure environments/descriptors are
        root-arena managed;
    named-Structure and physical-`merge:` descriptor declarations emit only
        namespace descriptors and hosted type/layout projections, never object
        storage or arena allocations;
    direct module-level named Structure, anonymous `():`, ordinary `[]:` and
        unmarked runtime `table:` sites use a source-ordered initializer after
        root attachment and retain only private static reference slots;
    arena allocation, array allocation, descriptors, lazy edges, freeze,
        absorption, tree-cut and arena-reclaim helpers exist; scheduled
        collection invokes tree-cut followed by mark/reclaim at end-turn;
    root-arena destruction releases ordinary generated Structures, Array/Table
        objects/headers, Table rows and owned backing storage;
    partial-callable binder ownership and other remaining unscoped allocation
        paths still require separate owner integration;
    generated standalone programs construct a worker pool only when `thread:`
        handlers are present; the application runtime and attached root exist
        in either case;
    pooled mailbox actors, owned-LMX FIFO inbox/outbox queues and exact local
        route-to-MessageThread delivery are implemented;
    empty/NULL endpoint selects local delivery; a per-runtime REST/LMX provider
        ABI accepts normalized absolute HTTP(S) targets and complete binary LMX
        bodies, while an unconfigured runtime fails remote sends explicitly;
    the hosted outbound adapter implements that ABI with libcurl on supported
        Unix-like builds, WinHTTP on Windows, or an explicit `none` provider;
    the transport-neutral inbound admission ABI copies complete binary LMX
        bodies into route-selected FIFO inboxes, wakes idle actors and rejects
        new external work once application exit or route shutdown begins;
    the hosted inbound REST/LMX adapter implements byte-exact endpoint-base and
        route mapping with CivetWeb, or an explicit `none` provider; generated
        startup opens it after source routes and blocks in server stop before
        route, pool and runtime destruction;
    source `thread:` declarations create one route-bound mailbox actor and lower
        one dequeued LMX document to one `IncomingMessage` handler turn;
    source `send:` is implemented for local and explicitly addressed HTTP(S)
        forms, evaluates its arguments once in order and lowers through the
        transactional per-turn outbox ABI;
    root and actor outboxes roll back a failed turn; successful entries publish
        FIFO, and teardown drains cascaded local messages to pool quiescence;
    source `exit:` is implemented as a first-request-wins cooperative
        application shutdown; the requester completes its turn, the generated
        controller stops and joins the application pool, completes the root
        boundary and releases runtime resources before host return;
    the neutral remote-provider hook, delivery-result mapping, outbound HTTP
        client adapter, transport-neutral inbound admission and inbound HTTP
        server adapter are implemented; message parsing/validation, direct
        inbound TLS, reply routing and envelope/address
        consistency checking remain separate work;
    `reply`, `await` and `schedule` are not yet implemented as generic source
        operations; shutdown across several manually constructed runtimes or
        pools is not coordinated by the current source profile;
    a runtime frame/cleanup stack for unwinding through nested Message Thread
        calls is not yet implemented;
    a distinct disposable per-turn/handler arena is not yet implemented;
    current collection first uses tree cutting to promote lazy-owned edges and
        then `lm_own_arena_reclaim` to mark registered roots/pins and release
        unmarked tracked allocations;
    precise address-domain-aware Lmx tracing and optional compaction are not yet
        complete.

Proposed but NOT IMPLEMENTED as generic source operations:

    copy;
    move;
    borrow;
    own;
    reply;
    await;
    schedule;
    general low-level freemem/thread/shared-block profile.

Conceptual future runtime material in this section describes required semantics
and possible lowering shapes. It must not be confused with already registered
source operators or with the current C ABI.


19.30. Explicit Reactive Registry and Query Receivers

19.30.1. Reactive Layer

An explicitly held Registry value may be updated by an operation that also
produces events. Events are messages. Query receivers run inside Message Threads
and receive the Registry/Table to query as an explicit operand.

19.30.2. Agent Candidates

An agent receiver may propose candidate rows or messages. A validator must
accept, reject or rank the candidate before an explicit operation updates the
    target Registry/Table. No runtime namespace exists or is created by this
    operation.

19.30.3. Boundaries and Bootstrap

Reactive Registry processing obeys Message Thread memory/message boundaries. A
minimal bootstrap may implement only:

    row inserted;
    query receiver;
    candidate row;
    validation;
    accepted row.


19.31. HTTP, Service and Transport Operators

19.31.1. Status

HTTP Lingvamyxa REST is a built-in service transport profile. It is not the
core execution model.

19.31.2. Service Instance

A service instance is a bundle of Message Threads connected by standard messages.
The bundle is not a shared mutable heap.

19.31.3. HTTP to Message

HTTP request handling is:

    HTTP request
        -> route table lookup
        -> IncomingMessage
        -> receiver validation and explicit policy combination
        -> executor
        -> result/failure message
        -> HTTP response mapping

The route map is an ordinary explicitly configured value, commonly a Table.
External HTTP data becomes an LMX message before service dispatch.

The standard request target is the normalized `MessageAddress.endpoint +
MessageAddress.route`. A remote sender posts the complete LMX Message document
as the request body. The receiving application resolves only the route part in
its local route table. An HTTP route never names an OS worker or exposes a raw
MessageThread handle.

The core runtime does not own an HTTP client singleton. Its per-runtime
REST/LMX provider receives the already normalized request URI and an exact
borrowed byte span, performs one POST with media type `application/lmx`, and
returns either an HTTP admission status or a native transport failure. This
keeps the message address, outbox and shutdown semantics identical for a test
provider, libcurl, WinHTTP or a later transport implementation.

19.31.4. Serialization, Client/Server and Service Boundaries

Serialization adapters are callable Lmx occurrences reached through an explicit
link or structural path and may be configured by explicitly supplied Tables.
HTTP client/server adapters are services or hosted native adapters that inject
messages into Message Threads. No codec is selected from an ambient namespace.
Service boundaries obey the same memory and authority rules as Message Thread
boundaries, with stronger default isolation for remote input.

The initial REST/LMX media type is `application/lmx`. HTTP status and response
mapping describe transport admission and, in a request/response profile, may
carry the resulting LMX reply. An asynchronous profile may acknowledge inbox
admission first and later deliver the semantic reply to `replyTo`; in both cases
the semantic result/failure is an ordinary LMX Message with `correlation` equal
to the request `id`.


19.32. Authentication and Authorization Operators

19.32.1. Status

Authentication and authorization are not a separate role system. They are
explicit verifier/policy values, validators and evidence attached to a message
flow. Tables are one optional representation.

19.32.2. Primary Credential and Verifier

A verifier is a protected Structure/value that can validate a primary
credential without storing the credential itself.

The default password verifier may use libsodium password hashing in the C99
runtime profile.

19.32.3. Default Installation Credential

A fresh installation may have a system user name and an empty primary password.
This is not global security policy. It is explicit initial verifier state. A
user or administrator may replace it with a stronger verifier.

19.32.4. Verifier Realms and Protected Objects

Protected objects may have their own verifier realm:

    protected module;
    protected service;
    protected archive;
    protected directory;
    protected administration operation.

Password reset may reset an access verifier to an empty/default state. It does
not recover separately encrypted content whose key depended on the old verifier.

A protected object may require:

    one verifier;
    multiple verifiers for one user;
    a quorum of users/sessions;
    creator-parent tracking.

Parent/creator rows do not grant content rights by themselves.

19.32.5. Delegated Ticket

After primary credential validation, the runtime may issue a delegated ticket
for a module or service. A profile may represent ticket/evidence data in a Table:

    ticket[user][module] = ticket_id
    ticketAuthority[ticket_id][operation] = allowed
    ticketExpiry[ticket_id] = time

Changing or resetting the primary verifier invalidates dependent tickets by its
explicit policy Structure.

19.32.6. External Authentication Modules

OAuth or OS-login modules are ordinary L2/L3 modules. How they obtain authority
is defined by the selected authentication/authorization profile, not by their
source level. They do not replace the explicitly configured verifier. They may request or
receive delegated tickets after the primary policy allows it.

19.32.7. Authentication Evidence Rows

Authentication validation produces an explicit `AuthEvidence` Lmx node or
subtree. Its fields/rows may include:

    authUser[flow] = user
    authVerifier[flow] = verifierRealm
    authTicket[flow] = ticketId
    authority[flow][operation] = allowed
    guarantee[flow] = sessionQuorumSatisfied

Message validators receive that evidence reference explicitly and may require
these fields/rows before producing a ConcreteExpression or ExecutionPlan. No
ambient evidence table is implied.

19.32.8. ANSI C / libsodium Runtime Sketch

A C99 runtime profile may use libsodium for:

    password hashing/verifier creation;
    random ticket material;
    constant-time comparisons;
    secure memory helpers where the runtime owns the memory region.

UI password entry is outside libsodium's control unless the UI layer is also
implemented with memory handling that the runtime owns.

19.32.9. Standard Statement

Authentication is a validation/evidence layer over the message pipeline.

    credential/verifier -> validator -> authority evidence -> message validation

Authorization is an explicit operation over that evidence and policy. The
operation may query a supplied Table; it is not a global role hierarchy.


19.33. Operator and Runtime Summary

This integrated model reduces to these rules:

    Value = Lmx reference;
    Lmx = exactly `{ Lmx *node; size_t len; void *data; }`;
    containment = ordered child occurrences plus reverse parent link `node`;
    complete graph = containment tree plus classified non-containment links;
    physical payload class = registered address domain containing `data`;
    node name metadata = sole external reverse index `Lmx * -> ShortNameId`;
    runtime namespace = absent;
    ordinary `name` lookup = first matching child, exactly `[0]name`;
    direct resolved reference = followed directly without textual re-resolution;
    relative lookup = starts at the supplied containing Structure;
    absolute/arbitrary textual lookup = explicit root plus current tree walk;
    explicit `node\field` = follow the actual parent link, then its first
                  matching child;
    source declaration/local/argument/result = Lmx nodes and links;
    source method = callable Lmx occurrence plus collision-free program-wide
                  addressable C entry;
    method invocation = `entry(callable->node)` with exactly one explicit Lmx
                  parameter; exact result/failure ABI remains open;
    construction = execution-time generic Lmx builder operation;
    merge = execution-time fresh-root ordered containment copy; operand roots
                  are not inserted, their direct children are copied; copied
                  `node` links and ShortNameId mappings are rebuilt;
    descriptor/class/object/vtable = no implicit source/runtime category;
                  description-like Structures are ordinary explicit data;
    table / Registry / RegistryView = optional ordinary Lmx data;
    table name / key / row / cell = data, never an implicit runtime binding;
    table query / join / overlay = explicit operations over explicit operands;
    expression = Structure whose already linked callable/receiver occurrences
                  are consumed through structural traversal;
    provider = explicit Lmx reference/provider operation, optionally configured
                  by ordinary Tables;
    ANSI C layout/storage/linkage = backend mechanisms constrained by Lmx
                  identity and method reachability, never source-semantic type
                  evidence;
    message = one LMX document with a standard envelope and ordinary LMX expression;
    message address = optional REST/LMX application endpoint plus required inbox route;
    message id = opaque identity of one message;
    replyTo = optional result/failure MessageAddress;
    correlation = relationship to another message id, never a destination;
    message expression = ordinary LMX admitted by receiver policy;
    validation = explicit operation from IncomingMessage Structure to an
                  ExecutionPlan; do not confuse its policy combination with
                  runtime Structure `merge`;
    executor = run only ExecutionPlans produced by validation and explicit
                  policy combination; this is not runtime Structure `merge`;
    autonomous/constrained profiles = explicit tables for budgets, freshness,
                uncertainty, idempotence/replay, effect fences, degradation and
                decision logs;
    L3 retry/retryable = local execution-frame restart, not transport resend;
    generator = create proposed messages from result/state/table rules;
    L2/L3 return = executable-body control operation whose exact generic Lmx
                  result/failure ABI remains to be selected;
    message-boundary reply = boundary wrapper from result/failure to ordinary message record;
    Message Thread = Message/Lmx execution graph with explicit roots,
                  exactly one owning arena, FIFO inbox/outbox, one active turn
                  at a time and no runtime source namespace; Registry/Table
                  state is optional ordinary data;
    Message scheduling = same-Message FIFO order and non-overlapping turns are
                  Revision-2 semantics; zero cooperative or N native workers,
                  worker migration and fairness between distinct Messages are
                  host/profile decisions;
    host thread provider = current hosted compile-time backend
                  `auto | pthread | win32 | single`, not a mandatory scheduler;
    stop / exit = current hosted cooperative lifecycle operations; their final
                  Message-style semantics must be re-derived for the selected
                  scheduler and lifetime profile;
    Mix = target Message-based graph in this same Message ontology; its current
                  Java procedural/multithreaded implementation is transitional
                  and requires a complete Message-style refactoring;
    memory/auth/service policy = explicit Lmx values consumed by explicitly
                  reached operations.

20. L2 Primitive, Address and Parser Operators

20.0. Scope of This Section

This section defines the minimal L2 operator/parser-form set.

L2 has the common `Value = Lmx reference` ontology plus explicit low-level
machine payload domains. Explicit machine storage/allocation forms produce Lmx
leaves whose `data` points into a typed-address or foreign-handle domain.
Receiver operations and parser profiles assign final semantics to Structures
built from source syntax.

The minimal L2 profile therefore has no ordinary Structure syntax for C-style
direct aggregate value access. Source primitive/scalar values are still Lmx
leaves; after address-domain classification a backend may operate directly on
their payload only as a semantics-preserving implementation step. Explicit
machine aggregate storage is reached by an explicit handle-domain Lmx leaf,
while every ordinary Structure expression yields an Lmx reference. The exact
ordinary Array domain remains open. This keeps
`u32: x` simple and prevents an embedded record from
silently becoming a copyable whole-Structure expression.

This section specifies the L2-specific low-level machine operator/parser-form
surface. Its machine forms are not part of L3; ordinary shared forms mentioned
for contrast retain their separately specified L2/L3 availability. In this
sense L3 is smaller than L2: L3 keeps the
high-level algorithmic/control, data/query and runtime surface and excludes raw
storage, address, physical layout-storage and unchecked access. A parser form
from this section is L2-only only when its source semantics expose such machine
state; parser involvement by itself is not a level boundary.

20.1. Pointer and Field Syntax Exclusions

L2 does not use `*`, `&`, `^`, `.`, or `->` as pointer/field syntax.

This rule should be stated once as an L2 invariant instead of repeated at every address example.

Consequences:

    `*`  remains available as a multiplication operator token.
    `&`  is not address-of.
    `^`  is not pointer dereference and is not a field-follow marker.
    `.`  is source indentation only when it appears in the leading dot prefix; it is not field access.
    `->` is not a Lingvamyxa field-follow operator.

L2 address and field forms are:

    @ⁿ: T x     reserved address-depth declaration head, for example `@@@: int ppp`
    @x          address of storage location `x`
    \x          raw load from address expression `x`
    x\field     typed field-follow through address/reference `x`
    x\[n]field  zero-based exact-name occurrence field-follow through `x`
    x[i]        indexed access through address/reference/container `x`

Field-follow and index chains bind tighter than prefix `@` and prefix `\`.

The C-profile mnemonic table in section 11.3 shows how the L2 forms cover the C declarator/expression roles of `*`, `&` and `->` without using those tokens as Lingvamyxa pointer/field syntax.

20.2. Primitive, Array, Address and Indexed Forms

The ordinary `[]:` family constructs an array value. It is not an ANSI C array
declaration and does not select stack, heap or static storage. Its surface forms
and rectangular/index semantics below remain language requirements; the exact
Revision-2 Lmx payload, shape and backing ABI is unresolved.

    Primitive: name [initialValue]
    []: ElementType name size [initialValue...]
    []...[]: ElementType name size1 ... sizeN [initialValue...]
    [size1]...[sizeN]: ElementType: name [initialValue...]
    @ⁿ: BaseType name

Examples:

    int: count 5
    []: int leafs count
    @: int p
    @@: int pp
    @@@: int ppp

The `leafs` constructor materializes an array occurrence and initializes the
requested elements according to the selected Array domain. Repeated bracket
groups specify rank and dimensions. Both empty and payload-bearing bracket
groups are accepted as described by the parser profile; missing dimensions are
a diagnostic. The domain must define how the array is reached from Lmx and how
`node`, `len`, shape, merge/copy and collection apply.

The leading `@` fields before an Array element descriptor specify an admitted
low-level address-depth element form. `@ⁿ:` itself produces the explicit L2
typed-address primitive described by the selected low-level profile; it is not
an ArrayReference.

Indexing an ordinary array uses Array semantics and explicit runtime bounds
metadata. Indexing a
raw typed address is a separate L2 low-level operation. If a backend needs raw
fixed C-array storage, it must expose a distinct explicit L2 machine-storage
operation and must not assign that meaning to ordinary `[]:`.

The distinct hosted C99 machine-storage receiver is `c.array`. It has exactly
one logical child, in one of these two forms:

    c.array: ArrayDeclaratorFrame
    c.array: const: ArrayDeclaratorFrame

`ArrayDeclaratorFrame` must have a head made only of one or more bracket groups.
The optional `const` Frame must itself contain exactly that one bracket Frame.
No other child, sibling declaration or qualifier is admitted by the minimal
receiver. Examples:

    c.array: [3]: int: top_values 2 4 6
    c.array:
        [][]: int matrix 2 2
            []: 1 2
            []: 3 4
    ---
    c.array: const: [2]: int: fixed_values 9 11
    c.array: [rows][columns]: int: matrix

The array rank is the number of bracket groups and must be at least one. A
non-empty group such as `[rows]` supplies its own extent expression. Each empty
group consumes exactly one following declarator field after the element type
and binding name, in bracket order. After all empty groups have consumed their
extents, the remaining fields are initializer clauses. A missing extent is a
`c.array` receiver diagnostic; an initializer is never used to infer a missing
extent. An extent supplied after an empty group must be a single atom in the
minimal hosted profile; a compound extent expression is written inside a
non-empty bracket group. Empty and non-empty groups may be mixed.

The element type uses the existing L2 C-declarator type forms, including an
admitted address-depth type for pointer elements. The optional `const` wrapper
has the same C-profile meaning as other declaration composition: it qualifies
the base element type. It does not turn the declaration into an ordinary
immutable ArrayReference.

`c.array` declares target-machine C array storage. It allocates no Array
object/header, arena backing, owner edge, length/shape/stride record or bounds
metadata. The storage is not a Lingvamyxa Value and the declared name is a
machine-storage designator, not an ArrayReference. Where the hosted C expression
profile admits array-to-pointer conversion, consuming the designator produces
the corresponding typed-address Primitive. Its address depth is determined by
the element's admitted address depth plus the one remaining array dimension:
indexing may expose a rank-one subarray for decay, while indexing every array
dimension exposes the element itself. A designator with more than one remaining
dimension is not silently flattened to an unrelated pointer type. Conversion to
or from an ordinary ArrayReference requires an explicit operation which states
the raw backing address, shape and lifetime contract.

At block scope the declaration has C automatic storage duration. Runtime extent
expressions select the admitted C99 variable-length-array rules; a VLA cannot
have an initializer. At module/top level the hosted lowering emits internal-linkage
`static` storage, requires constant extents and applies the C99 requirements for
static initializers. With no initializer, automatic elements are indeterminate
and static elements are zero-initialized. A present initializer follows C99
aggregate initialization. In the conservative hosted bootstrap, a
multidimensional initializer must spell each nested dimension with an explicit
`[]:` or Structure brace group; flat brace-elided scalar clauses are rejected.
Omitted elements are zero-initialized and excess or incompatible initializers
are diagnostics. The conservative hosted bootstrap additionally
requires a top-level address initializer to name storage whose declaration has
already been emitted; a source-forward machine-storage initializer is rejected
rather than reordered behind its dependency.

A block-scope `c.array` has the ordinary lifetime of its C automatic storage.
Storing its address in an Lmx graph does not extend that raw C lifetime. Until
an explicit adapter or raw-lifetime operation exists, the hosted backend
rejects escape of this raw storage instead of emitting a dangling pointer.
Ordinary anonymous Structures and ordinary arrays are Lmx nodes governed by
the selected Revision-2 runtime lifetime policy; their lifetime must not be
inferred from the automatic-storage rule of `c.array`.

Postfix indexing dispatches from the binding category. For `c.array`, `a[i]` is
unchecked raw C-array indexing and does not use the checked ArrayReference
operation. One bracket suffix consumes one dimension, so multidimensional raw
storage uses `a[i][j]`, not an ArrayReference multi-coordinate shorthand. A
partial index denotes the remaining raw subarray; a full-rank index denotes an
element lvalue and may be read, addressed or used as an update head. No view is
created and no bounds check is synthesized. Statically known dangerous accesses
remain subject to the selected C99 undefined-behavior policy.

Taking the address of a whole or partially indexed C array produces a C
pointer-to-array type rather than another ordinary typed-address depth. The
minimal hosted profile does not yet expose pointer-to-array declarators as a
Lingvamyxa type and rejects that expression instead of misclassifying it as
`T **`; address-of remains admitted after all array dimensions have been
indexed to an element.

The `const` wrapper qualifies the base element, following the declarator rule
above. For an array of pointers, the pointer slot itself remains assignable while
an update that traverses the declared pointer depth and reaches the const base
element is rejected.

The hosted bootstrap provides this exact `c.array` receiver. Intentional raw
arrays in production bootstrap code and low-level fixtures use `c.array`,
including buffers that require C array-to-pointer decay, `sizeof(array)` or a
raw-pointer ABI. `c.array` remains C storage and is not affected by ordinary
Array semantics.

20.2.H. Historical Hosted `LmSlice` Array Projection

The following `ArrayReference`, `LmSlice`, root-arena, closure-capture and
private-static-slot description records the Revision-1 hosted implementation.
It is non-normative for the Revision-2 Array ABI and must not be used to add a
descriptor prefix, hidden method parameter, namespace binding or per-name C
layout to ordinary arrays.

In the current hosted migration stage, an admitted ordinary `[]:` declaration
inside a managed function or directly on an executable module surface
materializes a persistent `LmSlice` Array header plus arena-owned backing
storage in the executing Message Thread's root owner arena (the generated root
actor's arena at process entry). A direct module site uses the source-ordered
initializer of section 19.29.12.2.1. This applies to both trailing extents and
payload-bearing bracket groups and to repeated declarations. Extent expressions
are evaluated exactly once in source order. The current bootstrap admits
statically typed integer extents in the non-negative `long long` range;
malformed or out-of-range C99 integer literals are rejected before C emission,
and a negative runtime result enters the hosted construction-failure path before
any backing allocation. Untyped `c.*` atoms and calls are not admitted as
ArrayReference extents in this stage, except for the dedicated `c.sizeof(...)`
expression form. Each repeated binding receives a distinct header, and any backing
allocations created for it are distinct rather than shared. Backing storage is
initially zero, after which present initializer elements are assigned in source
order; a multidimensional initializer uses explicit nested groups in this
conservative bootstrap. The hosted header currently stores only `ptr` and the
first-dimension `length`. The translator separately tracks binding-local rank,
element type/address depth and constness; when statically known, it checks them
at typed argument, return and whole-reference-assignment boundaries. Passing,
returning or assigning the ArrayReference publishes the existing header and
does not copy its backing. Prefix `@` applied to the binding addresses the
ArrayReference slot, while `arrayReference\ptr` remains the explicit raw-backing
bridge described below.

At a direct module/top-level site this stage retains only a private static
`LmSlice *` reference slot; the header and backing are root-arena allocations.
The hosted translator rejects such a site when the complete executable has no
unique non-imported root `main`, or when the site is conditionally emitted, as
specified in section 19.29.12.2.1. It also rejects ordinary Array construction
in an external function that has no managed Message Thread context. The current
unchecked indexing projection accepts only an unquoted direct ArrayReference
binding followed by enough immediate bracket suffixes to consume every rank. An
exact-quoted or parenthesized reference or a callable result must first be
rebound to an admitted unquoted ArrayReference binding before a complete index
chain is used. A chain that consumes fewer ranks is rejected until partial-index
Array views are implemented.
Capturing an ordinary ArrayReference through a closure environment, runtime
element descriptors, complete runtime rank/shape/stride/storage metadata in the
Array header, runtime bounds checking for ArrayReference indexing at every rank,
complete multidimensional shape checking and partial-index Array views remain
pending hosted work. Allocation descriptors record owner, count, rank and level
for current backing allocations, but that implementation metadata does not by
itself complete the source-level view contract.

The current hosted validator deliberately accepts a conservative subset of C
constant expressions before emitting top-level storage or an initialized VLA.
Literal/operator expressions and unresolved C macro or enum atoms are admitted;
known runtime bindings, call Frames and call-shaped `c.*(...)` atoms are
rejected. Cast and `sizeof` constant-expression classification is pending a
full C-expression classifier and may therefore be rejected even when a C99
compiler could prove the expression constant. This is a hosted limitation, not
a change to the C99 constant-expression rules above.

Until an explicit raw-storage/ArrayReference adapter receiver is registered,
the hosted translator conservatively rejects a typed boundary expression that
directly contains a `c.array` designator where an ArrayReference is required,
or an ArrayReference where a raw pointer is required. This includes casts and
compound expressions whose result category is not proven independently of their
root storage. For a direct `c.array` chain the hosted classifier tracks declared
rank, consumed postfix indices and element address depth: a rank-one remaining
subarray may decay to its corresponding raw address, and a full-rank index is an
element rather than the whole designator. Partial-array update heads remain
invalid because C arrays are not assignable. It must not emit an incompatible
pointer conversion and delegate the distinction to the C compiler.

The existing hosted spelling `arrayReference\ptr` (and a cast wrapped directly
around that spelling) is the current explicit backing-address bridge. It yields
only the raw data address; it does not carry length, rank or lifetime, and the
consumer is responsible for the missing contract. It is retained for bootstrap
compatibility until the dedicated adapter receiver replaces it.

Names that contain spaces use exact quoted identifiers as ordinary fields:

@: int `second slot`

This declares an address-depth-1 primitive slot with base type `int` and
introduces the exact quoted name `second slot`.

The same short-tail whitespace rule applies to address-depth declarations:

@@@: int ppp

This declares `ppp` as an address-depth-3 slot with base type `int`. The spelling `@@@: int, ppp` is the same declaration with an explicit comma field separator. The spelling `@@@: int; ppp` is different: the semicolon terminates the `@@@:` short form after `int`, so `ppp` is no longer the binding-name field of that declaration. The following `ppp` is parsed as the next source item at the current level.

20.2.1. Revision-2 C Lowering of Structures and Methods

Ordinary Lingvamyxa Structures never acquire a source-name-specific ANSI C
layout. Every runtime occurrence uses the exact common prefix/header:

    typedef struct Lmx Lmx;

    struct Lmx {
        Lmx *node;
        size_t len;
        void *data;
    };

The translator must not emit `typedef struct SourceName`, calculate
`offsetof(SourceName, field)`, represent a method as a C member, or use a
descriptor pointer to select a source layout. `data` is classified by registered
address domains; generic operations enumerate branch children, access primitive
pools, resolve callable payloads and handle explicit low-level storage.

Generated source methods are ordinary C functions emitted at translation-unit
level even when the source definition is nested. Every entry has a deterministic
program-wide unique identity; a long C name derived from the normalized source
path with path separators replaced by underscores is preferred. The build/link
profile must make every such entry addressable by any callable Lmx occurrence
in the linked/loaded program and prevent required entries from dead stripping.

The semantic generated-method input is fixed:

    method(callable->node)

That is exactly one `Lmx *`, the Structure containing the selected callable
occurrence. The C return/error convention is not yet fixed. Source arguments,
results and locals remain Lmx nodes; hidden `LmMessageThread *`, descriptor,
environment or one-C-parameter-per-source-argument ABIs are non-conforming for
generated source methods. Infrastructure wrappers may receive platform
contexts and then invoke the source method with the one required pointer.

Translation of a source Structure emits runtime construction/evaluation code,
not an already instantiated C aggregate. Translation of `merge` emits a generic
runtime merge call at the original execution site. A module may compile/link an
explicit dependency, but it does not inspect a whole directory or flatten the
dependency's Structure shape into the current C translation unit.

Ordinary field traversal lowers to generic Lmx operations:

    direct pointer                 -> use directly
    relative path                  -> start at supplied Structure
    node\field                     -> follow Lmx.node, then child lookup
    arbitrary textual absolute path -> require explicit root and traverse

Short-name comparison uses the sole `Lmx * -> ShortNameId` reverse index.
Full paths are reconstructed, not stored. A later optimizer may replace these
operations only after proving equivalent behaviour across mutation, runtime
merge, repeated occurrences, recursion and concurrency.

Raw L2 C constructs remain distinct. An explicit `c.struct`, `c.union`, foreign
function declaration or ABI record may and usually must emit the requested C
type/layout. Such a raw substrate artifact is not an ordinary Lingvamyxa
Structure and must not leak its representation into the universal Lmx model.

20.2.1.H. Revision-1 Namespace Descriptor/C Layout Projection (Historical)

The remainder of this subsection is non-normative hosted-translator history.
Its descriptor-to-typedef projection, declaration fingerprints and block-local
typedef replay may remain useful only for explicit raw L2 C artifacts. Applying
them to an ordinary Structure, merge result, callable, source local, Table row
or call context violates Revision 2.

At the parser level, a form such as `Name:` is a Structure/Frame and has no
intrinsic C-layout meaning. A structural descriptor affects resolution only
when an explicit declaration or import binds that descriptor in the namespace.

Constructing an instance resolves its descriptor, allocates one Structure in
the current arena and returns/binds a StructureReference. At module level the
current arena is the module or root arena. Construction does not create
descriptor rows, `layout.backend` facts, field tables or other Registry data.

A backend may derive a physical layout from a namespace-visible descriptor or
from a compile-time-known Structure shape. It may emit an ANSI C `typedef
struct`, accessors or internal layout metadata. These artifacts do not mutate
the namespace and are not Lingvamyxa values. A projected ordinary instance
remains arena-managed; ANSI C automatic/static duration never defines its
source lifetime. Static backing is conforming only when it is an internal
implementation of a module-arena contract.

For example, a descriptor explicitly bound as `LmTransSymbol` may expose the
logical fields `name`, `kind` and `depth`. A hosted backend may project it as:

---ANSI C---
typedef struct LmTransSymbol LmTransSymbol;

struct LmTransSymbol {
    LmP0Text *name;
    LmTransSymbolKind kind;
    unsigned depth;
};
---ANSI C---

This C declaration is a projection, not the identity or definition of the
source descriptor. A Table may be passed to an explicit descriptor-conversion
operation as authoring data, but the table name, keys and cells declare nothing
by themselves.

The current named-Structure fallback is confined to descriptor-driven C
type/layout projection and creates no source value or backing storage. Remaining
top-level untracked static backing in other bootstrap paths is not normative
language semantics and must be removed or confined behind an explicit backend
representation of the module/root-arena contract.

20.2.1.H1. Historical Source Descriptors and Target Projection

The source-semantic input is an explicit descriptor bound in the namespace.
That descriptor is an ordinary Structure. A Table may store descriptor-like
data, but table names and rows do not implicitly introduce descriptors, fields
or namespace bindings.

Type checking and `implements(varA varB Consumer)` read namespace-visible descriptors and
the consumer tree. Backend
layout projection consumes an already resolved descriptor and may choose C
structs, pointers, handles, universal records or accessors without changing
source semantics.

A projected non-primitive Structure field contains a StructureReference or
ArrayReference, never an embedded Lingvamyxa aggregate value. An ArrayReference
may project to a header plus backing allocation, but both remain governed by
their arena contract.

`LmSlice` and similar C records are backend ABI helpers, not mandatory
source-semantic registry classes. Likewise, C `const`, `struct`, `enum`,
`union`, pointer depth, alignment and forward declarations are target concepts.
An explicit backend profile may store projection choices in ordinary Tables,
but those Tables do not define the source namespace or Value ontology.

Thus source description and backend projection may coexist in one bootstrap
translator while remaining one-way dependencies:

    namespace descriptor -> resolved Structure/Array semantics
                         -> selected backend representation

The backend representation never creates the source descriptor retroactively.

A backend may maintain implementation-private projection state to ensure that a
target declaration is emitted once. The hosted C bootstrap scopes this state to
one output translation and shares it across imported declarations, prelude,
prototypes, bodies and bounded lexical-scope replays. It is reset before another
output is translated. This state is not a namespace binding, descriptor,
Registry or Table, and source code cannot observe or modify it. In particular,
a Table or relation named `typedef.emitted` has no privileged effect on backend
output.

Projection-state identity is the identity of the target artifact, not evidence
that a source descriptor exists. For a hosted C typedef managed by this shared
projection state, the collision key is its target identifier namespace, C
lexical scope and canonical emitted C identifier. Each declaration at that key
also carries a normalized complete target-declaration fingerprint and a
structural target-presence path. The fingerprint includes every token that
changes the projected C type, including struct/union/function-pointer category,
ordered fields, effective pointer depth, constness, array shape, return type and
parameters. The current bootstrap uses the exact unguarded declaration bytes
emitted at canonical indentation as this fingerprint, so comparison cannot
drift from generated C.

A prior declaration may suppress a later emission only when their fingerprints
are equal, the prior presence path dominates the later path and the prior
declaration is not later in final target-output order. The hosted C bootstrap
emits the prelude before the body, irrespective of the order in which translator
operations happened to request those outputs. Thus a body claim made first
cannot suppress a later prelude claim. Equal declarations under independent
guards therefore remain separately guarded emissions. A declaration emitted in
one conditional branch is not recorded as unconditional.

Different fingerprints at one collision key are an error whenever their
presence paths may overlap. They may coexist only in branches that the emitted
target structure proves mutually exclusive, such as siblings of the same
`#if`/`#elif`/`#else` group. Independent guards are not assumed exclusive.
Leaving a branch removes it from the active dominance path but does not erase
its declaration from per-output conflict validation. A later unconditional
declaration must consequently be compatible with every earlier conditional
alternative that it can overlap.

True block-local typedef fallback remains scoped to its C block and does not
publish translation-unit projection state or a translation-unit target guard.
It may be skipped only when an identical already-projected declaration dominates
that block. The projection state and generated target guard must not introduce a
source name, establish source compatibility, select a layout, or make a
first-declaration-wins choice.

For C tag-backed projections, a `c.struct` or `c.named-struct` layout is
compatible only with a `c.struct` forward declaration, and a `c.union` or
`c.named-union` layout only with a `c.union` forward declaration. Mixing the tag
families at one projected name is a projection error.

The common projection state covers named structures, physical `merge:`,
non-forward `c.struct`/`c.union` layout typedefs, alias/forward declarations,
function-pointer typedefs and implementation-private generated ABI typedefs.
The latter include callable closure layouts and aliases, capture and binder
environments, `fm` return layouts, Table row layouts and fixed
Message-Thread/runtime prelude types. Their generated names and ordering are
backend constraints and do not define language semantics. Translation-unit and
block-local claims use the same state; local claims additionally carry the
identity of their C lexical scope.

The current hosted bootstrap rejects creation of a lazy callable binder while
emitting a structural conditional branch. Its helper functions cannot yet be
hoisted safely when the source callable or prototype exists only in that
branch. This is an explicit backend limitation, not a source-language
restriction or a license to emit a branch-local typedef outside the shared
collision domain.

True block-local named/merged Structure and Table-row fallback typedefs are
claimed in that common state before emission. Incompatible declarations in one
lexical scope are rejected; an inner scope may explicitly shadow an outer
claim, and sibling scopes do not collide. An identical dominating outer claim
may suppress redundant local output. Local scope claims neither publish
translation-unit guards nor create namespace entries.

20.2.2. L1 Substrate Escape and Level Receivers

`L2` and `L3` are level receivers. `L1` is instead an external-substrate escape
receiver. All three consume one body Structure; since a Structure contains an
ordered field sequence, this is enough to consume any number of source fields.
The bootstrap records this distinction explicitly: `L1` is in
`receiver.substrate`, while only `L2` and `L3` are in `receiver.level`. Thus the
ability to embed a necessary substrate fragment does not make L1 a third
Lingvamyxa source-semantic level.

The `L1` receiver walks its body fields in order. If a field is a string/payload
atom, `L1` writes that payload at the current generated L1 position. This rule is
not tied to one spelling: ANSI-style strings, python-like quoted raw strings and
block strings are all string payload forms. If a field is a Lingvamyxa Structure
or Frame, `L1` dispatches it back to the translator. Thus an `L2` frame can be
nested inside `L1`, and an `L1` implementation fragment can be nested inside
`L2`.

A hosted ANSI C bootstrap profile may provide small L1-output receivers at the
root level and inside `L1`. These receivers are translator conveniences, not
new high-level descriptor/value categories.

Example:

include: "<stdio.h>" "<windows.h>"

This writes:

---ANSI C---
#include <stdio.h>
#include <windows.h>
---ANSI C---

An include string whose payload is already enclosed in `<...>` or `"..."` is
copied as the C include target. Otherwise the payload is treated as a local
quoted include target.

An `os` receiver may select L1 output branches by target platform:

os:
    win:
        include: "<direct.h>" "<windows.h>"
    unix:
        include: "<unistd.h>"
    default:
        "/* fallback L1 text */"
end: os

The bootstrap profile may lower these branch heads to target preprocessor
conditions such as `_WIN32`, `__APPLE__` or `__unix__`. The branch
body is still ordinary L1-output content: string payload, `include`, nested `os`
or another translator receiver admitted by the profile.

In an L2 translator context, `os` also controls which source declarations and
method bodies are compiled for the selected target. The compiler may add their
ShortNameIds, direct links and method-pool records to its compile-time work
table before lowering later L2 bodies; it does not create a runtime namespace.
Platform branches are treated as alternative implementations of a structurally
admitted interface: the same function or Structure name may appear in several
`win`/`mac`/`unix`/`default` branches. Each
same-name occurrence may be validated by the explicit directional
`implements(new old Consumer)` operation before its graph construction/link is
admitted. Branch declarations are considered in source order. The selected
branch emits the actual callable/Structure provider occurrences; ordinary
unqualified structural lookup still selects `[0]`, never the newest occurrence:

os:
    win:
        fn: platformValue () int
            return: 1
        end: platformValue
    default:
        fn: platformValue () int
            return: 2
        end: platformValue
end: os

external:
    fn: main () int
        return: platformValue() = 0
    end: main
end: external

The explicit `c.platformValue()` spelling is still available as a low-level
C-surface escape, but it is not required for names imported by L2 `os`.

The `L2` receiver also consumes one body Structure, but it does not print
standalone strings as ANSI C. The rule is relative to the Structure being
translated to ANSI C: if a string field appears at level 0 of the body that `L2`
is asked to translate, and no receiver consumes that field, it is a compile-time
error. Nested Structures owned by another receiver are not reclassified by this
generic rule; that receiver defines what its own body fields mean. If a receiver
asks `L2` to translate such a nested Structure, the same level-0 rule applies
again inside that translated body. This is a phase error of the active consumer:
for `L2` it is reported by the compiler/translator, while an interpreter profile
would report the corresponding error in its own execution phase. The `c.name`
form is likewise consumed by the `c` receiver/projection: it prints an ANSI C
ordinary identifier only at the position where that explicit receiver is used.

Example:

L2:
    L1:'''typedef enum LmTransSymbolKind {
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE = 1,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_FUNCTION = 2,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_PROCEDURE = 3,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_LABEL = 4,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_STRUCTURE = 5
    } LmTransSymbolKind;'''

    fn: main () int
        return: c.LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE
    end: main
end: L2

The enum constants do not become Lmx occurrences or source-visible structural
names. They remain ordinary ANSI C names and are referenced explicitly through
the permanent C surface escape, for example
`c.LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE`.

When the bootstrap translator reads explicit configuration Tables such as
`lm2/trans_registry.lm2`, keys follow the ordinary identifier rule. A valid
ordinary identifier is written bare:

    u32
    prefix
    sqrt_ansi_c

If a key contains operator characters, dots, arrows, whitespace,
punctuation, active structural words in that table position, or any spelling
that must round-trip exactly, it is written as an exact identifier:

    `return`
    `+`
    `!=`
    `c.*`
    `operator.infix`
    `translator class-->spelling`

The backticks are not part of the key. They are the exact-identifier delimiter,
and the resulting atom is an exact identifier, not a string literal.

The same canonicalization rule applies here as in ordinary source: if exact
identifier content is a valid bare identifier, the bare and exact forms name
the same key. This rule is purely lexical and does not make the key a class or
namespace binding.

The current bootstrap first reads configuration from `lm2/core.lm2` and then
from `lm2/trans_registry.lm2`. It uses an explicit loader to convert selected
rows into compiler-private receiver/operator/backend work entries required to
start the translator. That conversion is an implementation mechanism. Loading
the Tables does not create or mutate semantic runtime state, and arbitrary
application Tables are never scanned this way.

At the earliest bootstrap point, the restricted loader recognizes root
`table:` and ordered `join:` Frames because the ordinary receiver bindings do
not yet exist. Only `.lm2` profile candidates are currently accepted. This is a
bootstrap limitation, not a second table model and not source semantics.

For every ordinary `table:` form, the first column is simply the row key by the
selected Table profile. A column named `class`, a Table named `class`, or a key
spelled like source description metadata has no special structural effect. The first
body atom `source`, when present, requests a translation-time/module projection
without a public C table symbol; it does not select a RegistryView. L2 and L3
both produce ordinary Lmx Table values in the owning Message arena. A backend
may optimize their physical representation without changing
that rule.

Inside `columns:`, a bare atom is a column name. An anonymous Structure uses
its first atom as the column name and the remaining atoms as explicit metadata,
for example `(l2 binding)` names column `l2` and records metadata `binding`.
`columns` itself does not publish runtime names; it is consumed by `table`.

Rows are dense. The number of row cells must be divisible by the number of
columns. The loader does not use physical source lines to infer missing cells.
The selected Table profile defines empty-cell representation. Expression-level
`None`, if available, is a separately constructed explicit Lmx value and is not
defined by empty cells. A pretty-printer may align columns; alignment is not
semantic.

A bootstrap implementation may lower Tables to a flat `row: table key payload`
internal representation. That is a temporary backend shape, not a different
semantic model.

L2/L3 may also construct a Table through the `join` receiver in section 19.17:

    join: (BaseTable OverlayTable) TargetTable
        columns: class  (value char)
        rows:
                 local  value
    end: TargetTable

This is not an import directive or parser syntax. It explicitly resolves the
source Table operands, constructs a result Table under the selected runtime
lifetime policy and publishes its Lmx occurrence only when requested by the
form.

Every L2 `fn` and `sub`, including a nested definition, lowers to a uniquely
identified C method entry reachable from any callable Lmx occurrence that may
refer to it in the linked/loaded program. Concrete C linkage may be external or
translation-unit-local behind the generated method pool; reachability and
program-wide identity are mandatory. The `external` receiver wraps exactly one
`fn` or `sub` and additionally requests a directly exported raw ANSI C symbol
for foreign linkage:

L2:
    external:
        fn: main () int
            return: 0
        end: main
    end: external
end: L2

Declaration qualifiers are ordinary receiver composition in the C-profile
projection. For example, `const: @: LmP0Node p` lowers to `const LmP0Node *p`.
The same qualifier form may appear in structure fields, parameters and local
declarations when the active L2 lowering profile supports the corresponding C
declarator role.

Address-producing and address-consuming forms:

    @count              address of the primitive storage location `count`
    p: @count           store that address into an address-depth-1 slot
    pp: @p              store the address of `p` into an address-depth-2 slot
    \p                  raw load through the explicit typed address `p`
    leafs[3]            checked ArrayReference access at index `3`
    \(rawBase + 3)      raw load after address arithmetic when `rawBase` is an
                        explicit typed address from an L2 machine-storage operation


20.3. Primitive Expression Operators

The minimal L2 primitive operator profile assigns explicit operator roles:

    grouping        (expr)
    prefix          @expr, \expr, ++expr, --expr, +expr, -expr, !expr, ~expr
    postfix         expr++, expr--
    infix           +, -, *, /, %, =, !=, <, <=, >, >=, &&, ||, &, |, ^

Source style: prefix operator spellings such as `@`, `\`, `++`, `--`, `!` and
`~` should be written adjacent to their operand when the operand is on the same
physical line, for example `@slot`, `\item`, `++index` and `!ready`. This is a
style convention, not a P0 tokenization requirement; P0 still tokenizes
operator spellings as separate operator-token fields where appropriate.

Indexing is a postfix expression operator with the bracket form `target[index]`,
specified with storage and address forms above. For an ArrayReference it is
checked Array indexing; for an explicit typed-address Primitive it is the
separate L2 raw-address operation. The `index` part is consumed as its own
expression range by the active L2 expression profile. Nested index operators,
calls, bounded Structures, strings and ordinary operator tokens inside the
brackets are therefore lowered by the same expression machinery as ordinary
argument expressions. Field-follow remains the L2 `\field` form, not ANSI C
`.` or `->` source syntax.

The bracket in `target\[occurrence]field` is not this postfix operator: its
position immediately after the field-follow backslash makes it the
occurrence qualifier for the following exact field name. Therefore
`target\field[index]` and `target\[index]field` are distinct forms.

This bracket form is separate from the `[]:` receiver spelling. `[]:` is a head
symbol used by Array constructors. `target[...]` is a
postfix index/access suffix. A bare `[a b]` is not a portable L2 grouping form;
use `(a b)` for a bounded Structure unless a profile explicitly admits standalone
square-bracket values.

The token `=` is comparison/equality for expression/operator consumers. It is not native assignment.

The C-profile lowering maps the listed primitive operators to ANSI C operator
spellings, except that Lingvamyxa `=` lowers to ANSI C `==`, `@expr` lowers to
address-of and `\expr` lowers to raw load/dereference when it is not a
field-follow token. Shifts, overflow rules, integer division rules,
floating-point rules and conversion rules remain L2 profile details unless a
later specification selects a mandatory set.

20.4. Update and Callable Consumption

L2 uses the same head/update surface form as the rest of the structural core:

    target: value

Examples:

    count: 6
    tree\branch: branch
    leafs[3]: 10

After resolution, the target is an actual Lmx occurrence, an explicitly selected
child position, or an Array/index location in the selected Array profile. The
operation updates or appends nodes in that graph; it does not append a binding
to a runtime namespace. A plain short-name update begins at the current
containing Structure. If it selects an existing child, the profile defines
whether the child payload is replaced or a new same-name occurrence is appended;
in either case unqualified later lookup remains `[0]`, the first matching child.
An addressable field/index form updates the selected graph/storage location. A
function/subroutine/receiver form resolves a callable Lmx node and invokes its
entry with `callable->node`.

Any `implements(new old Consumer)` validation is an explicit compile-time or
runtime graph operation over supplied Lmx values. It is not a descriptor gate
that creates visibility. The reverse name index is updated for every created,
moved or removed occurrence. A backend may reuse physical storage only when it
proves that node identity, graph references, occurrence order and arbitrary
textual lookup in a mutated tree remain observationally identical.

The update operator tokens `++` and `--` are part of the minimal L2 C-profile as
both prefix and postfix expression operators. They are expression operators, not
a second assignment syntax. Other profiles may lower them differently, but they
still compose with the same operator-token consumption model.

20.5. Minimal L2 Control Receivers

The minimal L2 executable-body control set is:

    if / else
    while
    synchronized
    until
    for(init, condition, step)
    return
    break
    continue

These are source-level control receivers. They are not raw jumps in the source
semantics, even if a translator lowers them to labels and branches. In
particular, `else` is resolved to an explicit receiver/callable occurrence by
the compiler and runtime Structure rules; it is not parser syntax and does not
require a runtime namespace.

The first ANSI C bootstrap translator needs only:

    if / else
    while
    synchronized
    return
    break
    continue

`until` and `for` remain part of the minimal L2 semantic set, but may be added
after the first procedural bootstrap subset.

20.5.1. `if` and `else`

L2 `if` consumes:

    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `if` receiver

L2 `else` is a separate statement receiver. It consumes:

    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `else` receiver

`else` is valid as the false branch only when it immediately follows an `if`
statement at the same statement level.

Example:

if: count > 0
    print: count


With a following sibling `else`:

if: count > 0
    print: count

else:
    print: 0


20.5.2. `while`

L2 `while` is a pre-condition loop.

Semantic lowering shape:

    while(condition, statementStructure)

Example:

while: i < n
    print: i
    i: (i + 1)


20.5.3. `until`

L2 `until` is a conditional close for the current executable block.

Example:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

In this example, `record` is an explicit compact-call argument of `endOfInput`. The `until:` line consumes the whole tail `endOfInput(record)` as a boolean-producing condition Structure. The condition producer is the compact call `endOfInput(record)`, not the invalid space-separated spelling `endOfInput record`.

More explicit condition form:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

Conceptual lowering shape:

    until(currentStatementStructure, condition)

The body executes first. The loop repeats until the condition is true.

If the condition is false, control transfers to the beginning of the executable block closed by `until`. If the condition is true, the block completes and execution continues after the `until` line.

20.5.4. `for(init, condition, step)`

L2 `for` consumes:

    init      -> initialization Structure
    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    step      -> iteration-step statement Structure
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `for` receiver
