Vertical body form:

for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++
    print: i


Compact body form:

    for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++ print(i)

20.5.5. `return`

L2 `return` exits the current executable body.

Bare runtime form inside a body:

    return

Value runtime form inside a body:

    return: value

For an `fn`, `return: value` consumes an Lmx value admitted by the explicitly
requested `implements(value declaredReturn returnConsumers)` operation and
exits. The exact C return/status channel and graph location of the returned Lmx
value remain unresolved under sections 8.8 and 19.29.5; no hidden output
parameter or alternative Value category is implied. A bare `return` is not a
valid `fn` return in the core profile.

For a `sub`, `return` exits without producing a normal return value. `return: value` is invalid for `sub` unless a later profile defines status/result subroutines.

At a valid terminal trailer position for an open executable definition, an admitted return form may be terminal return. Terminal return appends the corresponding runtime return as the final body item and closes the executable definition, in the same checked close position where the executable's admitted `end:` target would otherwise be expected. For an `fn`, that target is the function name exposed by the `fn` receiver, such as `end: square`, and the terminal return form is `return: value`. The common compact position is the executable definition's own source level; a smaller source level may also be valid when the ordinary source-level cut reaches the visible executable tail and intervening frames admit that closure.

Example:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

20.5.6. `break` and `continue`

`break` exits the nearest active L2 loop frame.

`continue` starts the next iteration of the nearest active L2 loop frame.

For `while`, `continue` transfers control to the next condition check.

For `until`, `continue` transfers control to the post-condition check for the current iteration.

For `for`, `continue` transfers control to the iteration-step Structure, then to the next condition check.

20.5.7. `synchronized`

`synchronized` consumes an object/address expression and a statement Structure
owned by the `synchronized` receiver. It is a structured synchronization
receiver, not a raw lock/unlock pair in source syntax.

In the minimal L2 profile, `synchronized` has an object-expression plus
statement-tail receiver signature. The source after `synchronized:` is still the
ordinary P0 argument Structure.
Inline fields remain ordinary fields of that Structure. If an inline object
expression is followed by a child source level, that child part is materialized
as a nested Structure field; therefore:

    synchronized: @lock
        break

is structurally equivalent to:

    synchronized: @lock (break)

while:

    synchronized: @lock break

keeps `break` in the inline object-expression field sequence. A receiver may
then reject that expression semantically, but P0 must not silently reinterpret
the same-line word as a body. If the consumed body tail is a single Structure,
that Structure is consumed as its fields, as in the general
single-Structure-as-N-arguments rule.

Example:

synchronized: @object
    updateObject: object

The first ANSI C bootstrap projection may lower this to runtime hooks:

---ANSI C---
Lmx *lock_handle = lm_resolve_lock_handle(containing_structure, /* object */);
lm_synchronized_enter(lock_handle);
{
    Lmx *callable = lm_resolve_callable(containing_structure, /* updateObject */);
    lm_invoke(callable); /* obtains entry and calls entry(callable->node) */
}
lm_synchronized_leave(lock_handle);
---ANSI C---

The runtime hook must implement the selected monitor policy, including
Java-style reentrant entry for the same thread when that profile is selected.

The first ANSI C bootstrap translator registers each active `synchronized`
monitor as a cleanup entry. Ordinary `return`, `break` and `continue` transfers
emit the required `lm_synchronized_leave(...)` calls before leaving the protected
region. A value-return inside `synchronized` may use implementation-only Lmx
reference/status temporaries selected by the still-open generic result ABI;
after the value/result roots are made safe for that ABI, active monitors are
released and control follows the selected return channel. This rule neither
defines a native per-source return type nor creates separate Primitive,
Structure and Array return categories.

Diagnostic failure across a synchronized boundary belongs to the wider
runtime/translator cleanup rules.

20.6. Outside the Minimal L2 Set

The following section 19 operators are not part of the minimal L2 control set
unless an L2 profile explicitly enables or redefines them:

    match / default-pattern receivers
    each
    finally
    catch / throw / throws
    retry / retryable
    assert
    redo
    yield
    labelled break / labelled continue

They may still exist in L3 or a richer L2 profile.

This subsection describes the first/minimal L2 bootstrap control subset, not a
level-order rule where L3 is wider than L2. L2 remains the wider low-level level
because it also owns the machine forms in sections 20.1 through 20.4; L3 never
exposes those forms.

21. Runtime Structure Resolution, Source Locals and Updates

Revision 2 has no semantic runtime namespace. There is no Name-to-Binding
dictionary, active overlay, captured lexical environment, call-time merge of
visible names or return-time restoration. This prohibition applies even if the
implementation calls such a structure a context, frame, descriptor map or
cache.

21.1. The Only Runtime Name Index

The only mandatory per-node auxiliary mapping is:

    Lmx * -> ShortNameId

It is a reverse index from a concrete node address to one short path component.
It does not answer a name with a value, store full paths, select a type, record
lexical depth or track active calls. Creating, copying, moving and destroying a
named occurrence must update the index consistently with node lifetime.

A full current path for a known node is computed by reading each short name and
following `node` toward the root. An arbitrary textual absolute path is resolved
only when an explicit root is supplied: enumerate current children at each
segment, compare their ShortNameId values and select the requested occurrence.
An already resolved Lmx pointer does not repeat this lookup.

21.2. Relative Resolution and `node`

A generated method receives the Structure that contains the selected callable
occurrence. A relative path in that method starts from this supplied Structure
or another explicitly resolved Lmx pointer. The source spelling `node` follows
the actual parent pointer. Therefore `node\field` means:

    parent = current->node
    result = child_named(parent, ShortNameId(field), occurrence 0)

It never means "search an outer lexical scope". If a graph reference points to
a Structure outside the containment subtree, traversal starts from the
explicitly referenced target. The target's own `node` remains its containment
parent; a reference does not create alias parents.

21.3. Source Arguments and Locals Are Structure Nodes

Formal arguments, actual argument values, named body fields and values often
called local variables are materialized as Lmx nodes under the call/body
Structure selected by the final call-context ABI. Recursion and concurrent
calls receive distinct local occurrences. Updating a local changes that
Structure occurrence or its graph link.

The baseline translator must not replace a source local with an automatic C
scalar, a typed C struct member, a closure environment member or a runtime
namespace entry. C locals remain permitted as service temporaries for loops,
allocation, bounds checks and error propagation. A later optimizer may
scalarize an Lmx access only after proving observational equivalence for node
identity, mutation, arbitrary path lookup, runtime merge, recursion and
concurrency.

21.4. Callable Resolution and Invocation

A method name is represented by a callable Lmx occurrence. Its payload belongs
to the common callable physical class and resolves to a program-wide reachable,
uniquely identified C method entry. Nested methods are emitted recursively as
real translation-unit-level C bodies; named Structures do not turn them into
descriptor fields or suppress their bodies.

Invocation is structurally exact:

    callable_entry(callable->node)

The entry receives no second self, namespace, environment, descriptor,
MessageThread or separate source-argument parameters. Everything source-visible
is reached as an Lmx node. The exact return/error channel is unresolved, but it
must remain behind this one-containing-Structure-pointer boundary.

After runtime merge, the copied callable occurrence has a rewritten `node` and
the same callable payload. Calling it therefore passes the new containing
Structure to the existing machine code. This is the required dynamic behaviour;
it is not object dispatch or C inheritance.

21.5. Repeated Names and Structural Updates

Repeated names are ordered child occurrences, not stacked bindings. For a
given ShortNameId:

    field == [0]field
    [1]field is the next matching child in forward result order

A later declaration or merge input does not silently become the unqualified
default. An operation may append, replace, remove or move occurrences only when
its receiver explicitly defines that structural mutation. Scope exit does not
reveal a hidden previous binding; it merely changes reachability/lifetime of
the call/body tree under the final memory policy.

`implements(New Old Consumer)` may validate an explicitly requested structural
replacement using the paths actually used by Consumer. It does not itself
publish an occurrence, mutate the graph, call a converter or create a namespace.

21.6. Compiler Work Tables Are Not Runtime Semantics

The parser and translator may use symbol tables, source-position maps,
ShortNameId interning, method-identity maps and fixup lists while producing C.
These tables may resolve a constant source spelling to a direct Structure
recipe, ShortNameId, provider reference or method entry. They disappear as
compiler work state and must not be confused with a runtime namespace.

Across modules, a Structure dependency resolves to an Lmx pointer, explicit
root-relative lookup or provider returning an Lmx pointer. A method dependency
resolves through the common callable physical class. The exact module-linking
ABI remains open; a global Name-to-value dictionary is not an acceptable
answer.

21.7. Baseline and Optimization Boundary

The first conforming translator uses generic runtime Lmx construction,
traversal, invocation and merge. It deliberately retains source locals as
Structure nodes. Optimizations such as direct links, cached ShortNameId
comparisons, inlining or scalar replacement are later work and must be guarded
by proofs that runtime tree mutation and merge cannot change observable
behaviour.

The current hosted implementation's lexical namespaces, descriptor gates,
closure structs, per-name typedefs, top-level-only executable traversal and
direct C locals are implementation debt. They are not intermediate normative
stages of Revision 2.

21.H. Revision-1 Lexical Namespace Model (Historical)

Everything below this heading is retained solely to explain the old hosted
translator and existing tests. It is non-normative and must not be copied into
the replacement semantic/lowering core.

21.H1. Historical Namespace as Ordered Name-to-Binding State

At every source point, the lexical namespace is conceptually:

    Namespace = Name -> ordered Binding occurrences

A Binding records the visible value/reference or translator-known descriptor
role required to resolve that name. Bindings include variables, functions,
subroutines, receivers, callable/structural descriptors, labels, explicit Table
values and ordinary values.

Conceptual example:

namespace:
    fn: IntToInt (int) int
    factorial: IntToInt
    print: Sub(...)
    fn: AddX (int) int
    String: TypeDescriptor
    int: TypeDescriptor
    ---

The example is notation for name-resolution state. A translator may represent
it with an arena-resident Structure/Table, lexical maps, symbol indexes,
persistent overlays, direct references or any equivalent mechanism. Repeated
exact names and their order are source-semantic; ordinary lookup selects the
newest still-active occurrence.

The rule is shared lexical lookup, not global lookup. A view may be local to an
executable definition, block, receiver context, module, service, explicit
namespace import or runtime profile. Names in the visible chain share lookup and
the same `implements(new old Consumer)` admission rule, but the language does not define
one global dictionary for all programs and services.

Inside one active lexical namespace view, Lingvamyxa does not use separate
function, variable and label namespaces. A name introduced as a variable,
function, subroutine, label, local executable, callable descriptor, receiver,
structural/protocol descriptor or imported descriptor participates in the same
ordered occurrence sequence for that exact name. The newest still-active
occurrence is the one found by ordinary lookup. The value behind it may be used
in any way admitted by the consuming code; the namespace does not choose a
different binding according to whether the next form looks like a call, a
rebind or a control target.

21.H2. Historical Local Namespace View

Entering an executable definition creates a local namespace view.

The local view may be implemented as a persistent overlay, copy-on-write map,
statically resolved lexical environment or another representation preserving
the same behavior. No runtime namespace object is required.

A plain-name rebind inside an executable body applies `implements(new old Consumer)`
and appends the admitted occurrence to the local namespace view. It does not mutate
the preceding or outer occurrence. Updating an addressable field/index location
is instead storage mutation and does not append a namespace occurrence.

Local namespace occurrences do not mutate the outer namespace and do not change
the externally visible descriptor of the executable definition.

21.H3. Historical Compatibility of Local Redeclarations and Rebinds

Local redeclaration and plain-name rebinding use exactly the same rule as every
other namespace redeclaration:

    implements(NewType OldType Consumer)

Consumer is the remaining known source that still sees the name. Every path of
`OldType` that Consumer uses must be present on `NewType`. There is no additional
callable-signature, variance, mutability, effect, ownership or conversion
algorithm in the language core. If an application wants to validate any such
property, it represents that validation as ordinary data and code. A name such
as `validate` participates in `implements(varA varB Consumer)` only when Consumer uses that
path.

The old descriptor is captured before the pending local occurrence is visible.
On success the new occurrence becomes the result of ordinary lookup; on failure
the local namespace is unchanged.

21.H4. Historical Function/Subroutine Name Rebinding

Because function/subroutine names are ordinary namespace bindings, a same-name
local declaration or plain-name rebind uses the common structural rule. There
is no separate callable replacement rule.

Example:

fn: IntToInt (int) int

fn: compute (int(x)) (int)
    fn: `slow path` (int(v)) (int)
    return: v * v
    fn: `fast path` (int(v)) (int)
    return: v << 1
    IntToInt: strategy `slow path`
    if: x < 10
        strategy: `fast path`
return: strategy(x)

Here `strategy` is explicitly introduced as a local binding whose initial value
is reached as `slow path`. Rebinding it to the value reached as `fast path` is
valid only when `implements(fastPath slowPath remainingBody)` holds. The later
call `strategy(x)` is separately
validated by the resolved call consumer.

This operation does not replace `slow path`, `fast path`, `compute`, or any outer namespace binding. It only changes the local namespace view used inside the current executable body.

21.H5. Historical Directional Structural Redeclaration

Redeclaration is neither unrestricted replacement nor nominal type equality. It
is the one directional used-path check from section 7.3.1:
`implements(new old Consumer)` with Consumer the remaining known uses of the name.

Therefore:

    int: x 1
    x: "hello"

is invalid when the pending text value cannot be used as the old `x` in the
remaining known uses of that name.

And:

    factorial: "hello"

is invalid when remaining uses of `factorial` still require paths that the
pending text descriptor does not provide. The fact that one value is
callable is not checked by a second hidden compatibility system.

21.H6. Historical Interaction with Return and Function Names

The canonical return mechanism for an `fn` is `return: value`. When `return: value` is written in a valid terminal trailer position, it also closes the `fn` source frame. The current function name is an ordinary namespace binding, not a separate return syntax.

The same visible name may participate in ordinary function/subroutine lookup,
recursion and local namespace overlay operations. If a plain-name rebind appends
an admitted non-function value under that exact name, ordinary lookup selects
the new occurrence. Later recursive call consumption through that spelling then
fails unless the new value itself admits the requested call.

21.H7. Historical Translation and Optimization

The source semantics allow a local namespace copy or overlay, but implementations do not have to allocate an actual dictionary at runtime.

A translator may optimize local namespace operations by:

    avoiding a copy when no local updates occur
    using copy-on-write for a small number of updates
    representing the overlay as a persistent map
    replacing lookups by direct references when types and bindings are static
    inlining a newest callable occurrence when it is non-escaping
    lowering an admitted callable rebind to a local reference/descriptor update
    rejecting or de-optimizing cases that escape the local namespace view beyond the executable lifetime

The source rule is structural: local declarations and plain-name rebinds append
namespace occurrences only after `implements(new old Consumer)` succeeds when an older
exact-name occurrence is visible. No particular runtime namespace
representation is required.

The current hosted backend does not yet expose source access to an arbitrary
older occurrence or project every occurrence sequence as addressable target
data. Such optimization is conforming only while newest-occurrence lookup and
scope restoration remain observable as specified.

The hosted migration currently applies the descriptor gate when binding named
Structure and physical `merge:` descriptors. General plain-name runtime rebind
lowering and materialization of all binding occurrences remain implementation
work; legacy target-storage assignment is not the normative source model above.

The current low-level hosted bind API transfers unique ownership of a raw
descriptor object and therefore rejects passing the exact descriptor pointer
already owned by the preceding occurrence. That is an implementation memory-
ownership guard, not an additional source compatibility rule. A general rebind
of the same StructureReference remains admissible because
`implements(new old Consumer)` compares used name paths; its lowering must retain,
clone or reference-count the value instead of creating two raw owners.

Closure capture of nested `fn`/`sub`, including after the defining frame
exits, follows section 7.3.3: captured names live in the env Structure.
Rebinding an outer plain name that was not captured remains the ordinary
section 7.3.2 rule in the defining frame.

?
Escaping local namespace overlays and explicit global/external update syntax
are not specified in this document.
?
